  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   Report asserts: Client John Sullivan, age 69, married to Kim, age 65. Both in good health. Three grown-up children, all financially independent.

Marital Status Married. Spouse DOB 07/05/1960, Age 65, not financially dependent, living with client.     The Fact Find document contains personal details with blank fields for First Name and Last Name in the Personal Details table (P1), despite the Client Agreement (P3) referencing 'Sullivan'. The Middle Name field shows 'Alan'. The report identifies the client as 'John Sullivan' but the Fact Find does not confirm 'John' as the first name—the First Name field is blank. Age reconciliation: DOB 07/06/1956 per Fact Find and Standard Life illustration means age 69 as at the report's working date (early 2025) but age 70 as at the Fact Find completion date 19/09/2025. The report's stated age of 69 is consistent with the DOB when derived, applying the guard that age may differ by 1 year where the fact find predates the report. Spouse details reconcile: Kim, age 65, spouse, not financially dependent, living with client (P5). The critical discrepancy is the absence of any dependent children in the Fact Find Family and Dependants section. The report asserts 'three grown-up children, all of whom are financially independent' but the Fact Find contains only the spouse record and no child records. The report provides a quote from page 3 stating the assertion came from the suitability report itself, so this represents a statement in the report not corroborated by the Fact Find.

  [G1.2] Tax status stated in the report is consistent with the evidence

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts on page 20 that the client 'have not yet triggered the MPAA, and you do not have a lower annual allowance.' The report does not state a current income tax band, marital status for tax purposes, or other specific tax attributes. The extraction notes that incomeTaxBand, annualAllowance, and territorialProfile are absent from the canonical model.

    File:     The Fact Find (P17) records employment as HGV Driver with gross annual income of £0.00 and highest rate of income tax paid as 0%. This appears to be a data entry error or incomplete record, as the same document (P20) shows Net Basic Monthly Income of £1,200.00 and the report narrative (P22) states the client 'continue to work as an HGV driver earning around £300 per week net'. The policy information documents (P1-P12) contain no tax status details. The suitability report (P19) discusses Annual Allowance limits generically (£60,000 per annum 2024/2025) but does not apply a specific band to this client. No evidence document records whether the client has triggered MPAA, holds any lifetime allowance protection, or has a recorded tax band.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report asserts MPAA has not been triggered (page 20, P9), but the Fact Find (P17) records highest rate of income tax paid as 0% and gross annual income as £0.00, which contradicts the narrative descriptions of earned income. This creates ambiguity about whether tax position was properly assessed.

      - The report states the client does not have a lower annual allowance, but no evidence document records a formal check against the client's actual circumstances to establish this fact.

      - Fact Find (P17) gross annual income field shows £0.00, but P20 and P22 indicate the client earns £1,200 net monthly (£14,400 annually) plus £300 per week net as HGV driver (approximately £15,600 annually) plus £20 per week from Sea Cadets (approximately £1,040 annually). The Fact Find gross income field does not reconcile with narrative income figures in the report.

    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Client's recorded income tax band or tax code, Formal annual allowance assessment outcome, Client's territorial tax residency status, Verification of actual gross income to validate the £0.00 entry in Fact Find employment section, Evidence of MPAA check or lifetime allowance protection status check performed by adviser

    The report makes a specific assertion about MPAA trigger status and annual allowance on page 20 (P9), stating the client has not triggered MPAA and does not have a lower annual allowance. However, the comparison cannot be reliably performed because the Fact Find record (P17, P20) contains an apparent data quality issue: the gross annual income field shows £0.00 while narrative sections describe the client as earning approximately £300 per week net as an HGV driver plus other income. This inconsistency means the underlying tax assessment basis is unclear. The extraction's own report notes that the incomeTaxBand, annualAllowance, and territorialProfile fields are absent from the canonical model, indicating these were either not present in the report or not captured. The policy information documents provide no tax status corroboration. The suitability report discusses Annual Allowance generically but does not show evidence of a client-specific assessment. No document in the pack evidences that the adviser performed a formal check against MPAA rules or documented the client's tax band. The assertion about MPAA and annual allowance status is therefore unsupported by the evidence pack: the claim is stated in the report but the underlying tax position data needed to substantiate it is either missing or inconsistent. This is a guard-triggering gap: where tax treatment is asserted without a recorded tax position, the finding applies.

  [G1.3] Employment details are consistent with the evidence

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   The suitability report asserts three employment relationships: (1) Client employed as HGV driver through agency with intended retirement date 2027-10-14 (inferred from 'expect to retire within the next two years' as at 14/10/2025); (2) Client receives additional income of £20 per week from volunteer role with Sea Cadets; (3) A second client (Kim) intends to continue working until age 66, with intended retirement date 2026-10-14 (inferred from age 65 at 14/10/2025).

    File:     Fact Find (P5, P13) records: Client employed as HGV Driver by CDH Recruitment (not 'agency'), employment status Employed, with net basic monthly income £1,200.00 and state pension £230.00 monthly (£11,973 annually per P7). Suitability Report (P7, P15) states 'You receive £300 per week as an HGV driver' and 'You receive £20 per week helping out the sea cadets on a weekend'. Intelliflo baseline scenario (P3, P9) shows Employment income £16,640 (appears to be for one party) and separate employment income £13,200 for another party, totalling household employment income £29,840. Age data: P5 shows spouse date of birth 07/05/1960, age 65 at ID verification 08/12/2025. P14 states 'The Office of National Statistics Life Expectancy for a 65-year-old female is age 88' consistent with spouse being 65. No clear evidence of intended retirement age 66 for the spouse or retirement date 2026-10-14 is located in the passages.

    Discrepancies:

      - Employer name: Report infers 'agency' but Fact Find (P5, P13) states 'CDH Recruitment' specifically

      - Intended retirement date for HGV driver: Report infers 2027-10-14 based on 'expect to retire within the next two years' from 14/10/2025, but this would place retirement in 2027 rather than current income projections which show employment income continuing to 2027 in cashflow models

      - Intended retirement age 66 and date 2026-10-14 for spouse: Report asserts spouse intends to work until age 66 with retirement date 2026-10-14, but no evidence in passages specifies spouse's intended retirement age or date; spouse is stated as age 65 in P5 as at 08/12/2025

      - Employment income frequency and amount: Report uses £300 per week (£15,600 annually) plus £20 per week (£1,040 annually) in cashflow (P15), but Fact Find (P13) shows basic income £1,200 monthly (£14,400 annually) plus state pension £230 monthly; the £300 weekly figure appears to be net weekly amount per P15 and differs from stated gross basic annual income structure in Fact Find

    The primary employment details (HGV driver, Sea Cadets volunteer, £20 weekly) are corroborated across multiple passages. However, three material discrepancies exist: (1) The employer is named specifically as 'CDH Recruitment' in the Fact Find (P5, P13), not generically as 'agency' as the report infers — this is a factual mismatch on a named employer. (2) The intended retirement date for the HGV driver (2027-10-14) is inferred in the canonical model from a statement made on 14/10/2025 saying 'expect to retire within the next two years', which would place retirement in 2025-2027; the extraction inferred this as October 2027, but the Fact Find contains no explicit confirmation of this date. (3) Most significantly, the report asserts a second employment record for 'Kim' aged 66 intending to retire at age 66 in 2026-10-14, but the evidence shows spouse age 65 as at 08/12/2025 (P5), and no passage provides her intended retirement age or date. The cashflow models (P3, P9, P10) show dual income streams (£16,640 and £13,200) and project employment income continuing through the planning period, but do not explicitly confirm Kim's retirement intentions. The retirement date inference for the HGV driver depends on interpreting 'within the next two years' from a specific date; the guard notes that intended retirement age in a fact find is often superseded by later file notes — however, no later evidence contradicting the 2-year timeframe is present. The employer name mismatch (agency vs CDH Recruitment) is a clear discrepancy that must be recorded, though immaterial to affordability. The absence of explicit evidence for spouse's intended retirement age and date is a material gap where the report makes a specific assertion (age 66, date 2026-10-14) unsupported by any passage.

  [G1.4] Income and expenditure figures are consistent with the evidence

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   Report asserts: employment income £300/week net (C1 HGV driver); £20/week net (C1 Sea Cadets volunteer); £1,100/month net (C2 employment); State Pension £230/week net (C1 current); State Pension £230/week net (C2 future from 2026); household expenditure £1,700/month; monthly income total £4,486.67; monthly surplus £2,786.67; total assets £6,000; total liabilities £0.

    File:     Fact Find [P10] shows: employed income £1,200 gross basic monthly, £230/month State Pension; total monthly income £1,430. Fact Find [P1] shows: calculated total monthly household expenditure £1,718; total monthly disposable income -£288. Suitability Report [P17] confirms: £300/week typical employment income, £20/week Sea Cadets, £1,100/month Kim employment, £230/week State Pension current, £230/week State Pension future, £1,700/month household expenditure, £6,000 savings, no liabilities. Report [P9] states: £11,973 annual State Pension for C1 (approximately £230/week × 52), £11,973 annual State Pension for C2 from age 67, annual expenditure £1,700.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report derives monthly equivalent from £300/week as £1,300/month; Fact Find [P10] lists only £1,200/month gross basic employed income, creating a £100 discrepancy (8.3% difference) when comparing equivalents

      - Report states household expenditure £1,700/month; Fact Find [P1] states calculated total monthly household expenditure £1,718 (£18 or 1.1% difference)

      - Report monthly income total £4,486.67 (C1 HGV £1,300 + Sea Cadets £86.67 + State Pension £1,000 + C2 employment £1,100); this figure does not appear in evidence and sum cannot be verified against Fact Find [P10] which shows only £1,430 total monthly income

      - Report derives £230/week State Pension as £1,000/month; arithmetic check: £230 × 52 ÷ 12 = £999.33, rounds to £1,000, but Fact Find [P10] shows £230/month State Pension (not weekly), creating ambiguity about basis

    The report-first perspective requires that assertions in the suitability report be supported by evidence. The Fact Find [P1], [P10] and Suitability Report [P17], [P9] contain the primary income and expenditure data. Guard on weekly to monthly conversion: £300/week should annualise at 52 weeks, yielding £300 × 52 ÷ 12 = £1,300/month, not £1,200. The Fact Find [P10] shows £1,200 gross basic monthly employment income, but the report treats the £300/week as net, deriving £1,300 net monthly. The Fact Find lists gross, report lists net — this is a gross/net comparison issue covered by the false-positive guard, but the underlying employment income stated in both documents (£1,200 gross in Fact Find vs derived £1,300 net in report from £300/week) contains a material £100 discrepancy. The weekly to monthly conversion in the report is arithmetically correct (£300 × 52 ÷ 12 = £1,300), but the Fact Find gross figure of £1,200 does not reconcile to this. The State Pension figure of £230/week is stated consistently across Suitability Report [P17] and confirmed in [P9] as £11,973 p.a. (≈ £230/week). The report's derivation of £1,000/month (£230 × 52 ÷ 12 = £999.33 rounds to £1,000) is arithmetically sound. Household expenditure shows £1,700 in the report [P17] and £1,718 in Fact Find [P1], a 1.1% difference within rounding tolerance. The report's monthly income total of £4,486.67 is derived but not present in any single evidence document; it is calculated by the extraction system from component parts stated separately. The Fact Find [P10] shows £1,430 as total monthly income, which is substantially lower and appears to be incomplete (missing HGV £1,200 or derived £1,300, Sea Cadets £20, and C2 State Pension £230 future income not yet received). The report separates current income (£3,486.67 for C1 and C2 combined) from future income (C2 State Pension from 2026). No single reconciliation point exists in the evidence for the report's derived £4,486.67 total that excludes future C2 State Pension.

  [G1.6] State pension entitlement is consistent with the evidence

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   Client (JS) receives full State Pension of £230 per week, currently in payment. Client's spouse (Kim) will receive full State Pension of around £230 per week at State Pension age 67, payable from 2027-10-14. Report also states Kim receives state pension income of £11,973 per annum from age 67.

    File:     [P5] states 'You receive state pension income of £11,973 per annum' and 'Kim will receive state pension income of £11,973 per annum from age 67.' [P1] shows STATE PENSION AGE 66 for one party (KS - Kim). [P9] confirms 'You are in receipt of your full State Pension of £230 per week' and 'Kim will receive her full State Pension of around £230 per week when she reaches State Pension age.' [P5] indicates the model assumes retirement age 66 for Kim ('Retire' age 66 in 2026 appears in the baseline scenario).

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states Kim's State Pension age is 67, but [P1] shows STATE PENSION AGE 66 for KS (Kim). Report payable from date 2027-10-14 implies age 67; [P1] baseline scenario shows Kim retiring age 66 in 2026.

      - Report states £230 per week converts to £11,973 per annum (£230 × 52 = £11,960, not £11,973 - a difference of £13 or 0.1%). [P5] also states £11,973 per annum, which does not reconcile to £230 × 52.

      - State Pension age discrepancy: Report asserts 67; evidence in [P1] shows 66.

    The weekly amount of £230 is corroborated by [P9]. However, two material discrepancies arise. First, the report states Kim reaches State Pension age at 67 with a payable-from date of 2027-10-14, but [P1] explicitly states 'STATE PENSION AGE 66' for KS (Kim), and the baseline scenario in [P1] models Kim retiring age 66 in 2026, not 2027. This is a one-year gap. Second, the annual figure of £11,973 does not reconcile arithmetically to £230 × 52 = £11,960. The difference is £13 per annum or 0.1%, which falls within typical rounding tolerance. However, the State Pension age contradiction (67 vs 66) is not a rounding issue but a factual discrepancy about when benefits become payable. The guard acknowledges that State Pension age and retirement age are routinely conflated; however, [P1] is explicit about 'STATE PENSION AGE 66', not a retirement age. This represents a material factual difference that affects the cashflow model's assumptions. The report contradicts the evidence on when Kim's State Pension entitlement commences.

  [G1.8] Data mismatches and unsupported figures within the report itself

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The extraction identified six internal inconsistencies: (1) People's Pension value £6,601.55 is consistent but recommendation to 'Retain' contradicts objective OBJ3 to consolidate pensions; (2) Risk rating shows questionnaire result of 4 vs agreed/confirmed rating of 5 with unclear sequence; (3) Aviva existing charge 0.50% vs recommended 0.44% (0.21% + 0.22%), but text claims new plan 0.06% more expensive, contradicting the calculation; (4) Fund allocations show same funds (HSBC 50%, CT Universal Map 50%) but with slight naming variance between pages 2 and 12; (5) Employment income stated as 'typically £300 per week net' on page 3 but assumed as deterministic '£300 per week' in cashflow on page 14; (6) Individual existing arrangement values sum to £116,998.47 but stated total transfer value is £110,185, a discrepancy of £6,813.47.

    File:     This check compares the report against itself and does not rely on external evidence. The internal inconsistencies are drawn from the extraction report's own analysis of the canonical model extracted from the suitability report. No supporting document passages are searched or cited; the comparison is internal to the report's stated figures, narratives and calculations.

    Discrepancies:

      - Aviva charge contradiction: page 5 states 0.50% existing; page 11 recommendation table shows 0.21% platform + 0.22% investment management = 0.44% total; page 6 text states 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' which contradicts the arithmetic showing new charge is 0.06% cheaper (0.50% - 0.44% = 0.06% reduction)

      - Transfer value arithmetic: sum of five existing arrangements (£6,601.55 + £211.32 + £3,744.36 + £103,439.24 + £3,002.00) = £116,998.47 but page 9 states total transfer value as £110,185, creating unexplained shortfall of £6,813.47

      - Employment income qualifier: page 3 describes income as 'typically earning around £300 per week net' suggesting variability, but page 14 cashflow assumptions section lists 'You receive £300 per week as an HGV driver' without the qualifier 'typically', treating it as deterministic input

      - Risk rating sequence ambiguity: page 4 contains two statements—'We agreed that you are a risk rating 5 rather than a 4 as per the questionnaire' and later 'You confirmed that you wish to increase your Risk rating to 5'—creating unclear causal sequence between questionnaire result (4), adviser assessment/agreement, and client confirmation

      - Fund name consistency: page 2 recommendation summary lists 'CT Universal Map Balanced' and 'HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio' while page 12 lists 'CT Universal MAP' and 'HSBC' with allocations 50% each; the fuller names on page 2 appear to be the same funds but naming inconsistency exists across pages

      - Retention vs consolidation logic: recommendation states 'Retain your people's pension plan - £6,601.55' but objective OBJ3 directs consolidation of pensions; the retention of this one plan while consolidating/switching four others is not reconciled

    All six identified discrepancies are internal contradictions within the report itself and can be assessed without external evidence. Severity assessment: (1) The Aviva charge contradiction is material—page 6 asserts the new plan is 0.06% more expensive than recommended solution, but the fee table on page 11 and the arithmetic show the opposite (0.44% new vs 0.50% old = 0.06% cheaper). This is a high-severity self-contradiction on a figure that could drive the recommendation. (2) The transfer value shortfall of £6,813.47 is material and unexplained; it appears the report may have excluded one or more arrangements from the total without stating why, or there is an arithmetic error. This is high severity as transfer value is a driver of the recommendation. (3) The employment income qualifier ('typically' vs deterministic) is a moderate concern; the cashflow model treats £300/week as fixed but the narrative suggests it varies, which affects reliability of the financial projection. (4) The risk rating sequence is ambiguous but does not constitute a numerical contradiction—both statements conclude to risk rating 5, so the final position is clear even if the path to it is unclear; this is low-to-moderate concern affecting the documentation of consent. (5) Fund naming inconsistency (Universal Map vs MAP; full name vs abbreviation) appears to refer to the same funds and is a low-severity presentation issue. (6) The retention of People's Pension contradicts the stated consolidation objective, but the extraction notes this is a logical/strategic inconsistency rather than a data mismatch on the figure itself—still material and moderate severity. The core numerical contradictions are the Aviva charge statement (high severity) and the transfer value shortfall (high severity).

 

  [G1.10] Values expressed in different frequencies or formats reconcile (weekly/monthly/annual, gross/net, percentages and totals)

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   Report asserts five income streams with the following net values and frequencies: (1) £300 per week (HGV employment); (2) £20 per week (Sea Cadets volunteer); (3) £1,100 per month (spouse Kim employment); (4) £230 per week (client State Pension); (5) £230 per week (spouse State Pension future). Report derives monthly equivalents: £1,300, £86.67, £1,100, £1,000, £1,000 respectively. Report states total monthly net income as £4,486.67. Report states monthly expenditure as £1,700 net. Single expenditure category listed with monthly frequency.

    File:     Fact Find (Test 1) [P2] shows Total Net Monthly Income: £1,430.00 and Total Monthly Expenditure: £1,718.00. Fact Find [P2] details income: Basic Income (CDH Recruitment) Monthly £1,200.00 net; State Pension Monthly £230.00. Fact Find [P1] and [P2] show expenditure totalling £1,718.00 monthly. Fact Find [P4] shows emergency savings of £6,000.00. Retirement Planning document [P5] states client "needs a household income of £1,700 net per month" in retirement. File notes [P9] discuss consolidation and pension switching.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report asserts £300/week HGV income; Fact Find [P2] shows £1,200.00/month (equivalent to £276.92/week at 52-week annualisation), a 7.7% variance.

      - Report asserts £20/week Sea Cadets income; no corroborating figure found in fact find. Nearest is £25.00/month entertainment expenditure [P2], which is 25% away and is expenditure not income.

      - Report asserts £1,100/month spouse income; Fact Find [P2] shows £1,200.00/month basic income, a 9.1% variance.

      - Report asserts £230/week State Pension (client); Fact Find [P2] confirms £230.00/month, which at 52-week annualisation is £4.42/week, not £230/week. The report has scaled a monthly figure as if weekly.

      - Report asserts £230/week spouse State Pension (future); Fact Find [P2] confirms £230.00/month for spouse, not £230/week.

      - Report derives total monthly income of £4,486.67; recalculation from stated values (£1,300 + £86.67 + £1,100 + £1,000 + £1,000) yields £4,486.67, but reconciliation against fact find fails: £1,200 + £230 = £1,430 monthly (actual from [P2]), not £4,486.67.

      - Report states monthly expenditure £1,700; Fact Find [P1] and [P2] show Calculated Total Monthly Household Expenditure £1,718.00, a £18 variance within tolerance but presented without qualification.

    Critical frequency/basis mismatch identified. The report asserts State Pension income at £230 per week for both client and spouse, but Fact Find [P2] evidences £230.00 per month. At 52-week annualisation, £230/month = £4.42/week net, not £230/week. This is a 52-fold error in the frequency conversion, not a rounding variance. The report's derivation of £1,000/month equivalent for each State Pension (implying £230/week × 52 weeks ÷ 12 months = £1,000) confirms the report treated £230 as a weekly figure when the source is monthly. The HGV employment income shows 7.7% variance (£300/week report vs £276.92/week equivalent from £1,200/month in fact find), within the 1% tolerance when expressed consistently. However, the spouse employment income asserts £1,100/month against fact find evidence of £1,200/month, a 9.1% variance exceeding the 1% tolerance. The £20/week Sea Cadets income has no corroborating evidence in the fact find; the only adjacent figure is £25 monthly expenditure, not income. The total monthly income derived by the report (£4,486.67) cannot be reconciled to the fact find's stated Total Net Monthly Income (£1,430.00), a 214% discrepancy. The monthly expenditure asserted (£1,700) differs from fact find by £18 (£1,718 actual), within rounding tolerance. The report's income calculations appear to use the wrong frequency for State Pension (treating monthly as weekly) and to include income streams not clearly evidenced in the fact find (Sea Cadets £20/week) or with materially different values (spouse employment). These errors cascade through the model's cashflow and affordability analysis, affecting the recommendation's foundation. The guard permitting gross/net reconciliation is not engaged here because all figures are asserted as net; the guard permitting weekly annualisation at 52 weeks is engaged correctly for HGV income but misapplied to State Pension figures that originate as monthly amounts.

  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts that cashflow modelling was performed (wasCashflowModelled: true) with the following key facts and assumptions: General expenditure inflation 2%; Savings inflation 2%; State pensions inflation 2.5%; Aviva personal pensions inflation 5%; Mid growth rate for critical yield analysis 2.94%; Surplus income percentage saved 0%; Baseline cashflow to age 100 with no shortfall projected; High inflation stress test at 6% with no shortfall; Combined market crash (-30%) and high inflation (6%) with no shortfall; Monthly surplus of £2,786.67; Savings of £6,000; Retirement age 75; Annual expenditure of £1,700 per month (£20,400 annually); Income of £1,100 per month net; State pension income of £11,973 per annum; Transfer values for Aviva (£3,744), Zurich (£103,439), and Standard Life (£3,002).

    File:     The case file evidences (P1, P11, P12, P15) confirm: General expenditure 2%; Savings 2%; State pensions 2.5%; Aviva personal pensions 5%; Mid growth rate 2.94%; Surplus income percentage saved 0%; Cashflow runs to age 100 with no shortfall; High inflation stress test at 6% with no shortfall; Savings of £6,000; Monthly expenditure of £1,700; State pension £11,973 per annum; Employment income of £1,100 per month. P15 shows opening values: Aviva £3,744, Zurich £103,439, Standard Life £3,002. P7 shows Total Assets £122,997 comprising Property Equity, Liquid Assets £6,000, and Pensions £116,997. P1 confirms 'The cashflow runs until you reach age 100 and assumes all surplus income throughout your lifetime is spent.' However, the combined market crash scenario is described in P1 as showing '-30%' loss would result in 'no shortfall', and P12 confirms 'GENERAL 2%' and 'STATE PENSION 2.5%'.

    Discrepancies:

    The comparison can be performed because the suitability report was extracted into canonical form and the case file contains the supporting documents (Suitability Report Test 1.md, intelliflo planning documents, and pension switch report). The key inflation assumptions (2%, 2%, 2.5%, 5%) are corroborated by P11 and P12. The mid-growth rate of 2.94% is corroborated by P16 (pension switch report). The surplus income assumption of 0% is corroborated by P11 and P12. The personal facts (£6,000 savings, £1,100 monthly employment income, £11,973 state pension, £1,700 monthly expenditure, transfer values) are corroborated by P1, P15, and P7. The monthly surplus figure of £2,786.67 is marked as 'Derived' in the canonical model and represents a calculation rather than an assertion requiring direct evidence. The combined market crash scenario is described narratively in P1 as a '-30%' immediate loss combined with 6% inflation; the exact percentage is used in the report's assertion but appears in the evidence as narrative text rather than a discrete figure. No internal arithmetic contradictions are evident. The page number citations in the canonical model may reflect the PDF pagination of the suitability report, which is not fully disambiguated in the markdown evidence, but this is a presentation matter rather than a factual inconsistency. All material assumptions used in the cashflow modelling and scenarios are supported by evidence in categories F, G, or I.

CHK-002 — Objectives, Needs and Time-Horizon Alignment

Outcome: Potential Concern

 

  Potential Concern across 7 requirement(s). 6 raised a concern (G2.1, G2.2, G2.3, G2.5, G2.6, G2.7). Report asserts retirement 'in 2 years' (from 2025 baseline) but P8 intelliflo planning timeline shows retirement ages 70/66 in 2026 (Kim) and 71/67 in 2027 (John), which is consistent only if the advice was dated mid-2025 and both retire within two calendar years; however P3 Fact Find dated 19/09/2025 (September) makes retirement in 2025 impossible. The two-year target from September 2025 would mean retirement in September 2027, not 2026–2027. Report states current household net income £1,430 per month (P7 [B] Fact Find) but retirement objective requires £1,700 per month household income. Current expenditure stated as £1,718 per month (P7 [B]), creating a current shortfall of £288 per month. Retirement income target of £1,700 is only £18 below current expenditure, suggesting the objective presumes either expenditure will reduce or income sources will be secured in retirement (State Pension plus pension drawdown). No explicit clarification in evidence of how the £1,700 target will be met from pension funds plus State Pension contributions. Report asserts retirement target date 2027-10-14 (October 2027); evidence in [P10] shows retirement 2027 for JS (John) but 2026 for KS (Kim)—inconsistency in which client's date is being modelled or whether both are captured. 11 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

 

  [G2.1] Stated goals and their priority order

  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified

    Report:   Three objectives stated with priority rank 1–3: (1) Ensure continued ability to afford lifestyle through lifetime (isClientStated: true); (2) Retire within two years with household income of £1,700 net per month (isClientStated: true); (3) Consolidate and streamline pension arrangements (isClientStated: true).

    File:     P2 [I] Suitability Report confirms all three objectives verbatim: 'You want to ensure you can continue to afford your lifestyle through your lifetime', 'You want to retire in 2 years time with a household income of £1,700 net per month', 'You would like a clearer understanding of your pension arrangements and to ensure they are appropriately structured for your retirement. You also prefer a more streamlined approach to managing your pensions, rather than maintaining five separate plans with different providers.' P3 [B] Fact Find dated 19/09/2025 records 'Retirement Planning: Yes' and goals section states 'Just needs advice helping him organise his pensions in the best place. Wants to consolidate to help him get his head around what he has. Thinks he'll probably retire in the next 2 years and needs a household income of £1,700 net per month. ATR 5.' P5 [C] File notes confirm client's capacity for loss: 'You are in receipt of a full State Pension of approximately £230 per week, continue to work as an HGV driver earning around £300 per week net, and receive an additional £20 per week from your role with the Sea Cadets', and note household expenditure of £1,718 per month against net income of £1,430, with spouse Kim contributing £1,100 per month from her employment. P1 [B] Fact Find Declaration states 'I/We the undersigned confirm my/our financial planning objectives are those identified and prioritised in this document.' P8 [F] intelliflo planning proposed scenario shows timeline with retirement ages: 'Retire (Kim)' age 70 in 2026, 'Retire (John)' age 71 in 2027, contra the stated two-year retirement horizon from 2025. P12 [B] Fact Find confirms retirement income target: 'Regular Income (Today's Money) £1,700.00'.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report asserts retirement 'in 2 years' (from 2025 baseline) but P8 intelliflo planning timeline shows retirement ages 70/66 in 2026 (Kim) and 71/67 in 2027 (John), which is consistent only if the advice was dated mid-2025 and both retire within two calendar years; however P3 Fact Find dated 19/09/2025 (September) makes retirement in 2025 impossible. The two-year target from September 2025 would mean retirement in September 2027, not 2026–2027.

      - Report states current household net income £1,430 per month (P7 [B] Fact Find) but retirement objective requires £1,700 per month household income. Current expenditure stated as £1,718 per month (P7 [B]), creating a current shortfall of £288 per month. Retirement income target of £1,700 is only £18 below current expenditure, suggesting the objective presumes either expenditure will reduce or income sources will be secured in retirement (State Pension plus pension drawdown). No explicit clarification in evidence of how the £1,700 target will be met from pension funds plus State Pension contributions.

    The three objectives are clearly stated in the report and corroborated by the Fact Find (P3, P12), file notes (P5), and suitability report itself (P2). The client declaration (P1) confirms the objectives as identified and prioritised. Substance matches: consolidation, retirement within two years with £1,700 net household income, and ability to afford lifestyle through lifetime are all evidenced. However, there are two material discrepancies. First, the timeline: the advice is dated September 2025 (P3), and the statement 'retire in 2 years' would mean September 2027, yet the intelliflo planning document (P8) shows Kim retiring at age 70 in 2026 and John at age 71 in 2027. If John is currently 69 (born 07/06/1956 per P8), he would be 70 in 2026 and 71 in 2027, which aligns with the planning model but not strictly 'within two years' from September 2025. This is a minor discrepancy in precision rather than substance—the planning horizon of approximately 2 years is borne out by the model. Second, the retirement income target: the report asserts the goal is £1,700 net per month for the household. Current household net income is stated as £1,430 per month (P7), and current monthly expenditure is £1,718 (P7). The report does not explicitly address how a pension solution will bridge this to £1,700 or whether the £1,700 presumes a reduction in current expenditure or securitisation of additional income in retirement (such as confirmed State Pension). The evidence (P5) confirms State Pension of approximately £230 per week (~£997 per month for the couple), but it is not explicit in the evidence that this plus pension drawdown is modelled to achieve exactly £1,700 net household income. The proposed scenario (P8) shows pension values of £110,185 post-consolidation, and planning models show assets managed through 2027 retirement, but no explicit cashflow is quoted matching £1,700 to pension income, State Pension, and other sources. This is not an absence of supporting evidence—the planning is clearly done—but rather the £1,700 target is supported only at a high level, not with a detailed income reconciliation in the passages provided. The priority order (1, 2, 3) is not contested in the evidence. The objectives are substantively client-stated and corroborated by multiple categories (B, C, F, I).

  [G2.2] Retirement target date and investment time horizon details

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report asserts a retirement target date of 2027-10-14 (14 October 2027), a term in years of 2, and time horizons of LongTerm, MediumTerm, and MediumTerm across its objectives. It also asserts an intended retirement age of 66 for the client.

    File:     The evidence presents multiple retirement dates and time horizons across different documents. The intelliflo planning proposed scenario [P10] shows retirement age 66 in 2026 for one party (KS) and retirement age 71 in 2027 (JS). The risk profile report [P11] specifies an investment period of 2 years and investment of £106,000. The pension switch report [P1] references retirement age 75 as the default for Aviva plans. The Zurich policy [P15] shows selected retirement age 65 with planned retirement age 75. The Standard Life policy [P20] notes the member is already 69 and unable to provide illustrations to age 65. The intelliflo baseline scenario [P16] mirrors the proposed scenario with retirement ages 66 (2026) and 71 (2027).

    Discrepancies:

      - Report asserts retirement target date 2027-10-14 (October 2027); evidence in [P10] shows retirement 2027 for JS (John) but 2026 for KS (Kim)—inconsistency in which client's date is being modelled or whether both are captured.

      - Report asserts term in years of 2; evidence in [P11] corroborates 2 years investment period, but [P1] pension switch analysis uses retirement age 75 as default illustration age, creating ambiguity about which horizon drives the strategy.

      - Report asserts intended retirement age 66; evidence in [P10] and [P16] shows retirement age 66 in 2026 for one party and age 71 in 2027 for another party (JS/John). Report does not clarify which client the age 66 applies to.

      - Report time horizons listed as LongTerm, MediumTerm, MediumTerm (three entries) but no corresponding objective names or priorities provided in extracted model; evidence does not support the categorisation or let purpose of each horizon be traced to a specific goal.

      - Pension switch report [P1] uses retirement age 75 as the illustration default for Aviva and other plans, contradicting the 66/71 ages elsewhere in file.

      - Standard Life policy [P20] notes member already age 69, making age 66 retirement impossible; evidence shows this is inconsistent with report's assertion of age 66 as intended retirement age unless referring to different household member.

    The report asserts a single retirement target date of 2027-10-14 with a 2-year investment term and an intended retirement age of 66. The evidence file contains multiple retirement dates and ages across different scenarios and clients. The intelliflo planning documents [P10, P16] identify two household members: KS (born 07/05/1960, age 65, retiring age 66 in 2026) and JS (born 07/06/1956, age 69, retiring age 71 in 2027). The report's assertion of 2027-10-14 aligns with JS's retirement date in 2027, but the stated retirement age of 66 does not match JS's age 71 target—it matches KS's retirement profile. This suggests either (1) the report conflates two different clients' objectives, or (2) the extraction captured different objectives without linking them to specific parties. The 2-year investment term in [P11] corroborates the report's term figure, but the pension switch illustrations [P1] use age 75 as a default illustration age for existing plans, creating a separate horizon for illustration purposes unrelated to the client's stated retirement goal. The guards note that selected retirement age on a plan is a contractual default, not necessarily the client's intended date—the Zurich policy shows 65 selected but 75 planned; Aviva uses 75 as default. The evidence does not support a single coherent retirement objective: the 2027 date belongs to one client (JS) targeting age 71, while age 66 belongs to another (KS) retiring in 2026. The report's assertion of a single retirement target (2027-10-14) and single retirement age (66) cannot both be correct for a single client. The time horizons (LongTerm, MediumTerm, MediumTerm) are asserted without objective names or priorities, so their relationship to the 2027 date and 66 age cannot be verified. No evidence supports why three separate time horizons are needed or which objectives each serves.

  [G2.3] Access requirements — emergency, planned and ad-hoc

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts: (1) Emergency access to funds: 'Should you require access to these funds in the future it will usually take 10-15 working days for administration, disinvestment and banking processes to complete' (page 8), funded from pension via drawdown. (2) Emergency fund: client holds £6,000 in liquid cash (page 3). (3) Emergency fund sufficiency: 'You have confirmed that you retain sufficient liquid capital to cover any unexpected emergencies, giving you reassurance as you progress with your retirement planning' (page 3). (4) Liquidity acceptance: 'You are comfortable with the concept as you have liquid cash should you require instance access funds' (page 8). (5) No shortfall in cashflow: 'There are no issues with your cash flow forecast' and 'Your state pension income will cover your bills in retirement. Meaning your pension will be allowed to keep growing for large ad hoc spends' (page 24 of suitability report).

    File:     The case file evidences: (1) Emergency fund amount: Fact Find (P19) records 'Savings' as 'Cash Account' with value £6,000.00 dated 22/09/2025. Intelliflo planning baseline and proposed scenarios (P1, P2, P5) show 'Savings' under 'Surplus Account' valued at £6,000 as opening value in 2025. (2) Access timeline for pensions: Suitability Report (P10) describes Flexi-access Drawdown (FAD) as providing 'a flexible and tax-efficient means of drawing benefits from a pension fund'. (3) Planned withdrawals: Intelliflo planning proposed scenario (P9, P14) shows 'Required Withdrawal' analysis throughout the plan with no red bars indicating shortfalls. Cashflow table (P2, P9) demonstrates liquid assets and accessible pensions remain positive throughout the projection to age 100/104. (4) Ad-hoc access: No specific ad-hoc access requirement (beyond emergency and planned retirement drawdown) is disclosed in Fact Find (P11, P19, P22). (5) Foreseeable life changes: Timeline (P5, P13) shows 'Retire (Kim)' age 66 in 2026, 'Retire (John)' age 71 in 2027, 'Illness' events at age 100 for both clients, and 'Death' events projected at age 100. Employment income stated as £300 per week for John (HGV driver) until retirement 2027 (P17).

    Discrepancies:

      - The report states the client 'confirms' sufficient liquid capital and is 'comfortable' with liquidity, but the Fact Find (P11) shows 'Total Monthly Disposable Income' of minus £288.00 (monthly expenditure £1,718 exceeds net monthly income £1,430). This apparent contradiction between current cashflow deficit and stated comfort with liquidity is not explained.

      - The report does not specify the amount of emergency fund required or justify why £6,000 is sufficient, despite stating it has been confirmed as sufficient. No methodology for determining adequacy is provided in the evidence.

      - The report references access taking '10-15 working days' for pension funds but does not distinguish this from the £6,000 liquid emergency fund which could be accessed immediately. No clarity on whether the 10-15 day delay is acceptable for 'emergency' access as categorised.

      - The Suitability Report states pension funds are for 'large ad hoc spends' (page 24, P20) after state pension covers bills, but no specific ad-hoc spending requirement beyond the defined expenditure in the cashflow model is documented in the Fact Find.

    The core access requirements have been established: (1) Emergency fund of £6,000 in cash exists and is documented consistently across Fact Find and planning models. (2) Pension access via FAD is available from age 55 (rising to 57 from April 2028) per Suitability Report (P10, P21), providing planned drawdown capability. (3) The planning models (P2, P9, P14) show no projected shortfalls throughout the clients' lifetimes to age 100/104, meaning planned access to pension drawdown will be needed and available. (4) Foreseeable life changes (retirement ages 66/71, illness and death projected at 100) are documented in the timeline (P5, P13). However, there is a material inconsistency: the current monthly cashflow (P11) shows a deficit of £288 per month, yet the report asserts the client has 'confirmed' sufficient liquidity and is 'comfortable' with it. This is not reconciled—either the Fact Find data is outdated relative to the planning models, or the assertion of sufficiency is unsupported by current position. The planning models use £300 per week employment income (P17) which will cease at retirement (2027 for John), changing the cashflow significantly. The report does not explain how current monthly deficit transitions to the assumed surplus in the planning models. No specific ad-hoc access need beyond the regular expenditure modelled is disclosed; the report's reference to pension funds for 'large ad hoc spends' is aspirational rather than evidence-based on disclosed client requirements. The distinction between immediate access (£6,000 liquid) and 10-15 day access (pension drawdown) is stated but not evaluated for adequacy against defined emergencies. The report does not evidence what specific emergency amounts the £6,000 is intended to cover (e.g., one month expenses, three months, car repair, medical cost). Without this, the sufficiency claim cannot be verified from the file.

  [G2.5] Conflicting goals

  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report asserts three client objectives with priority ranks 1, 2, and 3. One target date is stated: 2027-10-14. The report states: (1) 'You want to ensure you can continue to afford your lifestyle through your lifetime' (priority 1); (2) 'You want to retire in 2 years time with a household income of £1,700 net per month' (priority 2); (3) 'You would like a clearer understanding of your pension arrangements and to ensure they are appropriately structured for your retirement. You also prefer a more streamlined approach to managing your pensions, rather than maintaining five separate plans with different providers' (priority 3). No target amounts are stated in the extracted model.

    File:     Evidence from the case file corroborates the three objectives. Passage P4 states: 'You want to ensure you can continue to afford your lifestyle through your lifetime', 'You want to retire in 2 years time with a household income of £1,700 net per month', and 'You would like a clearer understanding of your pension arrangements and to ensure they are appropriately structured for your retirement. You also prefer a more streamlined approach to managing your pensions, rather than maintaining five separate plans with different providers.' The planning timeline in passage P11 shows retirement ages: 'Retire (Kim)' age 66 in 2026 and 'Retire (John)' age 71 in 2027. The target date 2027-10-14 aligns with John's retirement year. The cashflow projections in passages P9 and P15 model income and expenditure to support the lifestyle continuation objective, with surplus/deficit tracking through age 75 and beyond. Passage P5 shows the planning extends to age 83/79 and beyond with positive surplus positions maintained.

    The report's stated objectives are directly supported by evidence in the case file. All three priorities appear verbatim in passage P4, the canonical source for objectives. The target date 2027-10-14 corresponds to John's retirement year in 2027 shown in the planning timeline (P11), consistent with the two-year retirement horizon stated for that client. The first objective—continuing to afford lifestyle through lifetime—is operationalised in the cashflow model (P9, P15) which projects income, expenditure and surplus/deficit through age 100, demonstrating the planning horizon extends beyond retirement. The second objective specifies £1,700 net monthly household income target, which is testable against the cashflow assumptions but is not contradicted by the evidence; the model in P15 shows first-year regular expenditure of £24,001 (approximately £2,000 per month), and P16 shows combined household employment income of £29,840 in the first year plus state pension of £12,173, totalling approximately £41,813 annually. The model's in-year surplus column (P9) shows surpluses maintained post-retirement, supporting the affordability objective. The third objective regarding pension consolidation and streamlining is confirmed by the recommendation to consolidate five separate plans into the Aviva Platform solution, evidenced across passages P3 (fund recommendations), P4 (objectives context), and the pension switch analysis. No structural conflict is apparent: the two-year retirement horizon for the advised client (John, retiring 2027) is compatible with the investment strategy recommended (Risk Rating 5 Balanced funds, P3), and the income target of £1,700 monthly is supported by the cashflow model maintaining surplus throughout retirement. Priority sequencing (1, 2, 3) is logical: ensure affordability first, retire on specified income second, restructure pensions third. The vulnerability overlay is not material here: no vulnerability drivers identified in the evidence materially affect the consistency between objectives and supporting evidence.

  [G2.6] Recommendations are appropriate for the stated goals given access needs, term and foreseeable changes

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report links six recommendations to three objectives: OBJ1, OBJ2, and OBJ3. The first recommendation (Retain People's Pension - £6,601.55) is linked to OBJ3. The second recommendation (Switch Scottish Widows - £211.32) is linked to OBJ3. The third recommendation (Switch Aviva pension - £3,744.36) is linked to OBJ2 and OBJ3. The fourth recommendation (Switch Zurich - £103,439.24) is linked to OBJ2 and OBJ3. The fifth recommendation (Switch Standard Life - £3,002) is linked to OBJ2 and OBJ3. The sixth recommendation (Aviva Pension Portfolio with £110,185 transfer value) is linked to OBJ1 and OBJ2. The report states a selected retirement age of 71 for the main recommendation, with projections shown at age 75. The client needs £1,700 net per month in retirement. Flexi-access Drawdown is recommended as the withdrawal mechanism. The fund allocation is 50% HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio and 50% CT Universal MAP Balanced.

    File:     The evidence establishes: (1) A stated retirement income need of £1,700 net per month (P1). (2) Current employment income of £300 per week net as an HGV driver, described as 'typically' £300 per week (page 3 of report per extraction), but modelled deterministically as £300 per week in cashflow (P10). (3) State pension income of £11,973 per annum from age 67 for the client and spouse (P10). (4) Annual expenditure in retirement expected to be approximately £1,700 (P10), which aligns with the monthly figure stated. (5) Pension access available from age 55, rising to 57 from 6 April 2028, with the ability to take 25% tax-free lump sum under Flexi-Access Drawdown (P6, P7, P19). (6) Projections at age 75 under mid-growth scenario: existing pension £127,000 vs new solution £115,000, a £12,000 or 9.4% decrease (P14, P15). (7) Critical yield of 4.8% required to match existing arrangement (P9, P14, P15). (8) Small pension pots under £10,000 can be taken as lump sums with first 25% tax-free (P19). (9) No explicit statement of client objectives by name (OBJ1, OBJ2, OBJ3) in the retrieved evidence. The objectives themselves are not defined or evidenced in the passages provided. (10) Cash flow runs to age 100 with no projected shortfall under baseline scenario (P10). (11) Employment income noted as variable ('typically') on page 3 but modelled as fixed £300/week.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report links recommendations to OBJ1, OBJ2, and OBJ3, but the evidence does not define or corroborate these objectives by name or their specific content. The actual client goals are not stated with named objective identifiers in any retrieved passage.

      - The report states a selected retirement age of 71 for the recommended plan, but projections are shown at age 75 (P14, P15), creating ambiguity about the intended retirement date and whether the plan is timed correctly to the stated goal.

      - The report identifies employment income as 'typically' £300 per week (per extraction from page 3), suggesting variability, but the cashflow model applies this deterministically without adjusting for the stated variability or modeling downside scenarios.

      - The report's second recommendation states 'Retain your people's pension plan' valued at £6,601.55, but the extraction notes this contradicts advice to consolidate pensions (OBJ3) and switch other four plans to Aviva, creating internal inconsistency in whether consolidation is an objective.

      - The critical yield of 4.8% required to match the existing arrangement is substantially above expected mid-growth rate of 2.95%, yet the report concludes the recommendation is achievable based on past performance. This creates a material discrepancy between the yield required and the growth assumption used to justify suitability.

    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Definition and content of the three stated objectives (OBJ1, OBJ2, OBJ3) - the objectives themselves are not articulated in any retrieved passage, Explicit corroboration that the named objectives are client-authored or client-confirmed rather than adviser-generated boilerplate, Clarity on the intended retirement date (71 vs 75) and whether the selected retirement age of 71 is a firm goal or planning assumption, Evidence of how the recommendations specifically serve each linked objective beyond generic statements about pension flexibility, Documentation of client priorities or hierarchy among the three objectives where they may conflict, Explanation of the material shortfall between the 4.8% critical yield requirement and the 2.95% mid-growth assumption used to project fund values, Evidence of any foreseeable life changes beyond the stated expectation of no change in expenditure (P24)

    This check requires that stated client goals and objectives be supported by evidence and that recommendations be explicitly linked to specific objectives rather than boilerplate. Three critical gaps emerge:

   

    1. Objectives Not Evidenced: The report asserts linkages to OBJ1, OBJ2, and OBJ3, but the evidence does not retrieve or define these objectives. The fact-find (P24) records 'No' to expected expenditure changes, but does not articulate the three named objectives or confirm they are client-driven. The extraction's ambiguities section notes the report is internally inconsistent about whether to consolidate pensions, undermining the coherence of the objective framework itself.

   

    2. Retirement Age Ambiguity: The report selects a retirement age of 71 for the recommended plan but projects outcomes at age 75. The evidence does not clarify whether 71 is the stated goal or merely an intermediate planning assumption. At age 69 (current age per data), access to a pension fund carries no statutory restriction from age 55 onwards; the guard 'Check the access mechanism, not the wrapper name' is therefore satisfied. However, the mismatch between selected and projected retirement age suggests the recommendation may not be timed to the stated goal.

   

    3. Critical Yield vs Growth Assumption: The pension switch analysis (P14, P15) shows that the new solution requires 4.8% critical yield to match the existing arrangement, but the mid-growth rate used in recommendations is 2.95%. This 1.85 percentage point gap is material. The report states (P9) that past performance suggests 4.8% 'is achievable,' but the evidence does not support this claim with specific fund track records. The projection at mid-growth shows a £12,000 decrease (9.4%), which directly contradicts the suitability assertion unless the report explicitly accepts this as a cost of achieving other objectives—which it does not state clearly in the evidence retrieved.

   

    4. Boilerplate Linkage: The recommendation text (P16) states 'It is the most suitable solution given your circumstances and retirement objectives' and lists generic benefits of personal pensions (tax relief, flexibility, drawdown options, death benefits, IHT treatment). These are standard pension features, not specific to the client's named objectives. No passage states 'This recommendation serves OBJ2 because you stated that [specific goal].' The linkage is by category (all recommendations linked to pension consolidation, drawdown, or longevity) rather than by client-specific objective naming.

   

    5. Access Needs: The evidence confirms the client can access funds from age 55 (rising to 57) and prefers Flexi-Access Drawdown (P16). Retirement income need of £1,700 net per month is stated (P1, P10). The plan projects sufficient assets to age 100 without shortfall under baseline (P10). The access mechanism is available and appropriate to the stated need. However, whether the recommended fund allocation (50% HSBC, 50% CT Universal MAP, both risk rating 5) is aligned with a 71-year-old's ability to sustain volatility for 4+ years to retirement is not explicitly addressed.

   

    6. Foreseeable Changes: The fact-find (P24) records 'No' to expected expenditure changes. Employment income is noted as 'typically' £300 per week, suggesting variability, but no scenario modeling for income reduction is evident. No other foreseeable changes (health, family, housing, relocation) are mentioned in the retrieved evidence.

   

    Conclusion on Comparison: The comparison cannot be fully performed because the objectives themselves (OBJ1, OBJ2, OBJ3) are not defined in the evidence. The report asserts linkages but the evidence does not retrieve the definitions of what is being linked to. The recommendations are appropriate to the factual needs (retirement income £1,700/month, access from 55/57, tax-efficient drawdown) but are not demonstrably linked to client-authored objectives by name. The critical yield shortfall is a material concern: a 9.4% reduction in projected fund value at the assumed mid-growth rate, balanced only by generic claims about past performance, is not a secure basis for suitability when the client is age 69 and retirement is imminent.

CHK-003 — Risk, Capacity for Loss and Investment Strategy Match

Outcome: Potential Concern

 

  Potential Concern across 10 requirement(s). 9 raised a concern (G3.1, G3.2, G3.3, G3.4, G3.5, G3.6, G3.7, G3.9, G3.10). The report asserts a 'Medium' capacity for loss rating, but no document in the evidence defines or establishes a capacity for loss rating scale. The intelliflo planning and Risk Profile documents do not use the term 'Medium' as a formal CFL classification. The report treats capacity for loss as separately assessed from attitude to risk, but the evidence contains no separate capacity for loss assessment or questionnaire result distinct from the ATR process. [P5] and [P20] record 'No additional notes were recorded' for capacity for loss, suggesting no separate assessment was documented.   [G3.2] Capacity for loss assessment

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts a 'Medium' capacity for loss rating. The assessment basis comprises six elements: (1) State Pension of approximately £230 per week; (2) continued HGV driver work at around £300 per week net plus £20 per week from Sea Cadets role; (3) spouse Kim contributes £1,100 per month from employment and will receive full State Pension of around £230 per week; (4) combined income comfortably meets household expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month; (5) savings of around £6,000 with no outstanding liabilities; (6) client confirmed that potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on day-to-day living. The report states capacity for loss is separately assessed from attitude to risk (isSeparatelyAssessedFromAtr = true).

    File:     The evidence corroborates all six component facts. [P11] and [P1] confirm: State Pension £230/week, employment income £300/week, Sea Cadets income £20/week, spouse contribution £1,100/month, household expenditure £1,700/month, savings £6,000, no liabilities, and client confirmation that underperformance would not impact day-to-day living. The intelliflo planning documents [P1], [P2] show total liquid assets of £6,000 and total assets (including pensions) of £122,997 at baseline, with positive cashflows throughout the planning horizon [P4], [P9]. Stress tests [P15] demonstrate the plan survives a 30% immediate market loss with no pension drawdown required. The Risk Profile [P19], [P20] records that under the natural risk level questionnaire, the client indicated 'More than £4,000' loss tolerance on a £20,000 investment, and notes 'No additional notes were recorded' for capacity for loss assessment. The Risk Profile [P5] states 'No additional notes were recorded' under the Capacity for Loss Questionnaire section. No formal CFL classification or scale is identified in any evidence document.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report asserts a 'Medium' capacity for loss rating, but no document in the evidence defines or establishes a capacity for loss rating scale. The intelliflo planning and Risk Profile documents do not use the term 'Medium' as a formal CFL classification.

      - The report treats capacity for loss as separately assessed from attitude to risk, but the evidence contains no separate capacity for loss assessment or questionnaire result distinct from the ATR process. [P5] and [P20] record 'No additional notes were recorded' for capacity for loss, suggesting no separate assessment was documented.

      - The CFL assessment basis in the report restates facts about financial position (income, expenditure, surplus, savings) but does not explicitly reason from these facts to a 'Medium' capacity for loss or define what 'Medium' means in this context.

      - The Risk Profile [P20] shows a loss tolerance response ('More than £4,000' on a £20,000 investment, approximately 20% tolerance) but this quantified response is not connected to or explained as supporting the 'Medium' CFL rating asserted in the suitability report.

    The financial facts undergirding the CFL assessment are fully evidenced and accurate: income sources, expenditure, surplus, liquid assets, and liabilities are all corroborated in the case file. The client's confirmation that potential underperformance would not impact day-to-day living is directly quoted in [P11]. The financial resilience is further supported by the intelliflo planning cashflows [P4], [P9] showing sustained positive liquid assets and surplus throughout the projection horizon, and stress testing [P15] demonstrating the plan's robustness to a 30% market shock. However, three material gaps emerge: (1) No capacity for loss scale or definition is established in any evidence document. The term 'Medium' appears only in the suitability report assertion; it is not a label or classification found in the Risk Profile, planning documents, or questionnaire. (2) The report claims CFL is 'separately assessed from ATR' (isSeparatelyAssessedFromAtr=true), but the evidence shows no separate CFL assessment. The Risk Profile [P5] explicitly records 'No additional notes were recorded' for the Capacity for Loss Questionnaire, and [P20] records the same for 'Capacity for loss for the goal'. The ATR questionnaire [P20] yields a rating of 4 (Cautious Balanced), later overridden to 5 (Balanced) per [P11]. There is no record of a distinct CFL questionnaire or assessment producing an independent rating. (3) The report's CFL assessment, while grounded in accurate financial facts, does not articulate or justify why these facts warrant a 'Medium' classification rather than another label, or how 'Medium' is calibrated relative to the client's risk rating of 5 (Balanced). The guard states: 'A CFL rating with no supporting financial reasoning is not evidenced merely because the words appear.' Here, the financial reasoning (income, surplus, savings) is evidenced, but the leap from these facts to the specific 'Medium' classification is not explained. The separate-assessment claim is unsupported: there is no evidence of a distinct CFL assessment tool, questionnaire result, or justification independent of ATR. This is a weak assessment not adequately evidenced by the case file.

  [G3.3] Tolerance for loss and need-level risk

  Potential Concern — severity Low

    Report:   The report asserts two statements about tolerance for loss: (1) 'You are aware that investment values can rise as well as fall' (page 4, Capacity for loss section); (2) 'You are aware that your funds may go up as well as down, and the higher risk you take with your funds, potential there is for larger drops in value' (page 4, Attitude to Risk & Capacity for loss section). No specific maximum acceptable loss percentage or amount is quantified. The report states the client 'confirmed that any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on your day-to-day living' and references the client's state pension (£230/week), employment income (£300/week), and additional income (£20/week from Sea Cadets), plus spousal contribution (£1,100/month). The agreed risk rating is 5 (Balanced), and the investment term is 2 years per the Risk profile report.

    File:     Evidence from [P18] (Suitability Report) confirms: 'You are aware that investment values can rise as well as fall' and 'You are aware that your funds may go up as well as down, and the higher risk you take with your funds, potential there is for larger drops in value.' [P18] also states: 'You confirmed that any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on your day-to-day living.' The client's income is documented as state pension £230/week, HGV driver income £300/week net, and Sea Cadets income £20/week, with spouse contributing £1,100/month. No quantified tolerance for loss (maximum acceptable loss percentage or amount) appears in any retrieved passage.

    The report's qualitative statements on tolerance for loss are fully corroborated by the evidence. The client's awareness of investment volatility and downside risk is documented consistently across [P18]. The statement that underperformance would not harm day-to-day living is supported by the financial position (secure income streams, spousal income, state pension, savings and pension funds as buffers). The agreed risk rating of 5 (Balanced) is consistently documented across multiple passages [P14], [P18], [P19]. The investment term of 2 years is confirmed in [P14]. However, the extraction metadata flags that tolerance for loss provenance quote is 'PresentButUnparseable', indicating the extraction encountered difficulty capturing formal quantification of tolerance for loss. No passage in the retrieved evidence quantifies a specific maximum acceptable loss percentage or amount. The absence of quantified tolerance for loss is a documentation gap rather than a contradiction. The guard provided states: 'Many firms do not quantify tolerance for loss separately; absence is a documentation gap, not a contradiction.' The stress test in [P23] modelling a -30% immediate loss suggests the firm considered but did not formally document a tolerance threshold. The 'need-level risk' is absent from both the extracted model and the evidence, indicating either the report does not address this or the extraction did not capture it. This is not a discrepancy between report and evidence but rather missing documentation that the requirement calls for. The qualitative tolerance statements are fully evidenced and consistent with the agreed risk rating and financial capacity.

  [G3.4] Time horizon used to support the risk profile

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   Investment term: 10 years. Objectives time horizons: Long Term, Medium Term, Medium Term.

    File:     Risk Profile report (P3) states 'Investment Period: 2 years' in the investment goal details table. SIPP research report (P9) references an agreed risk level of 5 'Balanced' but does not specify a term. Intelliflo planning scenarios (P4-P8) project cash flow through 2060, with retirement events at age 66 (2026) and age 71 (2027), extending approximately 30+ years from 2025. Key Features (P13) advises that initial costs mean investments should be viewed as 'medium to long term (typically more than five years)'.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report asserts 10-year investment term; Risk Profile report evidence shows 2-year Investment Period in the projections table (P3)

      - Report objectives include 'Long Term' horizon; Risk Profile document shows 2-year investment period, which is neither long term nor consistent with a 10-year assertion

      - No evidence in SIPP research report (P9) or other passages explicitly confirms a 10-year term; the longest documented horizon is the planning projection to 2060 (30+ years)

      - Mismatch between stated 10-year term and 2-year Investment Period used in risk projection modelling

    The canonical model asserts an investment term of 10 years. The Risk Profile report (P3), which is the primary document evidencing how the agreed risk level was determined, explicitly states 'Investment Period: 2 years' within the 'Risk level justification' section where projections are modelled. This is a material discrepancy: the report grounds its risk analysis on a 2-year projection, not a 10-year term. The objectives contain mixed horizons (Long Term, Medium Term, Medium Term), none explicitly labelled as 10 years. The Intelliflo planning scenarios project to age 100 (2060), representing a 35-year horizon for comprehensive financial planning, but this is distinct from the investment term used for risk profiling. The Key Features document notes that initial costs mean investments 'should be viewed as medium to long term (typically more than five years)', which neither aligns with 2 years nor clearly supports 10 years. The guard permits a longer investment horizon post-retirement, but the report must state this; no such explanation is provided. The 10-year term in the canonical model is not corroborated by the evidence, and the 2-year Investment Period used in the actual risk projection contradicts it. This is a material inconsistency: the risk level was justified using a 2-year horizon in the stochastic projections, yet the report asserts a 10-year term without explanation for the discrepancy or rationale for why the agreed risk level remains suitable under a longer horizon.

  [G3.5] Investment strategy and asset allocation

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts a target risk rating of 5 (Balanced). It recommends two funds with 50% allocation each: (1) HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc with risk rating 5 and 0.18% OCF, and (2) CT Universal MAP Balanced C with risk rating 5 and 0.29% OCF. Both funds are described as suitable for the client's agreed risk profile, and the combined portfolio is stated to reflect risk rating 5. The report states 'The funds reflect your risk profile both individually and combined. This means that should there be any portfolio drift, the risk being taken remains the same.'

    File:     Evidence confirms: (1) Agreed risk level is 5 per [P8], [P10], [P16] and [P22]. (2) HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio Accumulation C is recommended at 50% per [P8], [P11]. CT Universal MAP Balanced C (also called CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc) is recommended at 50% per [P8], [P11]. (3) HSBC fund carries risk rating 5 per [P11]. CT Universal MAP Balanced C carries risk rating 5 per [P11]. (4) OCF for HSBC is stated as 0.18% in [P11] and 0.18% in [P8] (ongoing costs). OCF for CT Universal MAP Balanced C is stated as 0.29% in [P11] and 0.29% in [P8] (ongoing costs). (5) HSBC fund objective states 'The Fund's risk profile is rated as 3' per [P12], contradicting the risk rating 5 assigned in the report and research. (6) Both funds are identified as Balanced risk category investments per [P10] and [P8]. (7) The portfolio strategy is described as a Model Portfolio per [P21] and [P7]. (8) Combined portfolio risk mapping shows risk level 5 matching the agreed level per [P16].

    Discrepancies:

      - HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio Accumulation C: Fund's own prospectus states risk profile rating of 3 per [P12] ('The Fund's risk profile is rated as 3 where 1 is a lower level of risk and 5 is a higher level of risk') while the report and research documents assign it a risk rating of 5 per [P11], [P8].

      - Fund name variant: Report lists 'HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc' while evidence sources refer to 'HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio Accumulation C' — these are the same fund (Acc = Accumulation) but naming inconsistency exists across documents.

      - Fund name variant: Report lists 'CT Universal MAP Balanced C' while some evidence refers to 'CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc' — these are the same fund but naming variants appear in documents.

    The primary discrepancy is the HSBC fund's risk rating. The fund's own marketing communication [P12] explicitly states its risk profile is rated as 3 on a 1-5 scale. However, the suitability report, the SIPP research report [P8], [P11], and the risk profile document [P16] all assign this fund a risk rating of 5. This is a material contradiction. The guard states 'Fund-level risk ratings from a provider are not necessarily on the firm's client risk scale' — this guard applies here: the fund's intrinsic risk rating (3) differs from the risk rating assigned in the firm's model portfolio framework (5). The question is whether the firm has properly mapped the fund to its client risk scale. The evidence in [P12] is from HSBC's own marketing communication dated August 2025, which describes the fund objective as targeting 'growth in line with its risk profile in the long term, which is a period of five years or more' with risk profile 3. The fund's KIID [P2] describes it as 'category 4' on the Risk and Reward Indicator (medium fluctuations). The research documents [P8], [P11] consistently treat both funds as risk level 5 investments suitable for a risk level 5 client. The combined portfolio is confirmed to have risk mapping of 5 matching the agreed risk level [P16]. The allocation split of 50/50 and OCF figures (0.18% and 0.29%) are consistently corroborated. The statement that funds 'reflect your risk profile both individually and combined' is not fully supported: the HSBC fund's stated own risk profile (3) does not match the 5 assigned to it in the portfolio framework. The CT Universal MAP Balanced C fund is consistently described as risk level 5/Balanced category. The fund name variants (Acc vs Accumulation, MAP Balanced vs MAP Balanced Fund) are naming conventions and do not represent material discrepancies. The model portfolio structure is confirmed. The core issue is whether a fund rated 3 by its manager can legitimately be positioned as a risk level 5 investment in the firm's framework without explicit explanation of the mapping rationale.

The evidence confirms discussion occurred and ratings 4 and 5 are referenced, but does not independently corroborate the adviser's specific reasoning that the questionnaire was misaligned with the client's appetite.
