  [G3.7] Recommendation or strategy exceeds risk tolerance, taking account of capacity for loss

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   Target risk rating 5 (Balanced). Agreed client risk rating 5. Capacity for loss rated as Medium. Recommended investment strategy comprises CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C (50%) and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio (50%), both Balanced risk funds aligned to risk rating 5. Existing arrangements retain various risk levels (9, 4, 2, 7, 4 on provider scales). No override of questionnaire result (which showed rating 4) is documented with justification or client acknowledgement.

    File:     Natural attitude to risk questionnaire completed 18/09/2025 shows rating 4 (Cautious Balanced). Report states adviser felt this did not fully reflect client's risk appetite and client agreed to increase to rating 5 (Balanced). Client confirmed wish to increase to rating 5. Capacity for loss described as medium based on income comfortably meeting expenditure with savings and pension funds. Recommended funds are CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio, both designated as Balanced (risk level 5). Evidence confirms agreed risk level is 5 and client acknowledged awareness that this is higher than questionnaire result and greater growth potential over 10-year period was client's stated objective.

    Discrepancies:

      - Questionnaire result (rating 4) was overridden upward to rating 5 without formal documented justification or client written acknowledgement recorded in extraction. Report narrative in P5 describes discussion and client confirmation but no signed override document or explicit risk override justification is cited.

      - Capacity for loss is described as 'Medium' but no formal capacity for loss assessment scale or rating framework is identified in the evidence. The justification given (income meets expenditure, savings present, pension funds available) is a restatement of financial position rather than a capacity for loss rating with defined loss tolerance.

      - Existing arrangement Zurich (figure shown as 0.93% at £961.98 charge implies £103,438.71 fund value) maps to a provider risk scale rating of 7, which sits above the agreed client rating of 5. No explicit statement that this retained arrangement aligns with the agreed risk profile or that client has been advised of this mismatch.

      - Tone of report narrative suggests adviser assessment ('we felt') preceded client agreement, creating ambiguity about whether override was adviser-initiated or client-initiated, though P5 confirms client confirmed the increase.

    The core requirement tests whether the recommended strategy exceeds risk tolerance and capacity for loss. The evidence establishes: (1) Questionnaire natural rating 4 (Cautious Balanced); (2) Agreed rating 5 (Balanced) after discussion; (3) Client acknowledged increase and stated objective of growth over 10 years; (4) Recommended funds both designated Balanced (risk 5); (5) Capacity for loss described as Medium and supported by financial position review. The recommendation aligns with agreed rating 5 on a 1–5 scale stated consistently in D, G categories. However, material gaps exist: (a) Capacity for loss is not quantified or formally rated on an identified scale—it is described qualitatively as 'medium' without loss tolerance threshold or scenario modelling. Severity guidance notes 'Low: tolerance for loss not quantified where no large-loss scenario is modelled'; here, no such scenario is evidenced. (b) The upward override from rating 4 to 5 is acknowledged by client in narrative form in P5 but no formal signed override document with dated client acceptance is cited by the extraction. The extraction flags this as 'PresentButUnparseable' in expectedButAbsent, suggesting the justification exists in prose but was not formally captured. (c) Existing Zurich arrangement (provider risk rating 7, fund value c. £103,438) is retained but no explicit statement aligns it to the agreed risk profile 5 or explains whether its higher risk rating is accepted. The 'Guard: A retained plan invested outside the agreed profile is a concern wherever the report claims alignment for it' applies: the report does not explicitly claim the Zurich arrangement aligns, but it also does not explain why a rating-7 plan remains under a rating-5 agreed profile. The ambiguity extraction flags around override rationale ('adviser felt' vs. 'client wished') reflects genuine tension in narrative: was this adviser-led override or client choice? P5 confirms client chose it, but the sequence described (adviser assessment followed by client confirmation) is the sequence typical of adviser-led override, not client-initiated increase. This is a Moderate concern: client has acknowledged the increase and stated growth objective, and recommended funds align with agreed rating 5, but the formal justification for overriding the questionnaire result is not crisply documented, capacity for loss is unquantified, and a retained high-risk arrangement sits unexamined against the agreed profile.

  [G3.9] Recommended funds and portfolio align with the client's attitude to risk, capacity for loss and investment term

  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report recommends a portfolio of two funds with agreed risk rating 5 (Balanced): HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc (50%, risk rating 5, 0.18% charge) and CT Universal MAP Balanced C (50%, risk rating 5, 0.29% charge). The report states the client has agreed attitude to risk of 5 on a 1-10 scale, medium capacity for loss, and a 10-year investment term. The report asserts the funds 'reflect your risk profile both individually and combined' and are 'risk-profiled solutions, meaning they are specifically designed to remain within your agreed level of risk'. The rationale links the portfolio to the client's financial goals and risk profile.

    File:     Evidence shows the agreed risk level is 5 (Balanced) per the SIPP research report [P6] and [P12], and the client's natural risk level from questionnaire was 4, upgraded to 5 after adviser assessment [P6]. The HSBC fund is described in its KIID [P9] as 'risk profile' rated 3 on a 1-5 scale (where 1 is lower risk, 5 is higher risk), meaning it maps to risk level 3, not 5. The fund's risk and reward profile [P1] shows it ranked 4 on a 1-7 SRRI scale. The CT Universal MAP Balanced fund is listed in the research report shortlist [P10] as a risk level 5 investment matched to the agreed risk level. Combined performance data [P4] shows both funds' discrete annual returns and volatility. The HSBC fund factsheet [P8] shows 3-year volatility of 7.12% vs peer group average 6.67%, with the fund positioned in the moderate allocation category. The investment term is 10 years [P6] per the risk profile report. Capacity for loss is stated as medium [P6] and [P12], based on the client's strong financial position with combined household income exceeding expenditure, £6,000 savings, and no liabilities.

    Discrepancies:

      - The HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio is described in its own KIID [P9] as 'rated as 3' on the fund's internal 1-5 risk scale, but the report recommends it with a stated risk rating of 5, creating a mismatch between the fund's own risk classification (3) and the risk rating assigned to it in the recommendation (5)

      - The report states both recommended funds have individual risk rating 5, but the HSBC fund's KIID [P9] establishes its own risk profile as 3 on its proprietary scale, and the SRRI indicator [P1] shows rank 4 on the 1-7 standardised scale

    A guard applies: 'A fund's SRRI or risk-and-reward indicator runs on its own scale and does not map onto the profiler's; use it as corroboration, never as a value match.' The HSBC fund's own KIID [P9] states it is 'rated as 3' within HSBC's range of five different risk levels (where 1 is lower and 5 is higher), but this is the fund manager's internal classification for that fund, separate from the client's agreed risk rating of 5. The client's agreed risk level 5 (Balanced) is supported by evidence [P6], [P12]: the natural risk questionnaire result was 4, which was discussed and upgraded to agreed level 5. The CT Universal MAP Balanced fund appears in the shortlist [P10] and is confirmed in the investment allocation [P12] at 50% of the portfolio. The HSBC fund is also confirmed [P4], [P12] at 50%. Both funds are listed as part of the agreed risk level 5 portfolio in [P12]. The report's claim that the funds 'reflect your risk profile both individually and combined' refers to the client's agreed risk rating of 5, not the funds' internal risk classifications. The portfolio (50/50 HSBC/CT) is evidenced in [P12]. The investment term of 10 years is stated in [P6]. Capacity for loss is assessed as medium and supported by the income and asset detail in the capacity for loss section. The guard explaining that marketing names ('Balanced') are not risk ratings applies here: the fund name and the fund's own risk classification (3 on its scale) do not contradict the client's agreed risk profile (5 on the profiler's scale). The alignment claim—that the funds are designed to remain within the client's agreed risk level—relies on the portfolio construction process evidenced in [P6], where each risk level is mapped to asset class breakdowns. The report's assertion that both funds have risk rating 5 must be understood as their designation within the agreed risk level portfolio, not as an independent fund property. No evidence contradicts that the portfolio as a whole is suitable for a client with agreed risk rating 5, 10-year term, and medium capacity for loss.

  [G3.10] Unsupported, contradictory or unexplained statements about risk, loss capacity, term or strategy

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   Report asserts: (1) Attitude to Risk questionnaire indicated rating 4 ('Cautious Balanced'); adviser felt this didn't fully reflect client's risk appetite; agreed rating 5 ('Balanced'). Client confirmed wish to increase rating to 5, aware this is higher than questionnaire result, happy to take on this risk. Client not reliant on pension funds, wishes best growth potential over 10-year period, feels balanced approach achieves this goal. (2) Capacity for loss rated 'Medium', based on: State Pension ~£230/week; employment income ~£300/week net as HGV driver plus £20/week from Sea Cadets; spouse Kim contributes £1,100/month and entitled to State Pension ~£230/week at SPA; combined income comfortably meets ~£1,700/month household expenditure; savings ~£6,000, no liabilities; potential underperformance would not detrimental impact day-to-day living. CFL consistent with agreed ATR level 5. (3) Investment term: 10 years. (4) Recommended strategy: Risk 5 Balanced portfolio, two funds each 50% (HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc risk rating 5, 0.18% charge; CT Universal MAP Balanced C risk rating 5, 0.29% charge). Funds reflect client's risk profile individually and combined; portfolio drift maintains same risk; diversified across asset classes; blend of active and passive; simple; competitive charges; good performance; part of CIP with quarterly review. Strategy aligns with financial goals and risk profile. All statements claimed as accurate and consistent.

    File:     Evidence retrieved shows: (1) Risk Profile Report [P1, P8] documents natural risk level 4 from ATR questionnaire ('Cautious Balanced') dated 18/09/2025. Agreed risk level 5 ('Balanced') reached following interactive discussion considering 'natural attitude to risk, capacity for loss and required return'. [P1]: 'The natural risk level, which is ascertained from the psychometric attitude to risk questionnaire score, is used as a starting point to discuss the actual level of risk you are willing and able to take to meet your specific investment objective.' [P8]: 'As a Balanced investor you do not see yourself as a particularly cautious person...' (2) Risk Profile Report [P4] notes: 'No additional notes were recorded' under both 'Capacity for loss questionnaire' section and 'Agreed risk level' section. Investment target section [P3] also shows 'No additional notes were recorded'. (3) ATR Questionnaire responses [P16] show client selected 'Cautious' as investment approach, expressed discomfort with >£4,000 loss on £20,000 investment, associates risk with 'Caution', 'More cautious' vs peers, willing to accept 'slight risk of capital loss'. Capacity for Loss questionnaire responses [P15] show income details and assess liquid savings. (4) Suitability Report [P17] states client 'aware that investment values can rise as well as fall', 'confirmed that any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on your day-to-day living', receives ~£230/week State Pension, earns ~£300/week net as HGV driver plus £20/week Sea Cadets, spouse contributes £1,100/month, combined income meets ~£1,700/month expenditure. (5) File notes [P7] document existing fund risk ratings: Aviva current risk 2, Zurich current risk 7, Standard Life current risk 4, People's Pension 'alternative option', Scottish Widows 'alternative option'. (6) SIPP Research Report [P5] confirms agreed risk level 5, describes Balanced investor profile, recommends CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc (50%) and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio Accumulation C (50%), both described as risk level 5. [P5] shows MiFID II cost disclosures. (7) Suitability Report [P18] confirms fund recommendation: HSBC 50% risk rating 5 with 0.18% charge, CT Universal MAP 50% risk rating 5 with 0.29% charge. States funds 'reflect your risk profile both individually and combined', provide diversification, blend active/passive, meet simplicity preference, have competitive charges and good performance, part of CIP with quarterly review. (8) Investment term not explicitly corroborated in retrieved passages; Risk Profile Report [P8] shows 'Investment Period: 2 years' in projections table, contradicting 10-year horizon stated in report. (9) Capacity for loss assessment basis appears in report [P17] but Risk Profile Report sections [P4] show 'No additional notes were recorded' for Capacity for Loss questionnaire discussion and Agreed risk level justification.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states client's investment term is 10 years ('best growth potential over a 10-year period'); Risk Profile Report [P8] shows 'Investment Period: 2 years' in the projections used for risk level justification, creating a 8-year discrepancy in stated investment horizon.

      - Report asserts CFL assessment rationale spans State Pension, employment income, spouse income, surplus income, liquid assets, and no detriment statement, all attributed to capacity for loss discussion section [P4]; but Risk Profile Report [P4] states 'No additional notes were recorded' under 'Capacity for loss questionnaire' section, suggesting no documented discussion notes beyond questionnaire responses.

      - Report states 'We agreed that you are a risk rating 5 rather than a 4 as per the questionnaire' and client 'confirmed that you wish to increase your Risk rating to 5'; the extraction metadata flags ambiguity: unclear whether final agreed rating is 4 or 5, whether adviser's assessment moved it to 5 or client chose to increase, and extraction identifies candidate readings 'Risk rating 4 per questionnaire' vs 'Risk rating 5 agreed and confirmed'.

    The report asserts a 10-year investment term while Risk Profile Report [P8] shows the projections table used to justify the agreed risk level models a 2-year investment period. This is a material discrepancy: investment term is a core component of suitability assessment and capacity for loss calculation. The discrepancy is not explained by a temporal guard (e.g., client changed horizon between assessments) because both documents are dated within the same file. The extraction metadata itself acknowledges this as an ambiguity at path '/replacementAnalysis', noting the internalInconsistency in stated investment periods.

   

    The report's capacity for loss assessment basis—State Pension £230/week, HGV driver income £300/week net, Sea Cadets £20/week, spouse £1,100/month, expenditure £1,700/month, savings £6,000, no liabilities, no detriment to day-to-day living—is cited verbatim from the report and corroborated in passages [P17] (Suitability Report) and [P15] (Questionnaire responses). However, Risk Profile Report [P4] explicitly records 'No additional notes were recorded' under the Capacity for Loss questionnaire section and under Agreed risk level, which suggests the detailed discussion documented in the suitability report was not recorded in the source risk profile document. This is not a contradiction of the facts stated (income figures, savings, expenditure are consistent across documents) but rather a documentation gap: the justification exists in the report but the risk profile document shows no notes recorded for discussion of capacity for loss or agreed risk level. The extraction identified this as an ambiguity: capacity for loss described as 'medium' but justification refers to strong financial position.

   

    On attitude to risk override: The report asserts questionnaire indicated rating 4, adviser felt this didn't reflect appetite, agreed rating 5, client confirmed wish to increase to 5 and was happy to do so. This is corroborated by [P1, P8] (Risk Profile Report showing natural risk 4, agreed risk 5, and methodology for reaching agreed level through discussion) and [P17] (Suitability Report repeating the same). The override direction and justification ('didn't fully reflect your risk appetite') is stated and acknowledged by client ('wish to increase your Risk rating to 5', 'aware this is higher', 'happy to take on this risk'). Extraction metadata flagged ambiguity on whether final rating is 4 or 5, but passages and report are consistent: agreed rating is 5. No contradiction stands.

   

    On recommended strategy and risk alignment: Report asserts both funds carry risk rating 5, portfolio designed to remain within risk level 5. [P5] (SIPP Research Report) and [P18] (Suitability Report) confirm both HSBC and CT Universal MAP are risk-profiled solutions rated level 5. No contradiction on fund risk ratings or strategy-to-risk alignment.

   

    On tolerance for loss: Report includes statements that client is 'aware that investment values can rise as well as fall' and 'higher risk...potential there is for larger drops in value'. These are corroborated by [P17]. No quantified maximum acceptable loss percentage or amount is asserted or required; extraction shows 'maximumAcceptableLossPercentage: null' and 'maximumAcceptableLossAmount: null'. Severity guidance notes 'Low: tolerance for loss not quantified where no large-loss scenario is modelled', but this is guidance for cases where such quantification is missing and material; no assertion of a specific tolerance threshold was made to contradict.

   

    The one material finding is the investment term discrepancy: 10 years vs 2 years. This directly affects the suitability assessment because investment horizon is a core input to risk capacity and strategy selection. The discrepancy stands and is unsupported/unexplained across documents.

CHK-004 — Knowledge, Experience and Product Complexity

Outcome: Potential Concern

 

  Potential Concern across 7 requirement(s). 6 raised a concern (G4.1, G4.2, G4.4, G4.5, G4.6, G4.7). Report states knowledge level is 'Moderate' but questionnaire records 'No understanding / knowledge' for stocks and shares, EIS/VCTs, and pension planning. Report implies client has moderate investment experience and understanding, but questionnaire shows no investment in Unit Trusts, stocks and shares, EIS/VCTs, or pension products within last 5 years. Questionnaire responses indicate limited knowledge confined to cash and savings products, not moderate knowledge across investment types. 3 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

 

  [G4.1] Client's stated product and investment knowledge level

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The suitability report asserts the client's knowledge level as 'Moderate'.

    File:     The risk profile questionnaire completed on 18/09/2025 records the client's responses to direct questions about investment knowledge and experience. On the question 'Do you understand how stocks and shares work and do you know the risks associated with them?' the response is 'No understanding / knowledge'. On 'Do you understand how Enterprise Investment Schemes and Venture Capital Trusts work and do you know the risks associated with them?' the response is 'No understanding / knowledge'. On 'Do you understand how pension planning and income in retirement works and do you know the risks associated with them?' the response is 'No understanding / knowledge'. The questionnaire also records that the client has not invested in Unit Trusts and General Investment Accounts within the last 5 years, has not invested in stocks and shares within the last 5 years, has not invested in Enterprise Investment Schemes and Venture Capital Trusts within the last 5 years, and has not invested in pension planning and income in retirement products within the last 5 years. The only investment experience recorded is 'Yes, by myself' for Current Accounts, Savings/Deposit Accounts, Cash ISA, National Savings and Investments within the last 5 years.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states knowledge level is 'Moderate' but questionnaire records 'No understanding / knowledge' for stocks and shares, EIS/VCTs, and pension planning

      - Report implies client has moderate investment experience and understanding, but questionnaire shows no investment in Unit Trusts, stocks and shares, EIS/VCTs, or pension products within last 5 years

      - Questionnaire responses indicate limited knowledge confined to cash and savings products, not moderate knowledge across investment types

    The suitability report states the client's knowledge level as 'Moderate', but the evidence from the risk profile questionnaire directly contradicts this assessment. The questionnaire, completed on 18/09/2025 (the same date as the meeting), contains direct questions on the client's understanding of key investment types. The client answered 'No understanding / knowledge' to three explicit questions: understanding of stocks and shares, understanding of EIS/VCTs, and understanding of pension planning. The questionnaire also evidences that the client has had no experience investing in any of these product types over the past 5 years. The only positive response on investment experience is for cash savings products. This is assessed knowledge, not self-deprecation; it is the response to a direct questionnaire item. The suitability report later states 'you understand that returns are not guaranteed' and describes the client as accepting higher risk with awareness, language suggesting comfort and understanding. However, the questionnaire evidence shows the opposite: no understanding recorded for the very products being recommended (balanced mixed-asset funds investing globally in equities and bonds). The Fact Find (page 7) lists employment and cash savings but records no prior investment holdings or experience beyond workplace pensions. The report's characterization of the client's knowledge as 'Moderate' is not supported by and directly contradicts the questionnaire evidence of 'No understanding / knowledge' across stocks, shares, pension planning and investment structures.

  [G4.2] Previous investment and pension experience

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts the client: (1) has previously taken out cash savings products; (2) has NOT taken out stocks & shares investments by themselves; (3) has held group pensions for some time.

    File:     The risk profile questionnaire (P1) records: (1) Yes, invested in Current Accounts, Savings/Deposit Accounts, Cash ISA within the last 5 years by myself; (2) No, have not invested in stocks and shares within the last 5 years; (3) No, have not invested in pension planning and income in retirement products within the last 5 years. The questionnaire also states: 'Do you understand how pension planning and income in retirement works' - Response: 'No understanding / knowledge'. The fact find (P15) lists client occupation as 'HGV Driver' employed by 'CDH Recruitment'. No explicit record of group pension holdings or duration is present in the extracted file evidence.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states client 'has held your group pensions for some time' but the risk profile questionnaire records 'No' to 'Have you invested in pension planning and income in retirement products within the last 5 years?' This indicates either no recent pension product experience or the questionnaire answer contradicts the report's assertion of 'some time' holding.

      - Report asserts client has held group pensions, but the risk profile questionnaire asks 'Do you understand how pension planning and income in retirement works' with response 'No understanding / knowledge', suggesting limited or no comprehension despite stated holdings.

      - The existing arrangements list five pension plans (People's Pension £6,601.55, Scottish Widows £211.32, Aviva £3,744.36, Zurich £103,439.24, Standard Life £3,002.00), but the fact find and risk profile do not explicitly confirm the client currently holds or has held these as group pensions versus personal arrangements, nor do they record the duration or nature of tenure.

    The report's three assertions about product experience must be tested against the questionnaire and fact-find evidence. (1) Cash savings products: The report cites page 4 stating 'You confirmed that you have previously taken out cash savings products.' The risk profile (P1) confirms 'Yes, by myself' to the question on Current Accounts, Savings/Deposit Accounts, Cash ISA within the last 5 years. This is consistent. (2) Stocks & shares: The report states the client 'have not taken out a stocks & shares investment by yourself.' The risk profile (P1) confirms 'No' to both 'Have you invested in stocks and shares within the last 5 years?' and broader understanding of stocks/shares is 'No understanding / knowledge.' This is consistent. (3) Group pensions: The report asserts 'You have however held your group pensions for some time.' This creates a material discrepancy. The risk profile questionnaire (P1) records 'No' to 'Have you invested in pension planning and income in retirement products within the last 5 years?' and records 'No understanding / knowledge' to the question 'Do you understand how pension planning and income in retirement works and do you know the risks associated with them?' (P1). The five existing pension arrangements listed in the canonical model and the fact-find (P18) show multiple pension plans with values totalling £116,998.47, but the risk profile does not affirmatively record prior experience with pensions, and explicitly records no understanding of pension planning. The discrepancy is material: the report claims 'held your group pensions for some time' implying demonstrated experience, but the questionnaire shows no understanding of pension planning and answers 'No' to investing in retirement products within 5 years. This suggests either: (a) the client holds pensions passively (e.g., workplace auto-enrolment) without active engagement or understanding, or (b) the report overstates the nature and duration of the experience. The guard provided states 'Holding a workplace pension is passive experience; a report distinguishing it from self-directed investing is being accurate, not inconsistent.' However, the report does not distinguish between passive and active experience—it asserts the client 'has held your group pensions for some time,' which in context of the knowledge & experience section implies engaged experience. The risk profile's 'No understanding' of pensions contradicts the implication of meaningful, longstanding group pension holdings. The vulnerability overlay requires consideration of any support needs or communication requirements. No vulnerability drivers affecting knowledge/experience assessment are recorded in the evidence.

  [G4.4] Recommended product is too complex for the stated level of knowledge, or the knowledge level has been misinterpreted

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report states the client's knowledge level is 'Moderate' and recommends a 'Complex' product. The suitability report asserts (P5): 'In addition to your attitude to investment risk and capacity for loss, I have also considered your investment objectives as well as your knowledge and experience, and the length of time you wish to invest for.' The report describes the recommended funds as multi-asset funds providing 'a professionally managed, diversified investment solution' and states 'You want to keep the investment strategy of your pension fairly simple.' The report also states (P7): 'If you have some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters, then you may be suited to a Balanced approach to investing.'

    File:     The fact find and risk profile record contradictory evidence of the client's actual investment knowledge. The questionnaire responses (P9) show: 'Do you understand how stocks and shares work and do you know the risks associated with them? No understanding / knowledge'; 'Do you understand how Enterprise Investment Schemes and Venture Capital Trusts work and do you know the risks associated with them? No understanding / knowledge'; 'Do you understand how pension planning and income in retirement works and do you know the risks associated with them? No understanding / knowledge'; 'Have you invested in Unit Trusts and General Investment Accounts within the last 5 years? No'; 'Have you invested in stocks and shares within the last 5 years? No'; 'Have you invested in Enterprise Investment Schemes and Venture Capital Trusts within the last 5 years? No'; 'Have you invested in pension planning and income in retirement products within the last 5 years? No'. The only affirmative investment response is 'Have you invested in Current Accounts, Savings/Deposit Accounts, Cash ISA, National Savings and Investments within the last 5 years? Yes, by myself'. The risk profile document notes (P10): 'No additional notes were recorded' under Investor Experience section, and 'No additional notes were recorded' under Capacity For Loss questionnaire notes. The report itself states (P7): 'You confirmed that you wish to increase your Risk rating to 5' and notes client's experience is 'limited to your workplace pension arrangements.'

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states knowledge level is 'Moderate' but questionnaire responses record 'No understanding / knowledge' of stocks and shares, their risks, pension planning, and income in retirement risks

      - Report states client has 'some experience in investments' (P7, reproducing description requiring such experience) but file shows no investment experience beyond basic savings accounts and workplace pension

      - Report asserts 'If you have some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters, then you may be suited to a Balanced approach' but file explicitly records no understanding of stocks and shares or pension planning

      - Report describes investment as keeping strategy 'fairly simple' yet recommends two actively managed multi-asset funds (HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio and CT Universal MAP Balanced) with layered complexity via 'CIP' approach, contradicting simplicity claim

      - Report states client awareness 'limited to your workplace pension arrangements' (P7) yet ascribes 'Moderate' knowledge level to justify recommending Complex product

    The core issue is a material contradiction between the report's stated knowledge level ('Moderate') and the file's recorded knowledge. The questionnaire data (P9) demonstrates no understanding of equity investments, their risks, or pension planning. The client's only investment experience is basic savings products. The report acknowledges this limitation ('experience is limited to your workplace pension arrangements') but then assigns a 'Moderate' knowledge classification to justify recommending a 'Complex' product. The precondition for the description quoted in P7 ('If you have some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters, then you may be suited to a Balanced approach') is explicitly not met: the file records no such experience or understanding. The guard applies here: the risk-profile description carries preconditions about experience that the file contradicts, and the report is using this description as evidence of knowledge it does not establish. The report's own assertion that the investment keeps the strategy 'fairly simple' is contradicted by the recommended structure: two actively managed funds (HSBC at 0.18% and CT Universal MAP at 0.29%) plus platform and adviser overlay, which the report itself describes as a layered, actively monitored 'CIP' approach requiring ongoing committee oversight (P8). This is not a simple solution for a client with no equity experience. The vulnerability overlay is relevant: the client is an HGV driver with limited income (£300/week net plus £20/week from Sea Cadets role), receiving State Pension of £230/week. Combined with spouse income of £1,100/month, the household is modestly positioned. The client's lack of financial knowledge, combined with modest income resources, creates a vulnerability that heightens the suitability concern when a Complex product is recommended without clear evidence the client comprehends its constituent parts or their interactions.

The risk assessment notes show 'No additional notes were recorded' for investor experience and capacity for loss discussion, indicating no detailed exploration of the client's actual understanding. The guard acknowledges that absence of recorded questions is not proof of non-understanding, but given: (1) the clear contradiction between the report's assertion and the file's record of limited experience; (2) the complexity of the recommended solution (active multi-fund management, diversification strategy, governance structure); and (3) the absence of any record of client questions or comprehension testing in the file, a material evidence gap exists regarding whether the client can actually evaluate the recommended products' risks and features.

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report states the client has: (1) previously taken out cash savings products; (2) NOT taken out stocks & shares investments by themselves; (3) held group pensions for some time.     File:     The evidence confirms five pension arrangements exist with documented holdings: (1) People's Pension - Global Investments (up to 85% shares) per P12, consistent with risk rating 9 per report; (2) Scottish Widows - SW Pension Portfolio Four CS1 & Three CS1 per P11/P13, risk 4; (3) Aviva Designer Stakeholder - My Future Focus Consolidation (Pre-2024) S2 per P2, £3,744.36, with auto-switching lifestage approach; (4) Zurich - Managed AP (accumulation) per P8, £103,439.24, risk 7; (5) Standard Life - At Retirement Universal (PP 10 year) per P5/P14, £2,994.30, invested 100% in SL At Retirement Universal fund. All five are occupational or personal pension arrangements, not stocks & shares ISAs or direct equity holdings. No evidence of cash savings products specifically documented in policy statements. No evidence the client has direct experience with stocks & shares self-selected investments—all holdings are within pension wrappers and several employ auto-switching/lifestyling approaches (Aviva, Standard Life).

    Discrepancies:

      - Report asserts client has 'previously taken out cash savings products' but no supporting evidence of cash savings product holdings or statements appears in the file; only pension arrangements are documented.

      - Report states client has 'not taken out a stocks & shares investment by yourself' which is consistent with the file showing only pension-wrapped holdings and no direct investment account or ISA evidence.

      - Report notes client held 'group pensions for some time' but existing arrangements include both occupational pensions (People's Pension appears to be workplace-auto-enrolled based on P12 glidepath language, though not explicitly labelled occupational) and personal pensions (Aviva, Scottish Widows, Zurich, Standard Life); the report does not distinguish between these categories in the stated experience narrative.

      - The existing holdings span risk ratings 2 to 9, including a high-risk holding (Global Investments up to 85% shares, risk 9) and two lifestyled funds (Aviva and Standard Life). The report does not explain how a client stated to have no direct stocks & shares experience came to hold such a wide risk spectrum, nor does it explain the apparent contradiction between limited knowledge and existing high-risk holdings.

    The report's assertion that the client has 'previously taken out cash savings products' is not supported by any evidence in the file. The policy statements and account information focus exclusively on pension arrangements; no cash savings product statements, ISA statements, or savings account evidence appears. This is a material gap: the report claims prior experience but the file does not corroborate it. The assertion that the client has 'not taken out a stocks & shares investment by yourself' is consistent with the evidence: all documented holdings are within pension structures, and no self-directed brokerage account or stocks & shares ISA appears in the file. However, the existing pension holdings themselves present a contradiction the report does not address. The client's arrangements span from risk 2 (lifestyled, conservative Aviva and Standard Life funds) to risk 9 (high-equity Global Investments). The report states the client has not taken out stocks & shares investments 'by yourself' but does not explain how the client came to hold a 85%-equities fund (People's Pension Global Investments risk 9) if the client has limited or no direct investment experience. The guardianship notes that 'holding a product is not the same as understanding it' and 'an arrangement inherited or set up by a previous adviser says little about the client's own experience.' Applied here: the existing diverse holdings, particularly the high-risk Global Investments and the lifestyled funds, suggest either prior adviser setup or workplace auto-enrolment with default allocations, not personal knowledge-driven selection. The report acknowledges the client is not a direct investor ('have not taken out...by yourself') but does not reconcile this with the fact that the existing arrangements include complex, high-risk holdings. The assertion of prior cash savings experience cannot be verified. The stated experience level (no direct stocks & shares, some group pension history) may be understated relative to the risk complexity already present in the existing portfolio, or those holdings may represent adviser-selected or default allocations rather than client-driven experience. A comparison can be performed because the holdings are documented; however, the report's narrative on prior experience is incomplete and partly unsupported.

  [G4.7] Adequacy of the rationale supporting a complex recommendation

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   Report asserts that recommended products are Complex, driven by: (1) Pension freedoms and flexible access drawdown options; (2) Multiple fund choices within platform; (3) Tax-efficient withdrawal strategies required; (4) Death benefit options and nominations; (5) Inheritance Tax planning considerations from April 2027. Report provides rationale across 10 topics: EmployerContributions, SmallPotsEncashment, LowerCosts, LifestylingRisk, GreaterInvestmentChoice, AdviserChargingFacilitation, OngoingMonitoring, MultiAssetFundAccess, EnhancedRetirementFlexibility, and FlexibilityAndChoiceInRetirement. Rationale addresses consolidation objectives, fund switching justifications, and platform selection benefits.

    File:     File evidence records client as risk rating 5 (Balanced investor). File notes on ATR state: 'If you have some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters, then you may be suited to a Balanced approach' [P8]. Investor Experience section in risk profile notes 'No additional notes were recorded' [P14]. Capacity for loss notes record client awareness that 'investment values can rise as well as fall, and although your experience is limited to your workplace pension arrangements, you understand that returns are not guaranteed' [P8]. File records natural risk level as 4, selected ATR as 5 [P12]. Fact Find completed 19/09/2025 with ATR/CFL fully completed with detailed notes [P12]. Risk profile report Investor Experience section shows no recorded discussion notes [P14]. File evidence does not record any discussion of pension freedoms, drawdown options, death benefit nominations, or IHT planning considerations from April 2027.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report identifies complexity drivers including 'Pension freedoms and flexible access drawdown options', 'Death benefit options and nominations', and 'Inheritance Tax planning considerations from April 2027' but file evidence contains no recorded discussion of these topics in Investor Experience section [P14] which shows 'No additional notes were recorded'

      - Report describes client as requiring 'Tax-efficient withdrawal strategies' but file records client experience as 'limited to your workplace pension arrangements' [P8], suggesting no prior experience with complex withdrawal strategy planning

      - Report rationale emphasizes 'Multiple fund choices within platform' and 'Greater Investment Choice' but file notes record client objective as simply 'Just needs advice helping him organise his pensions in the best place. Wants to consolidate to help him get his head around what he has' [P12], indicating preference for simplification rather than expanded choice

      - File records natural ATR as 4 (Cautious Balanced) with selected ATR as 5 (Balanced) [P12], but risk profile report Investor Experience notes show no recorded discussion explaining why client can handle complex product features despite limited investment experience [P14]

    The report asserts complexity and provides rationale, satisfying the structural requirement for a complex product recommendation. However, material gaps exist between the rationale offered and the file evidence of client knowledge and experience. The report identifies five complexity drivers, three of which (pension freedoms/drawdown, death benefit nominations, IHT planning from April 2027) are significant pension-specific features. The file records the client's experience as limited—explicitly stated as 'limited to your workplace pension arrangements' [P8]—yet contains no recorded discussion of the client's understanding of these complex features. The Investor Experience section of the risk profile report contains no notes whatsoever [P14], despite the fact find recording ATR/CFL as 'Fully completed with detailed notes' [P12]. This is a material inconsistency: if the client's knowledge was assessed and found adequate to support a complex product recommendation addressing drawdown options, death benefits, and IHT planning, that assessment should be documented in the risk profile. The client's stated objective was consolidation and simplification ('get his head around what he has') [P12], not expanded choice. While the report's rationale includes 'Greater Investment Choice' as a benefit, this appears to conflict with the recorded client objective. The discrepancy is not explained by any guard: the comparison can be made; both the report assertion and file evidence are present; and there is no temporal or contextual reason why a rationale addressing complex features should exist in the report but no corresponding record of client comprehension or agreement should exist in the file. The severity is High because the report's description of client knowledge (implicitly capable of handling complex withdrawal, death benefit, and IHT features) contradicts the fact file record of experience limited to workplace pensions, with no documented discussion bridging that gap.

CHK-005 — Financial Resilience, Affordability and Liquidity

Outcome: Potential Concern

 

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 8 raised a concern (G5.1, G5.2, G5.3, G5.4, G5.5, G5.6, G5.7, G5.8). Report asserts household expenditure 'approximately £1,700 per month' (P7); Fact Find records £1,718.00 monthly (P1). Difference of £18 (1.1%). Report asserts combined income 'comfortably meets' household expenditure (P7); Fact Find shows net monthly income £1,430.00 against expenditure £1,718.00, yielding disposable income of £-288.00 (P1). Income does not meet expenditure; there is a £288 monthly deficit. Report asserts pension funds 'totalling approximately £106,000' (P7); five identified arrangements sum to £116,998.47 (P1, P5, P8). Difference of £10,998.47 (9.4%). Additionally, the recommended transfer value stated as £110,185 (P19) differs from both figures. 25 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

 

  [G5.1] Affordability of initial and ongoing commitments

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report asserts an initial advice fee of £3,305.55 (3% of amount invested) and an ongoing annual charge of £1,101.85 (1% per annum based on current values and expected contributions minus initial fee). Affordability is asserted on the basis that: (1) combined income comfortably meets household expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month; (2) the client holds savings of around £6,000 and no outstanding liabilities; (3) pension funds total approximately £106,000; and (4) the client confirmed potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on day-to-day living.

    File:     The Fact Find (P1) records Total Net Monthly Income of £1,430.00, Total Monthly Expenditure of £1,718.00, and Total Monthly Disposable Income of £-288.00 (a deficit). The planning scenarios (P5, P8) show employment income of £15,600 p/a (£300 weekly) from CDH Recruitment until 2027, plus £1,040 p/a from Sea Cadets (£20 weekly), and Kim's income of £13,200 p/a (£1,100 monthly) until 2026. Existing pension arrangements total £116,998.47 (sum of five plans: People's Pension £6,601.55, Scottish Widows £211.32, Aviva £3,744.36, Zurich £103,439.24, Standard Life £3,002.00). Current liquid savings are £6,000 (P5). Monthly cashflow shows a £288 deficit before any investment contributions. The proposed scenario (P2) projects positive in-year surplus/deficit of approximately £21,000+ annually in the 2040s, sustained through State Pension (£11,973 each from age 66 for both clients).

    Discrepancies:

      - Report asserts household expenditure 'approximately £1,700 per month' (P7); Fact Find records £1,718.00 monthly (P1). Difference of £18 (1.1%).

      - Report asserts combined income 'comfortably meets' household expenditure (P7); Fact Find shows net monthly income £1,430.00 against expenditure £1,718.00, yielding disposable income of £-288.00 (P1). Income does not meet expenditure; there is a £288 monthly deficit.

      - Report asserts pension funds 'totalling approximately £106,000' (P7); five identified arrangements sum to £116,998.47 (P1, P5, P8). Difference of £10,998.47 (9.4%). Additionally, the recommended transfer value stated as £110,185 (P19) differs from both figures.

      - Report states affordability on basis client 'hold savings of around £6,000' (P7); evidence confirms £6,000 savings (P5), consistent. However, affordability assessment does not address how the £288 monthly deficit will be funded or whether the £6,000 savings are sufficient to absorb initial fees and ongoing shortfalls.

      - Initial fee of £3,305.55 represents 55.1% of liquid savings (£6,000), leaving only £2,694.45 in emergency reserves if fee is paid immediately.

      - No evidence that the £1,101.85 annual ongoing charge has been tested against the identified £288 monthly deficit in the affordability assessment.

    The guard states: 'Fees taken from the fund reduce the investment, not the client's income — do not test them against monthly surplus.' This guard applies to the initial fee of £3,305.55, which the extraction correctly notes will be 'paid directly by the pension schemes on transfer' (P7). The initial fee is therefore not a new commitment against current income. However, the ongoing charge of £1,101.85 per annum (approximately £91.82 per month at 1%) is stated to be paid via the Aviva Platform (P22), and its affordability must still be tested. The critical discrepancy is between the report's assertion that combined income 'comfortably meets' expenditure versus the Fact Find's recorded monthly deficit of £288. The report quotes: 'Your combined income comfortably meets your household expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month' (P7). The Fact Find contemporaneously records (P1): net monthly income £1,430.00, monthly expenditure £1,718.00, disposable income £-288.00. This is an internal inconsistency within the same document set. The report's affordability conclusion rests on an inaccurate premise. The expenditure figure in the report (£1,700) is close to the recorded figure (£1,718), differing by only £18, which is immaterial (1.1% variance). However, the assertion that income 'comfortably meets' this expenditure is directly contradicted by the Fact Find, which shows income falls short by £288 per month. The planning scenarios (P2, P6, P9, P11) do show positive surplus/deficit in most years from 2025 onwards (£21,064 to £21,329 annually), but these projections incorporate State Pension from age 66 (P5, P8) and assume retirement in 2026-2027. Current affordability (before State Pension) is not demonstrated. The report does not explain how the current monthly deficit will be addressed. The liquid savings of £6,000, while noted as evidence of financial resilience, are stated to be available for emergency provision (P5). Deduction of the initial fee (if not deducted from transfer proceeds) would consume 55.1% of these reserves. No evidence shows these savings are excluded from emergency fund provision or that they have been explicitly allocated to cover the monthly deficit. The ongoing annual charge of £1,101.85 is approximately 10% of the annual deficit (£288 × 12 = £3,456). This has not been tested in the affordability assessment, though the guard indicates fees are paid from the fund itself, not from income. The core finding is that the report's assertion of affordability is based on a statement (income comfortably meets expenditure) that contradicts the contemporaneous Fact Find record (income falls short of expenditure by £288 monthly). This is a material inconsistency that undermines the suitability conclusion unless it is explained by a constraint not visible in the pack (e.g., expected expenditure changes, income changes, or specific allocation of savings). The planning scenarios do show medium-term sustainability (positive surplus from income sources and accessible pensions in retirement), but do not address the short-term monthly deficit or explain how the recommendation is affordable in the current year. Vulnerability overlay: No vulnerability characteristics are noted in the extraction that would materially affect this assessment.

  [G5.2] Financial situation plus cashflow supports the recommendation

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts: total monthly net income of £4,486.67 (comprising HGV driver income £1,300, Sea Cadets £86.67, State Pension £1,000, and Employment £1,100); total monthly expenditure of £1,700; resulting monthly surplus of £2,786.67; total liquid assets of £6,000 with zero liabilities; total pension transfer value of £116,998.47 (five arrangements).     Discrepancies:

      - Report states total monthly net income £4,486.67; Fact Find shows total net monthly income £1,430.00 — a difference of £3,056.67 or 214% increase not explained by document composition

      - Report lists income components as HGV £1,300, Sea Cadets £86.67, State Pension £1,000, Employment £1,100; Fact Find [P21] shows Employment £1,200, State Pension £230 monthly — State Pension figure differs by £770 (770%) and Employment differs by £100 (8%)

      - Report states monthly surplus of £2,786.67; Fact Find shows monthly disposable income of -£288.00 (deficit not surplus)

      - Report income breakdown sums to £4,486.67 but Fact Find individual items (£1,200 + £230 = £1,430) do not reconcile with report's £1,100 + £1,000 + £1,300 + £86.67 = £3,486.67 in first four items alone

      - Report asserts total monthly expenditure £1,700; Fact Find shows £1,718.00 — differs by £18 (1%)

      - Report states all surplus spent; Fact Find shows actual deficit of £288/month suggesting surplus assumption is not grounded in cashflow inputs

    Critical discrepancies exist between the report's asserted financial position and the Fact Find evidence. The report's income total of £4,486.67 cannot be reconciled with the Fact Find's £1,430.00 monthly income. The income components listed in the report (HGV £1,300, Sea Cadets £86.67, State Pension £1,000, Employment £1,100) do not match the Fact Find which records Employment £1,200 and State Pension £230. The planning scenarios [P12], [P9] show first-year combined household income of £41,813 (£28,613 individual + £13,200 spouse), not the report's claimed figures. The Fact Find explicitly shows a monthly deficit of -£288.00, not the £2,786.67 surplus the report claims. The report's cashflow modelling assumes surplus income is spent, but the Fact Find baseline cashflow data [P22] and proposed scenario [P9] show 'In Year Surplus/Deficit' lines with zeros and modest positive figures, which do not support a consistent £2,786.67 monthly surplus. The State Pension income stated as £1,000/month in the report equates to £12,000/year, whereas the planning scenarios [P12] and Fact Find [P21] state State Pension as £11,973/year or £230/month. Employment income in the report (£1,100 + £1,300 HGV) does not align with Fact Find (£1,200 employment). The discrepancy in income of over £3,000/month is material to affordability assessment. The report's assertion that 'Your state pension income will cover your bills in retirement' [P1] is not supported when expenditure is £1,718 monthly but state pension is only £230 monthly per Fact Find, requiring employment income to meet bills. The planning models show no shortfall but rest on different income assumptions than the Fact Find. This creates inconsistency: either the report misapplied Fact Find figures to its cashflow model, or the Fact Find was not used as the primary input to the model. The Fact Find deficit position contradicts the report's surplus-based analysis fundamentally.

  [G5.3] Emergency fund and liquidity needs, foreseeable and unforeseeable

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   Report asserts emergency fund of £6,000 held and confirmed as sufficient by client. Client stated to have 'confirmed that you retain sufficient liquid capital to cover any unexpected emergencies'. Report also states client 'are comfortable with the concept as you have liquid cash should you require instance access funds.' Access to pension funds in emergencies stated as 'usually take 10-15 working days for administration, disinvestment and banking processes to complete'. No quantified emergency requirement specified in the report.

    File:     Fact Find [P6] and [P14] document cash savings of £6,000.00 held as at 22/09/2025. intelliflo proposed scenario [P7] shows opening liquid assets of £6,000 in 2025, declining to £5,956 in 2026, £5,912 in 2027, continuing downward trajectory to £5,573 by 2035. Baseline scenario [P16] shows identical decline pattern. intelliflo baseline scenario [P22] graph indicates liquid assets declining from approximately £6,000 in 2025 toward lower values throughout the projection period. Fact Find [P14] shows current monthly cash flow deficit: Total Net Monthly Income £1,430.00 versus Total Monthly Expenditure £1,718.00, creating Total Monthly Disposable Income of -£288.00 (deficit of £288 per month). Current arrangement shows monthly surplus/deficit analysis indicating negative cash position absent income changes or expenditure reductions.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report asserts £6,000 emergency fund is 'sufficient' but provides no quantified basis for that sufficiency assessment and no articulated emergency requirement standard against which to measure it.

      - Report states client 'confirmed' sufficiency but Fact Find shows client in current monthly cash flow deficit of -£288.00 per month (income £1,430 vs expenditure £1,718), creating tension with assertion that £6,000 liquid capital is 'sufficient for unexpected emergencies'.

      - Projected cashflow models show liquid assets declining continuously from £6,000 opening balance in 2025 through all future years (£5,956 in 2026, £5,912 in 2027, £5,573 by 2035 per P7 and P16), suggesting the £6,000 reserve is not a preserved emergency buffer but rather part of the working capital being drawn down.

      - Report's assertion of client comfort with emergency access to pension funds (10-15 working days) does not address the gap between that access timeframe and the acute emergency scenarios an emergency fund typically covers.

    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Quantified emergency fund requirement or benchmark (how much is 'sufficient' and on what basis — e.g. months of expenses, specific contingencies), Explicit confirmation whether the £6,000 is dedicated to emergencies or is working capital subject to ongoing expenditure needs, Analysis reconciling the stated sufficiency of £6,000 with the current monthly deficit of -£288 and projected multi-year decline in liquid assets, Documentation of whether client's current negative monthly cash position (expenses exceed income by £288/month) was addressed before determining £6,000 sufficient for emergencies

    The report states the client has confirmed £6,000 is sufficient liquid capital for emergencies and is comfortable with that position. However, comparisonPerformed is false because the comparison cannot be completed: the report asserts sufficiency as a qualitative statement ('confirmed', 'sufficient') but provides no quantified emergency requirement baseline against which to measure it. The guard 'An asserted sufficiency with no quantified requirement cannot be verified' applies directly. Additionally, material inconsistencies emerge when the asserted sufficiency is read against supporting evidence: (1) the Fact Find contemporaneously records a current monthly cash deficit of -£288 (income £1,430 vs expenditure £1,718), creating tension with the assertion that the client has 'sufficient' liquid capital; (2) the projected cashflow models (baseline and proposed scenarios from intelliflo) show the £6,000 liquid asset opening balance declining steadily year-on-year to £5,573 by 2035, suggesting these funds are not a preserved emergency reserve but are being consumed by the plan itself; (3) the report does not address whether this drawdown pattern is compatible with maintaining emergency resilience or whether a separate emergency fund should be carved out and protected. The report's reference to pension fund access (10-15 working days) does not resolve the emergency fund concern, as emergency access to pension investments requires disinvestment and involves delay, market timing risk, and may trigger tax consequences — this is fundamentally different from liquid cash reserves. The absence of a quantified emergency requirement means the claim of sufficiency cannot be tested against evidence. The direction of liquid asset decline over the projection period contradicts any meaningful interpretation that £6,000 is a dedicated emergency buffer.

  [G5.4] Initial and ongoing commitments are unaffordable based on income, expenditure and cashflow

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report asserts financial affordability based on: (1) Initial commitment of £3,305.55 (3% advice fee). (2) Ongoing commitment of £1,101.85 annually (1% per annum). (3) Combined income comfortably meets household expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month. (4) Client holds savings of around £6,000 with no outstanding liabilities. (5) Pension funds total approximately £106,000. (6) Monthly surplus of £2,786.67 derived from net income. (7) Affordability is asserted as true with basis that income comfortably covers expenditure, emergency reserves exist, and underperformance would not impact day-to-day living.

    File:     The case file evidences: (1) Fact Find shows total net monthly income of £1,430.00 with total monthly expenditure of £1,718.00, yielding a monthly disposable income of -£288.00 (a deficit, not surplus). (2) Employment income detailed as £1,200.00 net monthly plus State Pension of £230.00 net monthly = £1,430.00 total. (3) Detailed expenditure breakdown totals £1,718.00 per month (Rent £600, Council Tax £200, Electricity £120, Water £60, Food £300, Car/Travel £150, Building Insurance £25, Entertainment £100, plus other items). (4) Savings of £6,000.00 are corroborated. (5) Pension funds value per fact find: People's Pension £6,601.55, Scottish Widows £211.32, Aviva £3,744.36, Zurich £103,439.24, Standard Life £3,002.00, totalling £116,998.47 (not £106,000). (6) Planning documents (intelliflo) show baseline scenario with total regular expenditure of £24,001 annually (£2,000.08 monthly) and total regular income of £41,813 annually but note projected retirement events. (7) No monthly surplus of £2,786.67 is evidenced anywhere in the file.

    Discrepancies:

      - Monthly surplus claimed as £2,786.67 in report versus fact find showing monthly deficit of -£288.00 (expenditure £1,718 exceeds income £1,430)

      - Pension funds stated as approximately £106,000 in report versus evidenced total of £116,998.47

      - Income stated as 'comfortably meets' £1,700 monthly expenditure versus fact find showing actual expenditure of £1,718.00 per month with net income of only £1,430.00

      - Affordability assessment based on surplus that does not exist in fact find and actual position shows monthly shortfall

      - Initial advice fee of £3,305.55 cannot be verified against evidence (nearest match in passages is £3,385 tax figure)

      - Ongoing commitment of £1,101.85 annually stated versus intelliflo showing £1,200 employment income only (not available for ongoing payments after living expenses)

      - Report states client income 'typically' £300 per week (page 3) but cashflow assumes fixed £300 per week without addressing variability

    The affordability assessment in the report contains material contradictions with the evidenced financial position. The fact find unambiguously establishes that current net monthly income (£1,430) does not meet current monthly expenditure (£1,718), resulting in a monthly deficit of £288, not a surplus. The report's claim of a £2,786.67 monthly surplus is not supported by any document in the file and contradicts the fact find position. The report asserts income 'comfortably meets' expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month, but the actual documented expenditure is £1,718 with income of only £1,430. The report states pension funds total 'approximately £106,000' but the evidenced sum of all five arrangements is £116,998.47, a material overstatement. The ongoing commitment of £1,101.85 annually cannot be afforded from the documented surplus position, as there is no surplus—the client runs a monthly deficit. While the client does hold £6,000 in liquid savings (corroborated), this emergency fund would be consumed by the initial advice fee of £3,305.55 plus ongoing charges, leaving only £2,694.45 of emergency reserves. The guard that 'employment income due to cease within the term should be excluded' is highly material here: the intelliflo planning documents show planned retirement events (client to retire age 66 in 2026, spouse age 71 in 2027, approximately 1-2 years away), meaning the employment income of £1,200 that forms the bulk of current household income will cease imminently. The reported affordability analysis does not account for this. The recommendation thus depends on the client's capacity to fund initial and ongoing charges from either (a) a surplus that does not exist in current cashflow, or (b) drawdown of the £6,000 emergency fund, which would be substantially depleted. The suitability report's affordability conclusions are not supported by the evidenced financial position and contain internal contradictions with the fact find.

  [G5.5] Emergency funds not taken into account, or depleted or unavailable when required

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report asserts that liquidity and access needs are funded from Pension (extracted as /financialPosition/liquidityAndAccessNeeds[]/fundedFrom: 'Pension'). The report does not explicitly state the quantum of the emergency fund, its sufficiency, or whether it will be depleted. The extraction found the path /financialPosition/emergencyFund/shortfall absent, meaning the report is silent on emergency fund shortfall analysis.

    File:     The case file evidences £6,000 in liquid savings (Savings/Cash Account) as the only readily accessible emergency reserve at the start of the plan [P9]. This is held separately from the £116,997 in pension assets. The financial planning scenarios show a Surplus Account starting at £0 [P8, P9, P13]. In the proposed scenario, the Liquid Assets column shows values declining from £6,000 (2025) to £5,956 (2026), £5,912 (2027), and continuing to decline to approximately £5,500 by 2035, before stabilising around £5,400 thereafter through 2038 [P10, P12]. The cashflow shows consistent small annual deficits being met from liquid assets, with surplus income percentage saved set to 0% [P8]. No explicit emergency fund amount is quantified or discussed in the evidence as a distinct reserve separate from the general savings.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report asserts liquidity needs are funded from Pension, but the evidence shows the only accessible liquid capital at plan inception is £6,000 in a Cash Account (Savings), not pension funds [P9].

      - The report does not quantify or explicitly discuss an emergency fund, whereas the evidence shows liquid assets being drawn down annually to cover shortfalls, contradicting an implicit assertion of maintained liquidity reserves.

      - The proposed scenario shows Liquid Assets declining from £6,000 to £5,912 by 2027 and continuing to erode over time [P10, P12], indicating the emergency fund is being spent down, yet the report does not acknowledge this depletion or assess its sufficiency.

    The guard rule states: 'A cashflow that schedules the emergency fund to be spent has not retained it, whatever the report says about retaining liquid capital. Read the liquidation order in the scenario before accepting an assertion of sufficiency.' The proposed scenario [P1, P10, P12] shows that Liquid Assets are being liquidated second (after Surplus Account, which starts at £0), declining year-on-year from £6,000. The In Year Surplus/Deficit column shows £0 is being saved in every year [P1, P12], meaning all surplus is consumed. The cashflow mechanics reveal that the £6,000 cash reserve is being drawn upon to meet annual shortfalls. By 2035, Liquid Assets have declined to approximately £5,573 [P10]. The report's assertion that liquidity needs are 'funded from Pension' is inconsistent with the evidence that the modest liquid reserve is the first non-zero asset to be called upon. The second guard applies: the report has not explicitly addressed whether the emergency fund is sufficient, retained, or depleted. The evidence shows it is being eroded. No document in the case file contains an explicit quantified emergency fund assessment or a statement that the £6,000 is adequate for foreseeable needs. The plan assumes income continues until age 66/71 for retirement, but the employment income depends on continued employment (£300/week 'typically' for HGV driving, £1,100/month for Kim's employment [P1, P5]). Income interruption is not explicitly stress-tested against the liquid reserve. The recommendation involves consolidating five pension plans into one Aviva arrangement [P13], which is a transfer between pension wrappers and does not deplete the emergency fund per the first guard. However, the ongoing erosion of the £6,000 liquid asset base to meet cashflow deficits in the proposed scenario is not addressed in the suitability report's affordability narrative.

  [G5.6] Liquidity mismatch with access needs

  Potential Concern — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report recommends a Personal Pension wrapper with a selected retirement age of 71. The report does not explicitly state any liquidity or access needs that would require capital withdrawal before age 71.

    File:     The case file shows the client is currently 69 years old (born 07/06/1956) per the planning scenario [P12] and [P19]. The client has £6,000 in liquid savings [P12], [P19], [P21]. The planning documents show retirement ages: client retiring at age 71 in 2027 [P12], [P19]. The Aviva Platform terms permit access from age 55 (currently age 57 from April 2028) [P3], [P4]. The People's Pension policy allows access on medical grounds or as a small pot lump sum if under £10,000 [P2]. No explicit access needs or liquidity requirements before age 71 are documented in the file.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report asserts a retirement age of 71 but does not evidence or quantify any liquidity or access needs that would conflict with that age or the Personal Pension wrapper restrictions.

      - The extraction notes that '/financialPosition/liquidityAndAccessNeeds[]/requiredFrom' is absent from the canonical model, meaning the report contains no documented statement of when capital access is required.

      - The file contains financial planning projections [P5], [P12], [P19], [P24] but these do not specify a foreseeable financial need requiring pension capital withdrawal before age 71.

    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: /financialPosition/liquidityAndAccessNeeds[]/requiredFrom - The report does not state or evidence any documented liquidity or access need with a specified date or trigger before age 71. Without this, a comparison between the retirement age (71) and any earlier access requirement cannot be performed.

    The requirement checks whether the recommended Personal Pension wrapper and retirement age (71) create a liquidity mismatch where the client has an evidenced need to access capital before that age. The comparison cannot be completed because the report is silent on liquidity and access needs. The extraction metadata confirms '/financialPosition/liquidityAndAccessNeeds[]/requiredFrom' is absent from the canonical model—this is not an extraction failure but an absence in the document itself. The client is currently 69 years old and the retirement age of 71 is only 2 years away. The Personal Pension wrapper permits flexible access from the minimum pension age (currently 55, rising to 57 from April 2028) [P3], [P4], [P2], so there is no structural lock-in beyond age 55/57. However, without documented access needs in the report, no mismatch can be identified. The file contains no evidence of planned expenditure, foreseeable costs, or liquidity requirements that would necessitate pension withdrawal before age 71. The planning scenario shows a surplus account and liquid savings of £6,000 [P12], [P19], but no statement that these are insufficient or that pension access is required before age 71. The absence of documented access needs in the suitability report means the comparison required by this check cannot be made.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states State Pension startsOn as 2026-03-26 but Fact Find [P6] shows State Pension income from 22/09/2025, a difference of approximately 6 months.

      - Report asserts two State Pension income entries (two items in incomes array with StatePension category) but provides only one startsOn date, creating ambiguity about whether this represents one or two clients, one or two pension streams, or a data capture error.

      - Report extracts no gross State Pension amount despite two StatePension entries; Fact Find [P6] specifies £230.00 monthly from 22/09/2025, equivalent to £2,760 annually, but report canonical model does not capture this figure.

      - Intelliflo planning documents reference State Pension Age 66 and show retirement at age 66 in 2026 for the female client (born 07/05/1960), which aligns with 2026 start date in report, but the precise date 2026-03-26 is not evidenced; female client would reach age 66 on 07/05/2026, not 26/03/2026.

    The comparison can be performed because State Pension entitlement, age and anticipated start date are evidenced in the file. However, material discrepancies exist. The report's startsOn date of 2026-03-26 does not match any State Pension Age trigger in the evidence. [P1] and [P4] establish that one client (KS, female, born 07/05/1960) reaches State Pension Age 66 on 07/05/2026. The date 2026-03-26 (26 March 2026) is approximately 6 weeks before that client's 66th birthday and does not correspond to any documented qualifying event. The Fact Find [P6] shows State Pension income commencing 22/09/2025, which is earlier still and suggests State Pension forecasting has already begun or a pension credit/spouse's pension may have been recorded. The report extracts two State Pension entries but only one startsOn date, and provides no gross amounts despite [P6] specifying £230.00 monthly. The planning documents [P8], [P9], [P10], [P12] include State Pension in cashflow totals but do not isolate the figure, preventing verification that the £230 monthly from Fact Find is the figure used in affordability modelling. Without confirmation that £230 monthly is the actual figure assumed in projections, and given the date discrepancy of 6 months from Fact Find to report, the affordability assessment cannot be verified as based on the correct State Pension entitlement. A vulnerability overlay is noted: file contains no mention of support needs or reasonable adjustments that would affect this assessment, and no vulnerability characteristics that would alter the conclusion about State Pension timing or amount.
