  [G5.8] Planned future expenditure and foreseeable financial needs

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts four foreseeable changes: (1) Client retirement in Oct 2027 with net income loss of £1,386.67/month (loss of HGV employment £1,300/month and Sea Cadets income £86.67/month); (2) Kim's retirement in Mar 2026 with net income loss of £1,100/month from employment; (3) Kim's State Pension commencement in Mar 2026 adding £1,000/month net income; (4) IHT regime change for pensions from 6 Apr 2027 with unquantified financial impact. The report quotes client expectations to retire within two years and Kim to work until age 66. Emergency access to pension drawdown is stated as requiring 10-15 working days for administration, disinvestment and banking.

    File:     Fact Find [P1] states total monthly household expenditure of £1,718 with no expected changes in foreseeable future, and total net monthly income of £1,430 creating disposable income deficit of £288/month. Fact Find [P14] records State Pension entitlement of £230/month and employed basic income at £1,200/month with start date 22/09/2025. File notes [P15] confirm client 'Thinks he'll probably retire in the next 2 years and needs a household income of £1,700 net per month.' intelliflo planning scenarios [P12] show retirement events: Kim retire age 66 in 2026, John retire age 71 in 2027. Baseline and Proposed scenarios [P6][P12] show opening liquid assets of £6,000 (cash savings) plus accessible pension values totalling £116,997. Planning report [P5] shows Summary Cashflow with Required Withdrawal lines indicating drawdowns from pensions and investments where expenditure exceeds income in retirement years.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states employment income loss of £1,300/month on client retirement; Fact Find [P14] shows employed basic income of £1,200/month, a difference of £100/month unaccounted for.

      - Report states State Pension £230/week when Kim reaches State Pension age (derived as £1,000/month net); Fact Find [P14] records State Pension as £230/month, not weekly, creating inconsistency in quantum and basis.

      - Report asserts no change in household expenditure upon retirement; Fact Find [P1] states current expenditure £1,718/month but planning scenarios [P5] show expenditure line changing post-retirement, contradicting the assertion of no expected changes.

      - Report states current monthly disposable income as unspecified; Fact Find [P1] calculates current disposable income as negative £288/month (£1,430 income minus £1,718 expenditure), indicating current affordability constraint not reflected in retirement planning narrative.

      - Report provides IHT regime change event dated 6 Apr 2027 with null financial impact; no evidence in file quantifies or addresses the implications of this change for the pension recommendation.

    The report addresses planned future expenditure and foreseeable financial needs by identifying four material events with income impacts. However, significant inconsistencies between the report's stated figures and the supporting evidence create material uncertainty about affordability. First, the employment income quantum differs by £100/month between report narrative (£1,300 HGV) and Fact Find (£1,200 total employed). The Sea Cadets component (£86.67/month) is not separately itemised in any supporting document, making it impossible to verify this element of the income loss calculation. Second, State Pension is expressed as £230/week in the report quote but recorded as £230/month in the Fact Find [P14], a seven-fold discrepancy that fundamentally changes the income projection from retirement. If the report intends £230/week (approximately £996/month), this is consistent with the derived £1,000/month figure; if the Fact Find is correct at £230/month, the report overstates retirement income by approximately £766/month. Third, the report asserts that household expenditure will not change upon retirement, but the Fact Find [P1] explicitly records current expenditure of £1,718/month and the planning scenarios [P5] model expenditure lines that extend through retirement years without showing the flat continuation the report claims. The current financial position shows a £288/month deficit before retirement (income £1,430 vs expenditure £1,718), yet the affordability narrative in the report does not address how this is currently being funded or how the retirement income projections resolve this structural shortfall. The planning scenarios show Required Withdrawals from pensions and investments [P5], indicating drawdown-based retirement funding, but the suitability report does not detail the sequencing, quantum or sustainability of these withdrawals. The IHT regime change from 6 Apr 2027 is cited but unquantified, and no evidence addresses the report's treatment of this known future tax regime shift. The emergency access provision (10-15 working days for pension drawdown) is stated but not connected to the client's actual liquidity needs post-retirement. The file notes [P15] record the client's retirement income need as £1,700 net/month, which is £18/month above current expenditure of £1,718, but no justification is provided for why this stated need differs marginally from current expenditure while the cashflow analysis shows ongoing deficits requiring pension withdrawals. The conjunction of: (a) current cash position of only £6,000 against current monthly deficit of £288; (b) income quantum discrepancies of £100-£766/month depending on which statement is correct; (c) assertion of unchanged expenditure contradicted by planning model; and (d) unquantified future tax regime change—creates material obscurity about whether the recommendation's affordability has been fully demonstrated from a financial resilience perspective.

CHK-006 — Recommendation Rationale and Suitable Alternatives

Outcome: Potential Concern

 

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 6 raised a concern (G6.1, G6.2, G6.4, G6.5, G6.6, G6.8). Rationale [3] states 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' but derived figure analysis shows Aviva existing charge at 0.50% and recommended Aviva Platform at 0.44%, a difference of 0.06% in favour of the new plan (not more expensive); text contradicts its own arithmetic claim. Rationale [13] states 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' but no supporting charge comparison is provided in evidence for the arrangement this applies to, and the derived figures show charge discrepancies suggesting calculations were performed on wrong arrangements (EA5 rows computed on Zurich £103,439 not SL £3,002). Policy Research document (P4, P8) shows blank cells in 'Which objective/goal is this achieving?' column for Aviva, Zurich, and Scottish Widows recommendations, indicating objectives were not linked to individual arrangement rationales at research stage, yet report extraction shows linkedObjectiveIds present. 14 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

 

  [G6.1] Client-specific rationale for each recommendation, linked to specified goals

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report provides 28 distinct rationale texts across multiple recommendations. The canonical model marks 22 of 28 as isClientSpecific=true and 6 as isClientSpecific=false. Rationales include: (1) workplace scheme rationale linked to OBJ3; (2) small pot tax treatment linked to OBJ3; (3) lower costs (0.06% more expensive) linked to OBJ2 and OBJ3; (4) lifestyling risk drift linked to OBJ2 and OBJ3; (5) greater investment choice linked to OBJ2 and OBJ3; (6) adviser charging facilitation linked to OBJ1 and OBJ2; (7) ongoing monitoring linked implicitly to OBJ2; (8-17) repeated variations of Greater Investment Choice, Adviser Charging Facilitation, and Ongoing Monitoring; (18) portfolio drift control linked to risk profile; (19-28) generic statements about diversification, cost competitiveness, fund performance, CIP review, risk-profiled solutions, and multi-asset fund benefits. The report asserts rationale is present for each recommendation and linked to stated objectives OBJ1, OBJ2, and OBJ3.

    File:     Evidence shows: (P4, P8) Policy Research document records Overall File Strategy stating 'Client has come to us seeking advice because he has a lot of pensions that he doesn't understand how they work and ideally wants to put them all together to make it easier for him to understand and utilise in his retirement. Will be recommending to consolidate where necessary and provide cashflow planning around his retirement.' Alternative Strategies noted as 'Current set up isn't working because it is too much information for him to understand. Wants to simplify things.' Investment Choice Goals listed as 'ATR 5 CIP funds'. (P4, P8) Policy Research for individual arrangements (Aviva, Zurich, People's Pension, Scottish Widows) shows column 'Which objective/ goal is this achieving?' with all cells blank except notation of rationale (Lifestyle, expensive, no adviser servicing). (P10) Suitability Report states 'You have been provided with a copy of our Client Agreement and Important Information about our Investment, Protection and Pension Services... This report is designed to explain how and why my recommendations can help meet these needs and objectives'. (P9, P10) Report recommends switching Aviva pension into new advised Aviva pension £3,744.36 but rationale linking this specific action to a numbered objective is not quoted in evidence. (P7) SIPP research report provides MiFID II cost disclosures showing fund-level charges (HSBC 0.19% total, CT Universal MAP 0.55% total, aggregated 0.37%) but no narrative explaining why these specific funds meet client's risk profile OBJ2 or OBJ3. (P14) Ongoing service costs described as 1% per annum but stated rationale for adviser charging facilitation as advantage of switching is not directly corroborated by cost comparison evidence. (P12) Risk profile report documents agreed risk level process but does not quote specific rationale connecting fund selection to risk level maintenance post-switch. (P5) SIPP research report recommends £110,185 investment but provides no narrative explanation of why this amount or fund allocation meets stated objectives. Evidence contains no passages quoting or paraphrasing rationale texts extracted from the report for 8 of the 28 rationales; no evidence corroborates the claim that 'the plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' (rationale [3]) or 'the plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (rationale [13]); no research document explains why Greater Investment Choice or Adviser Charging Facilitation is suitable for this client's specific circumstances beyond generic template language.

    Discrepancies:

      - Rationale [3] states 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' but derived figure analysis shows Aviva existing charge at 0.50% and recommended Aviva Platform at 0.44%, a difference of 0.06% in favour of the new plan (not more expensive); text contradicts its own arithmetic claim.

      - Rationale [13] states 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' but no supporting charge comparison is provided in evidence for the arrangement this applies to, and the derived figures show charge discrepancies suggesting calculations were performed on wrong arrangements (EA5 rows computed on Zurich £103,439 not SL £3,002).

      - Policy Research document (P4, P8) shows blank cells in 'Which objective/goal is this achieving?' column for Aviva, Zurich, and Scottish Widows recommendations, indicating objectives were not linked to individual arrangement rationales at research stage, yet report extraction shows linkedObjectiveIds present.

      - Rationale texts [19-28] marked isClientSpecific=false in extraction are generic statements ('The charges are competitive', 'The funds have performed well', 'diversification and reduced risk') that are repeated identically or near-identically across multiple recommendations, violating the boilerplate guard.

      - Report asserts adviser charging facilitation as a switching advantage (rationale [6], [11], [17]) but evidence (P4, P8) notes existing arrangements show 'No' or blank for 'Can adviser charging be applied', suggesting existing arrangements did not permit adviser charging; this is not explained in the rationale as a switch benefit requiring explanation of current constraint.

      - Suitability Report (P9, P10) provides initial advice fee of 3% (£3,305.55) and ongoing 1% per annum but does not quote client-specific rationale explaining why fee structure is suitable for this client's means, linking it to OBJ1 or confirming client acceptance; P2 (Fact Find) does not record explicit client agreement to fee levels in the passages provided.

      - Transfer value discrepancy: report recommends £110,185 transfer (P5) but derived figures show existing arrangements total £116,998.47, a gap of £6,813.47 unexplained in any rationale or analysis; no rationale addresses why certain existing arrangements are excluded from consolidation objective OBJ3.

      - Fund allocation reported as HSBC 50% and CT Universal MAP 50% (P7 cost table) but rationale [9] explains Zurich blended allocation (risk 7 + risk 2 due to lack of risk 5 funds available) as a compromise; evidence does not clarify which fund allocation applies to which arrangement or whether both allocations were recommended, creating ambiguity about which rationale applies to which action.

    The extraction identifies 28 rationale texts but marks only 6 as non-client-specific (false). However, applying the boilerplate guard reveals substantial repetition: 'Greater Investment Choice' appears identically in rationale [5] and [8] and [15]; 'Adviser Charging Facilitation' appears in [6], [11], and [17] (three times); 'Ongoing Monitoring and Adjustments' appears in [7], [12], and [18] (three times); 'Enhanced Retirement Flexibility' appears in [10] and [16]; 'Lower Costs' and 'Lifestyling' each appear twice. These repeated bullets violate the guard and should be counted once per unique recommendation, not restated. The extraction marks [19-28] as generic ('provide exposure', 'professionally managed', 'regularly reviewed', 'cost competitive', 'performed well') and flags isClientSpecific=false for these, correctly. However, rationales [1-2] are genuinely client-specific: they name the workplace scheme, the £200 small pot, £170 net estimate, and reference 'your employer' and 'your retirement'. Rationale [3] contains a factual contradiction: it claims Aviva is '0.06% more expensive' when derived figures show 0.50% existing vs 0.44% new, making the new plan 0.06% cheaper. This contradiction between what the report states and what its own numbers show is a severity-highest finding. Evidence (P4, P8) shows Policy Research with blank objective linkages, suggesting contemporary research did not document which objective each recommendation addressed; the report's linkedObjectiveIds may be post-hoc assignment. Rationale [9] concerning Zurich's lack of risk 5 funds necessitating a 7+2 blend is explained but the evidence does not show whether this blend recommendation contradicts the client's agreed risk level or is a defensible workaround. The report asserts alternatives were considered (P4 notes 'Alternative Strategies' section) but provides minimal detail on why alternatives were rejected beyond 'too much information' and 'expensive'. The regulation COBS 9.4.1R requires clear explanation of suitability; the report provides rationale text but evidence does not corroborate that each recommendation was discussed with the client or that client agreed objectives are those documented in the report. Guard on vulnerability overlay: No evidence of vulnerability characteristics is cited in the pack; the overlay does not apply to this finding. The core issue is that rationale is present but generic in bulk, contradicted in specific claims (0.06% cost), unsupported by objective linkage documentation at research stage (P4, P8 blank cells), and lacks evidence of client agreement to the stated objectives or alternatives rejection logic.

  [G6.2] Justification for the particular product, provider, wrapper and investment

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts that Aviva Pension Portfolio (Personal Pension wrapper) is recommended. For provider selection, it lists 8 rationales: Financial strength (A rating, £407bn AUM); Choice (6,900+ funds); Expertise (Aviva Investors award-winning strategies); Value (transparent, competitive charges); Convenience (online tools); Governance (regulatory oversight); Innovation (digital investment); and Lower Costs (lower cost structure vs existing pensions). For investment strategy, it recommends HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc (50%) and CT Universal MAP Balanced C (50%), justified by: risk profile alignment; diversification; blend of active/passive; simplicity; competitive charges; strong performance; CIP membership with quarterly review; and multi-asset fund rationale. The report states the new scheme has lower overall cost structure and greater investment choice than existing arrangements.

    File:     The research documents show: (P1, P9) Aviva Platform was identified through a research process with 4 products shortlisted (Aviva Platform, Elevate/Aberdeen, Aegon ARC, Wealthtime), with no DNA analysis applied and no filters used. (P5) A separate SIPP research identified 1 product from 118 matched (Pension Portfolio - Choice by Aviva). (P10) Fund research shows HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio with ongoing costs of £99.17 (0.18%) and CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund with ongoing costs £159.77 (0.29%), giving total aggregated costs of £406.74 (0.37%). Performance data (P12) shows both funds' discrete returns over multiple years. (P13) Policy research notes for Aviva: 'Lifestyle, expensive, no adviser servicing' as reasons it isn't suitable, yet the same Aviva is recommended as the new solution. For Zurich: 'Expensive, no adviser servicing'. (P14) Pension switch report recommends consolidation to Aviva Pension Portfolio - Choice on Aviva Platform. (P2) Discussion of investment pathways notes they offer 'potentially lower costs; reduced complexity' but were rejected because client requires tailored solution, not generic pathways. (P8) Key Features describe the Platform and Pension Portfolio as flexible SIPP offering consolidation benefits. No direct comparison of Aviva Platform against the 3 other shortlisted platforms (Elevate, ARC, Wealthtime) is evidenced.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report asserts provider selection based on financial strength, choice, expertise, value, convenience, governance, and innovation as absolute strengths. Evidence shows research identified 4 shortlisted platforms (Aviva, Elevate, Aegon ARC, Wealthtime) but no comparative analysis or DNA benchmarking was performed (P1: 'No Platform DNA analysis was carried out'; P9 shows only Aviva's record).

      - Report claims lower costs than existing arrangements. However, P13 notes on existing Aviva arrangement state 'Lifestyle, expensive, no adviser servicing' yet the recommended solution is also Aviva (albeit Pension Portfolio platform variant). The claim of lower costs versus existing Aviva is not explained.

      - Report recommends Aviva despite P13 research noting existing Aviva as 'expensive'. The contradiction between calling existing Aviva expensive and recommending new Aviva is not resolved in rationale.

      - Report lists provider rationales as generic industry-wide attributes (financial strength rating, fund choice quantity, regulatory oversight, innovation) rather than client-specific comparisons explaining why Aviva was chosen over Elevate, Aegon ARC, or Wealthtime, which were equally shortlisted.

      - Investment strategy rationales are partially generic: 'diversification', 'blend of active and passive', 'competitive charges', 'strong performance', 'governance' apply to many multi-asset funds and are not fund-specific justifications. Some rationales (CIP membership, quarterly review) are adviser-specific monitoring claims rather than product-specific features.

    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Comparative analysis between the 4 shortlisted platforms (Aviva Platform vs Elevate vs Aegon ARC vs Wealthtime) showing why Aviva was selected. Research document (P1) shows 4 products shortlisted but states 'No Platform DNA analysis was carried out' and 'No filters were applied', indicating no discriminating analysis was performed., Explanation for the contradiction between P13 research labeling existing Aviva as 'expensive' and the recommendation to switch to new Aviva (Pension Portfolio on Aviva Platform)., Comparative fund analysis explaining why HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio (0.18% ongoing) and CT Universal MAP Balanced (0.29% ongoing, 0.55% total MiFID II cost per P10) were selected over alternatives in the 6,900 fund universe., Investment pathway rejection rationale specific to this client's circumstances, beyond generic statements that client needs tailored solution., Client-specific justification linking provider selection (Aviva Platform) to stated objectives. Objectives referenced are OBJ1, OBJ2, OBJ3 but provider rationales do not name which objectives each provider strength addresses.

    The report presents two classes of justification: generic provider attributes and adviser-facilitation claims. Guard 1 applies: the provider rationales (financial strength, fund choice, expertise, value, convenience, governance, innovation) are absolute marketing-style claims, not comparative statements explaining selection over the 3 other shortlisted platforms. The research evidence (P1, P9) shows Aviva Platform was one of 4 shortlisted with no discriminating analysis applied ('No Platform DNA analysis was carried out', 'No filters were applied'). This means the comparison cannot be shown to have been performed; the evidence reveals shortlisting but not selection reasoning. Guard 2 (panel/CIP membership) partially applies to investment strategy: the report states funds are 'part of our CIP (Centralised Investment Proposition)' regularly reviewed by investment committee, which is legitimate documented-panel justification, corroborated by P2's discussion of CIP monitoring. However, this explains adviser-driven governance, not why these specific two funds (rather than other CIP members) suit this client's risk and objectives. A material contradiction appears in P13: the policy research document evaluates the existing Aviva arrangement as 'Lifestyle, expensive, no adviser servicing' as reasons it is unsuitable, yet the recommended solution is Aviva Pension Portfolio (still Aviva as provider). The report does not explain how switching from an expensive Aviva product to a different Aviva product resolves the cost objection. The fund rationales mix generic quality claims ('competitive charges', 'strong performance', 'diversification') with client-specific framing ('your risk profile', 'your financial goals'). However, no evidence demonstrates that these specific funds were compared against alternatives, or that their costs (0.37% total MiFID II per P10) are competitive relative to other Balanced/risk-rated funds in the 6,900-fund menu. The lower-cost claim versus existing arrangements lacks quantified support: existing Zurich is noted as 0.93% (P13), Aviva existing 0.50%, so new Aviva at 0.44% platform + fund charges would be lower, but this calculation is not explicit in the report's narrative rationale.

  [G6.3] Personalised language linked to individual circumstances

  N/A — severity High

    Report:   The canonical model asserts isClientSpecific across 28 rationale elements, with 22 marked as true and 6 marked as false. The report claims to provide personalised rationale linked to client objectives for the majority of recommendations.

    File:     The suitability report excerpt [P13] states 'This report is designed to explain how and why my recommendations can help meet these needs and objectives - whilst at the same time highlighting any potential disadvantages and risks'. The Fact Find [P5] records a single retirement planning objective: 'Just needs advice helping him organise his pensions in the best place. Wants to consolidate to help him get his head around what he has. Thinks he'll probably retire in the next 2 years and needs a household income of £1,700 net per month. ATR 5.' The Policy Research file [P14] notes 'Client has come to us seeking advice because he has a lot of pensions that he doesn't understand how they work and ideally wants to put them all together to make it easier for him to understand and utilise in his retirement.' The same file's recommendation table [P15] shows recommendations for Aviva (Switch), Zurich (Switch), People's Pension (Retain), and Scottish Widows (Switch), with reasons cited as 'Lifestyle, expensive, no adviser servicing' (Aviva); 'Expensive, no adviser servicing' (Zurich); 'Active workplace pension' (People's Pension). The Suitability Report excerpt [P13] provides only one visible rationale: 'Switch your Aviva pension into a new advised Aviva pension - £3,744.36' with no explanation of why this meets the client's objectives.

    Discrepancies:

      - The canonical model reports 28 rationale elements with 22 marked isClientSpecific=true, but only one recommendation rationale is visible in the suitability report excerpt [P13], making it impossible to verify that 22 distinct personalised rationales exist in the document.

      - Six rationale elements are marked isClientSpecific=false in the canonical model, but no evidence is provided showing which recommendations these are or what non-personalised rationales appear.

      - The Policy Research file [P15] shows recommendation reasons that are generic descriptors (e.g. 'Expensive, no adviser servicing') rather than client-specific rationales linking to the stated objective of consolidation and simplification.

      - The visible recommendation in [P13] ('Switch your Aviva pension into a new advised Aviva pension - £3,744.36') contains no rationale text at all explaining why this switch meets the client's objectives of consolidation and understanding.

    The requirement demands that the suitability report contain personalised, client-specific rationales linked to individual circumstances for each recommendation. A client-specific rationale for, for example, switching Aviva or Zurich pensions would explain why consolidation of those specific plans into an advised Aviva Platform meets this client's specific need for simplification and how the recommended structure supports the £1,700 retirement income goal. The evidence does not support that such rationales appear in the suitability report. The guard on standard regulatory wording is satisfied (none is confused with personalisation). The absence of the full suitability report text prevents confirmation of whether isClientSpecific assertions are accurate. The visible excerpt and working notes suggest personalisation is limited or absent. This is not evidence that the report fails the requirement; it is evidence that the comparison cannot be completed from the pack provided.

  [G6.4] Alternatives considered and reasons for rejection

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The report asserts that four discrete alternatives were considered with client-specific rejection reasons: (1) 'Leave pot to grow until retirement' rejected because encashing is 'preferred to simplify arrangements' (stated as inferred, not client-specific); (2) 'Internal fund switch within Zurich' rejected because client's 'main priority is to consolidate your pensions, gain better control over them, and be well-positioned for retirement with all available drawdown options. Multi-asset funds not available within your risk range and blended funds have not performed as well as Aviva options' (client-specific, linked to OBJ3); (3) 'Stakeholder pension with single multi-asset fund' rejected on cost grounds ('more cost effective than a typical stakeholder pension') and flexibility grounds ('typically do not allow for flexible access and have limited fund ranges'); (4) 'Investment Pathway solutions' rejected as lacking tailoring, not reflecting individual circumstances, requiring ongoing review, and underperforming. The report also documents five options in research: OPT1–OPT3 (listed without rejection reasons), OPT4 (employer scheme, rejected with three reasons including loss of employer contribution and higher costs), and OPT5 (new plan, recommended). For existing arrangements, the report recommends: People's Pension Retain, Scottish Widows Encash, Aviva/Zurich/Standard Life Switch to Aviva Platform.

    File:     The evidence provides: (1) Pension switch report [P1, P3, P5, P10, P14, P19, P20] showing comparative projections (existing vs new solution at retirement age 75 under low/mid/high growth scenarios, with 'Effect of switching' showing projected maturity value decrease ranging from £2,000–£15,000 or 1.6%–10% depending on arrangement); (2) Research notes [P22] indicating Scottish Widows, Aviva, Zurich, and Standard Life were each assessed and discounted with brief reasons ('no servicing and reduced flexibility', 'risk rated 4 does not meet clients ATR', 'no multi asset funds', 'all funds more expensive'); (3) SIPP research report [P7, P8, P11, P12, P15] documenting platform selection (Aviva Platform recommended from shortlist of 4 products: Aviva Platform, Elevate, Aegon Retirement Choices, Wealthtime) with note 'No additional justification text has been entered' [P8]; (4) Suitability report [P6, P16, P18] stating 'Lower Costs', 'Consolidation preference', rationale that recommended solution is 'more suitable', and that stakeholder pensions 'do not allow for flexible access and have limited fund ranges'; (5) Pension switch report summary [P20] stating 'consolidate your existing pensions into a new solution' but showing comparative effect of switching produces projected maturity value decreases of 1.6%–10% at mid/high growth rates [P1, P3, P5, P10, P14, P19].

    Discrepancies:

      - The report claims the recommended solution is 'more cost effective than a typical stakeholder pension' but the pension switch report [P1, P3, P5, P10, P14, P19] shows no stakeholder pension was modelled or compared with actual charges—only a generic statement of maximum stakeholder charges (1.5% for 10 years, then 1%) appears without client-specific cost comparison. The stakeholder alternative is rejected on generic grounds, not on research.

      - The report rejects 'Internal fund switch within Zurich' citing that 'blended funds have not performed as well as Aviva options' but the research notes [P22] state 'The blend of funds have not performed as well as what we are offering on Aviva marginally'—a marginal difference is downplayed in the report as a sufficient reason for rejection without quantifying the performance gap or linking it to client objectives.

      - The SIPP research report [P7, P8] notes 'No additional justification text has been entered' for platform selection, yet the report asserts Aviva Platform was selected on grounds of suitability and cost-effectiveness without documented platform comparison reasoning visible in the evidence.

      - The report lists four alternatives to pension consolidation (Leave, Internal switch, Stakeholder, Investment Pathway) but the research file [P22] documents assessment of four individual existing arrangements (Scottish Widows, Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life), creating a mismatch: the report's 'Internal fund switch within Zurich' is reasoned as rejection of a specific product, not a generic alternative type; similarly, the Aviva and Scottish Widows notes describe why switching to those plans was rejected, not why staying in-situ was rejected.

      - The pension switch report [P1, P3, P5, P10, P14, P19] shows 'Effect of switching' producing decreases in projected maturity value (e.g., '£2,000 or 1.6% decrease' at mid-growth for one arrangement, '£3,000 or 2.1% decrease' for another, up to '£15,000 or 10.0% decrease' for the largest), yet the report's rationale for consolidation emphasizes 'Lower Costs' and 'Consolidation' without addressing this projected shortfall or explaining why accepting lower projected retirement values is in the client's interest.

      - The report states the employer scheme (current workplace) rejection reason includes 'plan is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution with £4.50 annual admin charge' [P18], but the derived figures analysis notes this comparison is unreliable: the charge rows are computed on the wrong plan values (Zurich not Standard Life), making the cost comparison unverifiable.

      - Research notes [P22] state for Scottish Widows 'Discounted as no servicing and reduced flexibility', but the suitability report [P18] additionally cites that 'Scottish Widows does not offer a single multi-asset fund that aligns with your risk rating of 5' and that adviser management is required—these rationales do not appear uniformly or with equal weight across documents.

      - The report's rejection of Investment Pathway solutions (OPT from research) as 'not performing as well as the recommended investment strategy' is stated without quantified performance comparison, comparable periods, or benchmark data in the evidence.

    The requirement tests whether the report provides clear, client-specific explanation of why recommendations meet client's demands and needs, with consideration of relevant alternatives and reasons for rejection. The comparison can be performed because the report makes explicit assertions about alternatives and the evidence contains research assessments and suitability rationales. However, material gaps exist: (1) Generic vs bespoke reasoning: The stakeholder pension alternative is rejected on generic grounds ('typically do not allow for flexible access and have limited fund ranges') without modelling a specific product against the client's circumstances. Research notes confirm no stakeholder was evaluated, only generic maximum charges are cited. (2) Performance claims unsupported: The rejection of Zurich funds and Investment Pathway solutions relies on performance comparisons ('have not performed as well', 'underperformed') stated without quantified evidence, ranking data, or comparable periods in the pack. (3) Cost rationale contradicted by projections: The report emphasizes 'Lower Costs' as a primary rationale for consolidation, yet the pension switch report shows the new solution will deliver 1.6%–10% lower projected maturity value at mid/high growth rates across the arrangements being consolidated. This is an arithmetic fact from the report's own projections, not a disputed interpretation. The report does not reconcile this shortfall with its claim of cost-effectiveness or explain why accepting lower projected retirement funds is consistent with the stated objective of 'be well-positioned for retirement'. (4) Research gaps between platforms and products: The SIPP research report explicitly states 'No additional justification text has been entered' for platform selection, yet the suitability report asserts Aviva Platform was chosen on suitability grounds without showing comparative analysis visible in the pack. (5) Mismatch between alternatives framed and alternatives researched: The report's four categorical alternatives (Leave, Internal Switch, Stakeholder, Pathway) do not align with the research notes, which assess four specific existing arrangements. This creates ambiguity about whether all generic alternatives were considered or only specific product rejections documented. (6) Charge comparison reliability: The cost comparison for the employer scheme rejection is flagged by derived figures as computed on wrong plan values, making the 0.06% figure and £4.50 admin charge basis unverifiable. (7) Client-specificity variance: Some rationales are explicitly marked client-specific and linked to objectives (e.g., Zurich rejection citing consolidation priority, OBJ3), while others are generic (stakeholder charges, investment pathway performance). No vulnerability flags are recorded in the evidence that would materially alter this assessment. The checks against COBS 9.4.1R-9.4.2R and 9A.3.1R-9A.3.3R require that suitability be based on adequate knowledge of the client's circumstances and that the advice be explained with reasons demonstrating it meets the client's objectives. The presence of generic rationales, unsupported performance claims, and unamended research templates alongside a fact (projected maturity decrease) that contradicts the stated cost rationale creates material inconsistency between what the report asserts and what the evidence documents or justifies.

      · Pension switch report - updated.md [G] (P1)

  [G6.5] Every existing arrangement has a stated recommended outcome and a rationale supporting it

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report identifies 5 existing arrangements: (1) People's Pension - Retain; (2) Scottish Widows - Encash; (3) Aviva pension my future focus consolidation - FullTransfer; (4) Zurich Managed Pn AP - FullTransfer; (5) SL at retirement universal (PP 10 year) pension fund - FullTransfer. Each has a stated rationale. People's Pension rationale: 'This is your current workplace scheme, and you and your employer are still contributing to this and will continue to until your retirement' and 'If you were to transfer away, you would miss out on the valuable benefit of your employer's contributions.' Scottish Widows rationale for encashment: 'I would recommend you withdraw the full amount of this pension under the small pots rules and put the money into your savings account. This would not detract from your financial retirement plan' with tax calculation detail. Aviva, Zurich, and Standard Life transfer rationales cite: Lower Costs, Lifestyling risk concerns, Greater Investment Choice, Enhanced Retirement Flexibility, Adviser Charging Facilitation, Ongoing Monitoring and Adjustments, and Multi-asset fund availability.

    File:     The case file contains policy documents for each arrangement. [P3], [P5] and [P21] show a policy research table listing 5 arrangements: Aviva (SP50163762), Zurich (P10026-719-001/DL), Standard Life (D2301334000), Peoples Pension (6197175/PP), and Scottish Widows (ZU4225575). Individual policy documents [P7] for Aviva, [P9] for Zurich, [P4] and [P6] for Standard Life, and [P13] for Peoples Pension confirm these arrangements exist with specified values. [P21] contains a recommendation table stating 'People's Pension - 6197175/PP' has 'Other Considerations: Current workplace scheme' and 'Scottish Widows - ZU4225575' shows 'Retain and Encash'. [P23] explicitly states 'Standard Life - D2301334000' advice as 'Switch to Aviva Platform' with detailed rationales for Lower Costs, Lifestyling concerns, Greater Investment Choice, Enhanced Retirement Flexibility, Adviser Charging Facilitation, and Ongoing Monitoring. No specific evidence document states the Aviva and Zurich transfer recommendations or their rationales verbatim.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report rationale on Standard Life states 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' [P23] but the canonical model extract for Aviva shows 0.44% ongoing charge against Standard Life's 0.18% or 0.26% (depending on row interpretation in derived figures), creating an arithmetic contradiction: 0.18% or 0.26% is not 0.08% more expensive than 0.44%.

      - Report cites 'Lower Costs' as rationale for Standard Life transfer, but [P23] states 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution', which contradicts the rationale that costs are lower in the receiving scheme.

      - Aviva arrangement (EA3) current value £3,744.36 appears in policy document [P7] but the recommendation to 'FullTransfer' lacks explicit textual support in the retrieved passages; the canonical model asserts this action but no passage contains the specific recommendation statement for Aviva's transfer.

      - Zurich arrangement (EA4) current value £103,439.24 appears in policy document [P9] but no passage retrieved contains an explicit recommendation to fully transfer Zurich; the rationales in canonical model reference Zurich's fund limitations but do not appear verbatim in evidence.

    All five existing arrangements have stated recommended outcomes in the canonical model: Retain (People's Pension), Encash (Scottish Widows), and FullTransfer (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life). Each outcome is supported by client-specific rationales in the extraction. However, the evidence base for verification is partial. [P21] and [P23] corroborate the People's Pension retention (workplace scheme, ongoing contributions), Scottish Widows encashment (small pots rules with tax relief potential), and Standard Life transfer (lifestyling concern, investment choice, lower costs framing). The rationales for Aviva and Zurich transfers are present in the canonical model but not retrieved in the searched passages; the file shows policy documents confirming these arrangements exist, but the specific advice to transfer them is asserted by the extraction rather than directly quoted. A critical arithmetic discrepancy emerges: the report claims Standard Life charges are 'lower' in the receiving scheme (Aviva Platform) yet [P23] simultaneously states 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution.' The derived figures show this contradiction: if Standard Life carries 0.18% or 0.26% and Aviva Platform carries 0.44%, Standard Life is not more expensive. This is a material internal inconsistency within the report's own rationale. The report also applies shared boilerplate rationales ('Lower Costs', 'Greater Investment Choice', 'Lifestyling', 'Enhanced Retirement Flexibility', 'Adviser Charging Facilitation', 'Ongoing Monitoring') across multiple different arrangements (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life), reducing specificity to individual arrangement circumstances. The Scottish Widows and People's Pension rationales are sufficiently specific (workplace scheme continuation, employer contributions, small pot tax relief). No vulnerability factors identified in the evidence materially affect this assessment.

  [G6.6] Where a change is suggested, the report states whether it is recommended, who will implement it, and the intended outcome

  Potential Concern — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report makes six recommendations, each with: (1) an action type (Transfer, Encash, Switch, Switch, Switch, Rebalance); (2) a summary describing what is to be switched or encashed; (3) an advised status of 'Recommended' for all six; (4) an intended outcome for each (e.g. 'Consolidate pension arrangements while retaining employer contributions...', 'Withdraw small pension fund under small pots rules...'); and (5) implementation details stating the adviser will implement all six, with a timescale of '10-15 working days' for administration. The report states 'Expected Timescale: 10-15 working days' and lists next steps including completion of beneficiary nomination form, review of cancellation notice (30 days), and acknowledgment of out-of-market risk. For all six recommendations, /recommendations[]/implementation/willAdviserImplement is true and /recommendations[]/implementation/responsibleParty is 'Adviser'.

    File:     The case file contains terms and conditions for the Aviva Platform Personal Pension (P1-P8), a risk profile report showing agreed risk level 5 Balanced (P9, P12, P15), policy information for People's Pension and Zurich (P14, P13), a SIPP research report (P12) detailing the fund allocation (CT Universal MAP Balanced 50%, HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio 50%), a client agreement (P10), and investor experience notes showing no additional notes recorded (P11). The file does not contain a document explicitly stating the adviser's recommendation status, implementation responsibility, or intended outcome for each of the six changes. The risk profile document (P15) states 'we agreed a risk level' and projection details, but does not detail the specific pension switching actions or their rationale. No passage in the file directly addresses whether the adviser recommends each change, who will implement each one, or the intended outcome of each specific action.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report asserts six discrete recommendations each with Recommended status, intended outcome, and adviser implementation responsibility. The file contains no corroborating passages that explicitly state this advice pattern for any of the six recommendations.

      - The report states implementation timescale of '10-15 working days' and lists next steps. The file contains terms and conditions describing how the Aviva platform operates (P1-P8) but does not evidence that these timescales or next steps were communicated in advice or agreed with the client.

      - The report's intended outcome for recommendation 1 is to 'Consolidate pension arrangements while retaining employer contributions and current workplace scheme benefits' but the file does not evidence this specific rationale or outcome.

      - The report's intended outcome for recommendation 2 is to 'Withdraw small pension fund under small pots rules and deposit into savings account' but the file does not evidence this specific outcome or reference to small pots rules.

    The requirement G6.6 limb on Consistency requires that where a change is suggested, the report states whether it is recommended, who will implement it, and the intended outcome. The canonical model extract shows all six recommendations carry the fields /adviceStatus='Recommended', /implementation/willAdviserImplement=true, and /implementation/responsibleParty='Adviser'. These fields are present and consistent in the report's own assertion. The /intendedOutcome field is populated for all six recommendations with specific, differentiated statements of purpose (consolidation, withdrawal under small pots, flexibility improvements, cost reductions, etc.). The /solution/implementation section specifies a timescale ('10-15 working days'), lists next steps (beneficiary nomination, cancellation notice review, out-of-market risk acknowledgment), and includes quotes from the report's pages 8 and 16. The requirement is fundamentally about whether the suitability report itself contains these statements; it is not a requirement that external evidence corroborate them. The method of assessment is PresenceOnly. On this method, the comparison cannot fail on the basis that the file's passages do not repeat or corroborate the report's statements. The file's silence about recommendation status, implementation responsibility, or intended outcome is not evidence that the report omitted these elements. The extraction metadata indicates expectedButAbsent and ambiguities relating to other fields (risk rating, capacity for loss) but not to the /recommendations[] fields assessed here. The report's own canonical model shows these fields are present and populated. On a PresenceOnly method, this constitutes a valid statement by the report that each change is recommended, who will implement it, and what the intended outcome is. The report states these facts internally consistently: all six are Recommended, all will be implemented by the Adviser, all have stated outcomes. There is no internal contradiction. The guard 'An implementation step handled by the provider rather than the adviser is still a stated step; the finding is silence, not who happens to act' does not apply here because the report explicitly names the Adviser as responsible party. The guard 'A change the client instructed and the adviser is merely executing should say so' does not apply because there is no evidence in the file of a client instruction overriding adviser recommendation. The assessment is whether the report itself states these elements, not whether the file corroborates them. The report does state them.

  [G6.8] Recommendations state what action will be taken, when, and by whom where that is material to suitability

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts six recommendations with the following implementation details: (1) Transfer - 10-15 working days, Adviser; (2) Encash - 10-15 working days, Adviser; (3) Switch - 10-15 working days, Adviser; (4) Switch - 10-15 working days, Adviser; (5) Switch - 10-15 working days, Adviser; (6) Rebalance - Adviser. All are attributed to 'Adviser' as responsible party. Overall solution timescale stated as '10-15 working days'. Next steps include: (a) Complete nomination of beneficiaries form 'after the transfer process is begun' (client-specific, page 16); (b) Review cancellation notice with '30 days to review and consider options' after plan setup (boilerplate, page 16); (c) Stock market movements disclosure stating client 'are comfortable with this concept as you have liquid cash should you require instance access to funds' (client-specific, page 8). Out-of-market risk disclosed as true. Implementation and timing are stated at solution level and individual recommendation level.

    File:     Evidence retrieved comprises provider terms and conditions (Aviva Pension Portfolio Terms and Conditions, Key Features), fund factsheets, and planning scenario documents. The Aviva terms state at [P1] that 'If You want to cancel Your regular income payments once they have started, then Your Adviser will need to do so at least 10 working days before Your existing regular income payment date.' Terms at [P5] specify adviser charges are deducted from Payment(s) before investment or from Pension Portfolio depending on type. Terms at [P7] state 'Any instruction to withdraw Your Investment will not take place on a non Business Day and will wait until the next Business Day. This could mean that You may not receive Your withdrawal proceeds straight away.' Terms at [P3] address fund closure: 'We will, where possible, notify Your Adviser or You and request reinvestment instructions 30 days in advance.' The Scottish Widows policy info [P10] notes 'Money4Life Plans (Plans starting with ZU) do not support ongoing advisor charging, the transferring of Servicing Rights or Commission.' No evidence retrieved specifies timescales for transfer execution, implementation responsibility allocation across multiple recommendations, or confirmation of 'Adviser' as the identified responsible party for each discrete action.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states timescale '10-15 working days' applies to recommendations 1-5 (Transfer, Encash, Switch, Switch, Switch) but evidence does not corroborate this specific timescale for these actions; Aviva terms reference '10 working days' only in context of cancelling regular income payments, not for initial transfer or encashment

      - Report identifies 'Adviser' as responsible party for all six recommendations but evidence does not specify adviser responsibility for all discrete actions, particularly for Rebalance or for actions dependent on provider-initiated processes

      - Report states next step requires completion of beneficiaries nomination form 'after the transfer process is begun' but evidence does not specify when this action should occur relative to transfer completion or whether adviser or client is responsible for obtaining and submitting the form

      - Report asserts client 'are comfortable' with out-of-market risk based on possession of liquid cash, but no evidence in the file corroborates that the client was asked about or confirmed comfort with this risk

      - Report at page 8 quotes disclosure of stock market movements but does not cite evidence showing client agreement to bear this risk or that liquid cash holdings were assessed as sufficient buffer

    The report states timescales and responsibility for implementation but the evidence base does not include the suitability report itself or client correspondence confirming these allocations. The Aviva provider terms and conditions ([P1], [P5], [P7]) govern certain operational timescales (e.g., 10 working days for cancelling regular payments, non-Business Day delays) but do not address the adviser's committed timescale for the initial transfer and encashment actions recommended. The Scottish Widows policy info ([P10]) introduces a constraint on ongoing adviser charging for Money4Life Plans (ZU plans), which may affect adviser responsibility allocation but is not cross-referenced in the report's implementation section. The report asserts Adviser responsibility uniformly across all six recommendations, including a Rebalance action, but the evidence does not confirm who is responsible for initiating rebalancing or whether this is delegated to a discretionary investment model (DIM). The next steps regarding beneficiaries nomination and cancellation review are stated in the report, but the evidence does not confirm the timing relative to transfer completion or the allocation of responsibility between adviser and provider. The out-of-market risk disclosure references client comfort based on liquid cash, but no evidence in the retrieved file demonstrates that the client was specifically questioned on, or explicitly agreed to, this risk. The report's language ('You are comfortable with this concept') is assertive but not corroborated by client confirmation in the evidence. These gaps mean the report states what action will be taken and by whom, but the evidence does not systematically corroborate these assertions or demonstrate that they were agreed with the client.

CHK-007 — Costs, Charges and Value of the Recommendation

Outcome: Potential Concern

 

  Potential Concern across 11 requirement(s). 11 raised a concern (G7.1, G7.2, G7.3, G7.4, G7.5, G7.6, G7.7, G7.8, G7.9, G7.10, G7.11). The derived figure analysis reveals the platform charge (0.21% of £231.39) implies a fund value of £110,185.71, and the investment management charge (0.22% of £253.43) implies a fund value of £115,195.45. These imply different fund values, which is arithmetic inconsistency. However, the total ongoing charge stated as 0.44% (£484.81) implies a fund value of £110,184.09. The individual percentage components (0.21% + 0.22%) sum to 0.43%, not 0.44% as stated. The fund charge layer shows HSBC at 0.18% and CT Universal MAP at 0.29%, which when applied to allocations of 50% each would produce a weighted average of 0.235% (0.18% × 0.50 + 0.29% × 0.50), but P8 states weighted average as 0.23%. The aggregated total of platform (0.21%) + investment management (0.22%) + weighted fund charges (0.235%) = 0.655%, which is materially higher than the stated total of 0.44%. This discrepancy is unexplained. The recommended transfer value for calculation purposes is stated as £110,185 in the report (per the derived figures commentary on page 9), but the sum of all five existing arrangements totals £116,998.47. The charges tables appear to be calculated on £110,185, not the actual aggregated value of £116,998.47. This creates uncertainty about which fund value the charges are applied to. 14 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

 

  [G7.1] All material costs disclosed — product, platform, fund, transaction, advice

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   The suitability report discloses: (1) Platform charge 0.21% (£231.39 annually); (2) Investment Management charge 0.22% (£253.43 annually); (3) HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio fund charge 0.18%; (4) CT Universal MAP Balanced C fund charge 0.29%; (5) Total ongoing charge 0.44%; (6) Initial advice fee 3% (£3,305.55); (7) Transaction costs excluded because they are variable and subject to change based on market conditions and trading activity, but are already accounted for in fund performance figures presented net of all charges; (8) Cumulative effect disclosed in personal illustration; (9) Aggregated total disclosed at 0.44%.

    File:     Evidence from multiple sources: (P3) Aviva illustration confirms Platform 0.21% (£231.39), Investment Management 0.22% (£253.43), Total Charge 0.44%, and states 'The charges quoted in this report exclude the transaction costs of the underlying funds, as these are variable and subject to change based on factors such as market conditions and trading activity. Transaction costs are not fees paid to the asset manager; instead, they include expenses like broker commissions, taxes, and the cost of executing trades. Despite their variability, these costs are already accounted for in the fund's performance figures, which are presented net of all charges.' (P1) HSBC KIID shows ongoing charge 0.18%, entry charge 0.00%, exit charge 0.00%. (P8) Illustration shows CT Universal MAP Balanced C Acc at 0.29% and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc at 0.18%, with weighted average charge 0.23%. (P6) Key Features confirms adviser charges are agreed with adviser and shown in illustration. (P17) Suitability report states initial advice fee 3% (£3,305.55) 'as per the terms stated in our Client Agreement' and 'This will be paid directly by the pension schemes on transfer.' Transaction cost exclusion and net-of-charges methodology is confirmed in P3. Fund charge layers appear consistently across documents.

    Discrepancies:

      - The derived figure analysis reveals the platform charge (0.21% of £231.39) implies a fund value of £110,185.71, and the investment management charge (0.22% of £253.43) implies a fund value of £115,195.45. These imply different fund values, which is arithmetic inconsistency. However, the total ongoing charge stated as 0.44% (£484.81) implies a fund value of £110,184.09. The individual percentage components (0.21% + 0.22%) sum to 0.43%, not 0.44% as stated.

      - The fund charge layer shows HSBC at 0.18% and CT Universal MAP at 0.29%, which when applied to allocations of 50% each would produce a weighted average of 0.235% (0.18% × 0.50 + 0.29% × 0.50), but P8 states weighted average as 0.23%. The aggregated total of platform (0.21%) + investment management (0.22%) + weighted fund charges (0.235%) = 0.655%, which is materially higher than the stated total of 0.44%. This discrepancy is unexplained.

      - The recommended transfer value for calculation purposes is stated as £110,185 in the report (per the derived figures commentary on page 9), but the sum of all five existing arrangements totals £116,998.47. The charges tables appear to be calculated on £110,185, not the actual aggregated value of £116,998.47. This creates uncertainty about which fund value the charges are applied to.

    The suitability report discloses all material cost layers: platform (0.21%), investment management (0.22%), fund charges (0.18% and 0.29% for individual funds), initial advice fee (3%), and ongoing adviser remuneration (implied through Client Agreement reference). Transaction costs are explicitly excluded with detailed reasoning aligned across P3 and P17 that they are variable, already accounted for in net-of-charges fund performance, and not separately estimable. This exclusion is supported by evidence and meets regulatory requirements for disclosure of material information. Fund literature (P1, P8) confirms individual charge figures. The report's aggregation and disclosure method is consistent with supporting documents. However, three arithmetic concerns emerge that affect the reliability of the cost comparison: (1) The stated total of 0.44% does not equal the sum of its components (0.21% + 0.22% + weighted fund average ~0.235% ≈ 0.655%). (2) The monetary charges (£231.39 + £253.43 + fund charges) appear to be calculated on a fund value of ~£110,185, which is £6,813.47 less than the actual sum of existing arrangement transfer values (£116,998.47). The report states on page 9 a recommended transfer value of £110,185 but this figure is not independently justified—the individual plans sum to £116,998.47. (3) The weighted average fund charge is stated as 0.23% in P8 but calculation of 50%/50% allocation yields 0.235%. These are rounding-level differences. The first issue—that platform + investment management charges do not sum to the stated total, and that this total is materially lower than what the full set of component percentages would produce—represents a substantive inconsistency. The illustration (P3) repeats the same figures (0.21%, 0.22%, 0.44% total) without explanation of how 0.21% + 0.22% = 0.44%. This internal arithmetic cannot be reconciled. The second issue—uncertainty about the fund value to which charges apply—creates ambiguity about whether the report's comparison of existing vs. recommended charges is made on a like-for-like basis. The evidence does not clarify whether £110,185 or £116,998.47 is the correct basis. All material cost layers are disclosed with supporting evidence. The exclusion of transaction costs is reasoned and documented. However, the aggregation of the stated percentages does not reconcile, and the fund value basis for monetary charge calculations is unclear. These defects affect confidence in the cumulative cost comparison even though individual layers are evidenced.

  [G7.2] Initial and ongoing adviser fees, consistent with the client agreement

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   Initial fee: 3% of amount invested, stated as £3,305.55 with current transfer values, paid directly by pension schemes on transfer. Ongoing fee: 1% per annum of fund value, stated as circa £1,101.85 for the first year, paid via Aviva Platform pension scheme.

    File:     Client Agreement [P12] states 'The fees you are required to pay for our advisory services are set out in the suitability report and any subsequent charges confirmation.' For investments, 'A fee based on the work we do to arrange the initial investment, and a fee based upon any ongoing reviews.' Standard terms [P13] state initial advice on pensions is '3% of the value of the funds that we advise on' with minimum £1,350, and for regular contributions '30% of the first year's payments'. Ongoing service [P3] states 'we charge an on-going annual review management charge of 1%' (with minimum £100 per annum). Suitability report [P9] confirms 'initial advice fee of 3% of the amount invested' and [P5] confirms 'The costs for this service are 1% per annum.'

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states initial fee of £3,305.55 but does not reference the minimum fee of £1,350 stated in the standard terms [P13], creating ambiguity about whether the minimum applies or has been exceeded

      - Report calculates ongoing fee as £1,101.85 based on £110,184.09 implied fund value at 1%, but the derived figures show this fund value does not match any existing arrangement value (total existing arrangements are £116,998.47) and the comparison is therefore made on an unstated and unverified basis

      - The ongoing fee of 1% is stated to apply but the report does not disclose the £100 per annum minimum stated in [P3] and [P13]

    The report asserts an initial fee of 3% and an ongoing fee of 1%, both of which match the percentage rates stated in the client agreement and standard terms [P3], [P12], [P13]. The percentages are consistent. However, there are material concerns about completeness and arithmetic integrity. First, the initial fee amount of £3,305.55 is stated as 'with current transfer values' but the underlying fund value is not disclosed in the suitability report itself. The derived figures show the 3% fee implies a base of £110,185, which does not match the total existing transfer value of £116,998.47. This 6.5% shortfall is material and unexplained in the report. Second, the ongoing fee calculation similarly rests on a £110,184.09 fund value that does not correspond to any stated arrangement value. The report does not disclose or explain why the fee is calculated on this value rather than the total transfer value. Third, the standard terms [P13] establish a minimum initial fee of £1,350 for pension advice; the report does not disclose this floor or confirm it does not apply. Fourth, the ongoing charge is subject to a £100 per annum minimum per [P3]; the report does not state whether this applies. The report satisfies the percentage rates in the agreement but fails to disclose key conditions (minimums), fails to reconcile the fee bases to stated plan values, and does not explain the arithmetic underpinning the monetary amounts. The client agreement [P12] requires confirmation 'by approving them expressly or by written consent', and the report presents the fees as though agreed, but without explaining the basis on which they are calculated, the client cannot meaningfully confirm them. This is a structural gap in the disclosure of fees 'consistent with the client agreement'.

      · IMPORT_2.md [A] (P3)

        "For most investment funds that we transact on your behalf, we charge an on-going annual review management charge of 1% (with a minimum fee of £100 per annum)."

      · Suitability Report Test 1.md [I] (P5)

        "The costs for this service are 1% per annum. Based on current values & expected contributions, minus the initial fee, this would be equal to circa £1,101.85 for the first year."

      · Suitability Report Test 1.md [I] (P9)

        "For researching, recommending and arranging these investments, we charge an initial advice fee of 3% of the amount invested as per the terms stated in our Client Agreement. In monetary terms this is £3,305.55 with current transfer values, this figure may fluctuate depending on transfer values. This will be paid directly by the pension schemes on transfer."

      · TFAS Wealth Client Agreement Privacy.md [A] (P12)

        "The fees you are required to pay for our advisory services are set out in the suitability report and any subsequent charges confirmation."

      · IMPORT_2.md [A] (P13)

        "There will be a charge of 3% of the value of the funds that we advise on. In order to ensure we are able to consistently provide a high standard of professional service, our minimum initial advice fee for Pensions & Investment related advice is £1,350."

 

  [G7.3] Cumulative impact of costs and charges

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts Reduction in Yield (RIY) for the Recommended solution at Low growth 2.1%, Mid growth 2.2%, High growth 2.3%; Critical Yield at Mid growth 4.8% and High growth 7.8%; Effect of switching at Low growth -£10,400 (9.6% decrease), Mid growth -£12,000 (9.4% decrease), High growth -£15,000 (10.0% decrease), all at age 75. These appear on page 18 of the suitability report. The report also states at page 11 that 'Based on a mid-growth rate of 2.94%, including our advice fees, the recommended plan would need to grow by 4.8% per year up to age 75 in order to meet the same value as your current plan.' The suitability report presents a Pension Switch Charges table at page 19 showing Aviva Platform Personal Pension platform charge 0.21% (£231.39) and investment management 0.22% (£253.43), totalling 0.44% (£484.81). An initial adviser charge of £3,305 is stated and an ongoing annual adviser charge of 1.00% is stated.

    File:     Passage [P1] (Suitability Report page 18) presents an identical comparison table showing RIY for Recommended at Low 2.1%, Mid 2.2%, High 2.3%; Critical Yield Mid 4.8%, High 7.8%; Effect of switching Low -£10,400 (9.6%), Mid -£12,000 (9.4%), High -£15,000 (10.0%). Passage [P11] (Suitability Report page 11) states the same critical yield of 4.8% at mid-growth rate of 2.94%. Passage [P19] shows the charge breakdown: Aviva Platform 0.21% (£231.39), Investment Management 0.22% (£253.43), Total 0.44% (£484.81). Passage [P10] (Illustration) shows projected values at age 75: Low growth £97,500, Mid growth £115,000, High growth £135,000. Passage [P3] states illustrative projections at age 75: Low £97,600, Mid £115,000, High £135,000. The figure arithmetic in the canonical model shows: Platform charge 0.21% on £231.39 implies fund value £110,185.71; Investment management 0.22% on £253.43 implies £115,195.45; total 0.44% on £484.81 implies £110,184.09. None of these match existing arrangement values. Passage [P2] shows total existing arrangement value £116,997. The derived figures show total current value is £116,998.47 but the solution charges are calculated on £110,185, a discrepancy of £6,813.47 unexplained.

    Discrepancies:

      - The fund value underlying the charge calculations (£110,185) does not match the total existing arrangement value (£116,998.47); shortfall of £6,813.47 is unexplained

      - Effect of switching amounts (-£10,400, -£12,000, -£15,000) are not arithmetically reconcilable to the underlying fund values used for charge calculations

      - The critical yield figure of 4.8% is stated as required growth to match existing plan value, but the existing plan projected value at mid-growth (£127,000 per [P1]) differs from what would produce this critical yield requirement

      - RIY figures are presented but no explicit working is shown linking these to the stated charges (0.44% ongoing adviser charge + 1.00% annual adviser remuneration)

      - The charges table shows charges calculated on estimated investment amount, but states 'charge in monetary terms shown here may differ from the illustration due to differences in calculation methods', creating ambiguity about which basis supports the RIY

      - Transfer value (£110,185 implied) used for new solution projections differs materially from sum of existing arrangements (£116,998.47); this affects whether the effect of switching percentages are calculated on correct base

    The comparison can be performed because all cited figures and passages are present in the evidence pack. However, material inconsistencies are present. (1) Fund value mismatch: The charge layer calculations in the report (platform 0.21%, investment management 0.22%, totalling 0.44%) are computed on a fund value of £110,185 (derived from the arithmetic: £484.81 ÷ 0.44% = £110,184.09). This value does not correspond to any existing arrangement value or the total of existing arrangements (£116,998.47). The shortfall of £6,813.47 is not explained in the report or supporting documents. (2) Percentage base for effect of switching: The effect of switching percentages (9.6%, 9.4%, 10.0%) are stated as 'of current fund value' but it is unclear whether these are calculated on £110,185 (the transfer value implied by charges) or £116,998.47 (the sum of current values). If calculated on the smaller figure, the percentages would be different. For example, £12,000 on £110,185 = 10.9%; £12,000 on £116,998.47 = 10.3%. Neither matches the 9.4% stated. (3) Critical yield reconciliation: The report states critical yield at mid-growth rate of 4.8% is 'the growth rate required from the new solution to match the projected maturity value of the pension solution' and that the new solution must grow by 4.8% to match the existing plan value. But passage [P1] shows existing plan projects to £127,000 at mid-growth whereas the new solution projects to £115,000; the difference is £12,000. If the new plan (starting at £110,185 after initial adviser charge deduction) grows at 4.8% per annum for ~50 years to age 75, applying the 2.2% RIY drag, the net effective growth would be 2.6%, which would not close a £12,000 initial gap. The working underlying these figures is not explicit in the suitability report. (4) Charge composition ambiguity: The report discloses product, platform, investment and adviser charges separately but does not clearly show how these combine into the RIY figure of 2.2% at mid-growth. Page 19 shows 0.44% ongoing and 1.00% ongoing adviser remuneration, but it is unclear if the 2.2% RIY includes switching costs, initial adviser fees amortised, or both. (5) Real terms and nominal terms distinction: Passage [P1] states values are 'in real terms which mean that they have been adjusted to take into account the effect of inflation' and inflation rate of 2.0% is applied. The illustration [P3] and [P10] also state values are in real terms. However, the report's charge percentages (0.44% ongoing) are typically stated in nominal terms. The reconciliation between nominal charge rates producing a real-terms RIY is not shown. These gaps prevent full verification that the cumulative impact of all disclosed costs is accurately reflected in the stated RIY and critical yield figures.
