  [G7.4] Cost comparison with alternative options

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts on page 11 that 'our calculations show that the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive than your current plans' and states 'This means that your proposed plan can underperform your current plan by £264 in the first year (in monetary terms).' The Pension Switch Charges table on page 11 breaks down the impact by arrangement: EA3 (Aviva existing) shows 0.50% existing charge (£18.72) with -0.06% impact (£-2.25); EA4 (Zurich) shows 0.93% existing charge (£961.98) with -0.49% impact (£-506.85); EA5 (Standard Life) shows 0.18% existing charge (£186.19) with +0.26% impact (£268.94). The recommendation page 6 states 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' in relation to Aviva, and page 19 states the new plan 'need to outperform your current plan by £4,672 in the first year (in monetary terms).' The new solution shown on page 11 is Aviva Platform Personal Pension at 0.44% total charge (0.21% platform + 0.22% investment management = £484.81 annually).

    File:     Page 5 Existing Arrangements table shows: People's Pension 0.50% fund + £4.50 annual admin; Scottish Widows 0.19% fund + 0.10% platform; Aviva pension my future focus consolidation 0.50% fund charge; Zurich Managed Pn AP 0.18% fund + £2.12 per month admin (stated as 0.93% in comparison table, implying total of 0.18% + 0.75% monthly admin equivalent); Standard Life 0.52% fund charge. Page 11 Pension Switch Charges table states comparison basis as 'Pension Switch Charges Only Assessment' and shows the three arrangements being switched. Page 3 shows Aviva Platform Personal Pension recommended with 0.21% platform and 0.22% investment management. Page 24 (SIPP research report) shows CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund with total aggregated fund costs of 0.37%, composed of ongoing costs 0.24%, transaction costs 0.13%. Derivation check confirms: 0.21% platform on £110,185 = £231.39; 0.22% investment management on £110,185 = £253.43; total 0.44% = £484.81 annually. However, the sum of existing arrangement values is £116,998.47, not £110,185, creating a 5.9% discrepancy in the fund value basis for the calculated charges.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states 0.24% cost saving overall, but figure is not independently matched in passages (nearest corroboration is 0.24% in P24 relating to fund ongoing costs, not the plan comparison).

      - Report asserts -0.06% impact for EA3 but computed impact of -0.06% on £3,744.36 yields -£2.25, which matches the stated figure; however, the report page 6 states Aviva 'is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' which directly contradicts the -0.06% improvement shown in the comparison table.

      - Report shows EA4 (Zurich) existing charge at 0.93%, but the charge schedule on page 5 lists only 0.18% fund charge + £2.12 per month admin (equivalent to ~0.03% on £103,439), totalling approximately 0.21%, not 0.93%. The 0.93% figure appears to be arithmetically derived from the stated £961.98 annual charge (£961.98 / £103,439.24 = 0.93%), but this disagrees with the provider documentation which shows 0.18% fund only.

      - Report shows EA5 (Standard Life) existing charge at 0.18%, but page 5 provider documentation states Standard Life at 0.52% fund charge. The 0.18% in the comparison table does not match.

      - Report states EA5 impact of +0.26% (£268.94), but Standard Life is valued at £3,002. Applying 0.26% to £3,002 yields £7.81, not £268.94. The £268.94 figure, when divided by 0.26%, yields £103,438.46 — the value of Zurich (EA4), not Standard Life (EA5). This indicates the EA5 row is computed on the wrong plan's value.

      - Report states overall comparison of -0.24% on basis of £110,185, but total existing arrangements sum to £116,998.47. The £110,185 figure matches neither the individual plan values nor the sum, and the basis for selecting this denominator is unexplained.

      - Report states in comparison table that arrangements being switched are EA3, EA4, EA5 (three arrangements), but page 5 shows five existing arrangements. People's Pension (EA1, £6,601.55) and Scottish Widows (EA2, £211.32) are not included in the cost comparison calculation, despite being existing arrangements.

      - Report page 19 states 'need to outperform your current plan by £4,672 in the first year' but page 11 comparison table shows total impact of £264 positive (cheaper). These two figures are irreconcilable: £4,672 outperformance required contradicts the claim of £264 annual savings.

      - Charges arithmetic check: Platform charge 0.21% on £110,185 = £231.39 (stated as £231.39, consistent); Investment management 0.22% on £110,185 = £253.43 (stated as £253.43, consistent); but £110,185 is not reconcilable to any single existing arrangement or their sum (£116,998.47).

    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Reconciliation of fund value basis: the report's cost comparison uses £110,185 as the implied fund value denominator (derived from stated charges of 0.21% × £110,185 = £231.39), but this does not match the current value of any single arrangement (£6,601.55, £211.32, £3,744.36, £103,439.24, or £3,002), nor the sum of those being switched (£110,798.60 for EA3+EA4+EA5 per page 5), nor the total of all arrangements (£116,998.47). The basis for the £110,185 figure is not explained., Clarification of arrangement scope: page 5 shows five existing arrangements; the comparison table on page 11 addresses only three (EA3, EA4, EA5). The report does not explain why People's Pension and Scottish Widows charges are excluded from the cost comparison despite being existing arrangements., Provider documentation for Zurich total charge: page 5 states Zurich at 0.18% fund + £2.12 per month admin, but the comparison table asserts 0.93%. The composition of the 0.93% is not documented. Page 2 of Zurich policy states '¾% per year' (0.75%) annual management charge, which could account for part of the difference, but full reconciliation is absent., Provider documentation for Standard Life: page 5 states Standard Life at 0.52% fund charge, but the comparison table uses 0.18%, and the impact calculation on 0.26% uses Zurich's fund value (£103,438.46) not Standard Life's (£3,002). This indicates a data error that renders the EA5 row unreliable.

    The cost comparison cannot be reliably performed because three material inputs are missing or irreconcilable: (1) The fund value basis of £110,185 is not matched to any document or calculation in the case file. It is neither the sum of the arrangements being switched (£110,798.60) nor the total of all arrangements (£116,998.47) nor any individual arrangement value. (2) The comparison excludes two of five existing arrangements (People's Pension and Scottish Widows) without explanation, making the 'like-for-like' basis claim unsupported. (3) Existing charge figures in the comparison table do not reconcile with provider documentation: Zurich is stated at 0.93% in the table but documented as 0.18% fund only on page 5 and page 2 indicates a 0.75% annual management charge component not fully explained; Standard Life is stated as 0.18% in the table but documented as 0.52% on page 5. Additionally, the EA5 (Standard Life) impact row is arithmetically computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,438.46 / 0.26% = £268.94) not Standard Life's value (£3,002), rendering that row unreliable. The internal contradiction between page 11's claim of -£264 annual savings and page 19's statement that the plan 'need to outperform your current plan by £4,672 in the first year' is irreconcilable without clarification of whether the £4,672 includes adviser fees or other costs not shown in the comparison table. The report's statement on page 6 that 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' directly contradicts the comparison table's -0.06% impact for EA3, indicating internal inconsistency. Without reliable fund value basis, complete arrangement scope, and reconciled charge figures from provider documentation, the comparison cannot be verified as arithmetically sound or supported by evidence.

  [G7.5] Benefit versus cost — fair value

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts seven material benefits to justify the recommendation: (1) Lower overall cost structure compared to existing pension, meaning more savings remain invested for growth [P1, page 7]; (2) Lifestyling funds automatically reduce investment risk as approaching retirement, eventually moving portfolio outside agreed risk range [P1, page 7]; (3) Greater Investment Choice allowing more tailored strategy suited to risk profile with scope for future adjustments [P1, page 7]; (4) Adviser Charging Facilitation allowing direct deduction of charges from plan without separate payments [P1, page 7]; (5) Ongoing Monitoring and Adjustments by adviser to ensure alignment with changing needs and market conditions [P1, page 7]; (6) Multi-asset fund selection providing professionally managed diversified investment solution aligned with financial goals and risk profile [P1, page 12]; (7) Enhanced Retirement Flexibility providing more flexible retirement options for tax-efficient withdrawal strategies [P1, page 7]. The report states recommended Aviva advised funds have demonstrated stronger performance compared to existing Aviva holdings [P1, page 13], and that Aviva platform funds align with agreed risk tolerance and have delivered comparable performance with reduced volatility [P1, page 13]. The report claims based on past performance of recommended funds, the plan is believed achievable to outperform by critical yield of 4.8% per year to age 75 [P1, page 11], stated as 'I do believe that this is achievable' [P1, page 13].

    File:     Evidence shows: (1) Fund recommendation table [P6] lists HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc at 50% with 0.18% annual charge and CT Universal MAP Balanced C at 50% with 0.29% annual charge. Research report [P3] confirms these funds' performance data. (2) Existing Aviva arrangement shows 0.50% fund charge [P1, page 5 table]. Performance comparison chart [P1, page 13] shows Aviva Risk 5 portfolio (B) achieved 37.25% over 3 years and 15.58% over 5 years as at 13/10/2025, versus existing Aviva Pension My Future Focus Consolidation (A) at 22.89% over 3 years and 15.58% over 5 years. (3) Zurich Managed Pn AP (D) achieved 34.56% over 3 years and 44.32% over 5 years, outperforming recommended Aviva Risk 5 portfolio (B) which achieved 37.25% over 3 years and 15.58% over 5 years. (4) Report states 'However, they have not outperformed your current Zurich funds' [P1, page 13]. (5) Volatility analysis [P1, page 13] shows Aviva Risk 5 portfolio (B) volatility of 7.56% versus Zurich Managed Pn AP (A) volatility of 7.89%. (6) Discrete performance table for HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio shows 2024 return of 10.82% and YTD 7.7% [P3, P5]. CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund shows 2024 return of 11.27% and YTD 8.04% [P3, P5]. (7) Fact Find [P10, P11] shows client net monthly income £1,430 against monthly expenditure £1,718, creating deficit of £288 [P14]. (8) Intelliflo proposed scenario [P23] lists People's Pension at £6,601, Scottish Widows at £211, Aviva at £3,744.36, Zurich at £103,439.24, Standard Life at £3,002. (9) Report page 5 table [P1] shows five existing arrangements totalling £116,998.47. Report page 9 shows recommended transfer value of £110,185. Shortfall of £6,813.47 is unexplained.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report asserts 'Lower Costs' as key benefit but existing Aviva charge is 0.50% and recommended Aviva Platform shows 0.44% combined charge (0.21% platform + 0.22% investment management per charge arithmetic). Text on page 6 states 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' which contradicts the stated cost saving benefit.

      - Report claims recommended Aviva advised funds 'have demonstrated stronger performance compared to your existing Aviva holdings' [P1, page 13] but cumulative performance at 13/10/2025 shows Aviva Risk 5 portfolio (B) and existing Aviva Pension My Future Focus Consolidation (A) both at 15.58% over 5 years (identical, not stronger).

      - Report states recommended funds 'have not outperformed your current Zurich funds' [P1, page 13], yet recommends consolidating Zurich (£103,439.24) into lower-performing Aviva. Zurich achieved 44.32% over 5 years versus recommended Aviva at 15.58%.

      - Report claims benefit of outperformance at critical yield of 4.8% per year to age 75 [P1, page 11] as 'achievable' based on 'past performance of your recommended funds' but provides no performance data reconciling to this specific rate. Charge arithmetic implies fund value of £110,188.68 at 4.24% but no arrangement equals this amount.

      - Report transfer value shows £110,185 on page 9 but sum of five existing arrangements equals £116,998.47 (£6,813.47 shortfall unexplained).

      - Report page 5 table shows Zurich arrangement with 0.18% fund charge plus £2.12 per month admin, but charge arithmetic for EA5 and EA5 impact rows compute on Zurich Managed Pn AP (£103,438.46) not on SL at retirement universal pension (£3,002), creating unreliable comparison basis.

    The report's core claim of 'Lower Costs' as a benefit is contradicted by its own charge figures. The recommended Aviva platform combined charge of 0.44% is lower than the existing Aviva 0.50%, supporting a modest cost saving only for that arrangement. However, the consolidation includes Zurich at 0.93% (£961.98 on £103,438.71), Scottish Widows at 0.29%, and Aviva at 0.50%, with a blended existing charge substantially higher than the flat recommended 0.44%. The report's statement that 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' directly contradicts the stated benefit. The performance justification fails on two grounds: (1) the recommended Aviva Risk 5 funds matched (not exceeded) the existing Aviva Pension My Future Focus performance at 15.58% over 5 years, contradicting the claim of 'stronger performance'; (2) the Zurich Managed Pn AP at 44.32% over 5 years substantially outperformed both, yet consolidation into lower-performing Aviva is recommended. The report acknowledges this: 'However, they have not outperformed your current Zurich funds.' The critical yield assertion of 4.8% annually is stated as 'achievable' without supporting analysis. The passage cited [P1, page 13] contains only the statement 'Based on the past performance of your recommended funds, I do believe that this is achievable' — a claim unsupported by performance reconciliation to the 4.8% rate. Per the guard 'An assertion that required outperformance is achievable is a claim, not an analysis; require supporting performance data before accepting it.' The transfer value discrepancy (£110,185 recommended vs £116,998.47 sum of existing) is material and unexplained, suggesting either missing funds or undisclosed reductions. The charge arithmetic for EA5 rows uses Zurich Managed Pn AP fund value (£103,438.46) rather than the SL fund value (£3,002), making any comparison resting on those rows unreliable. The client's financial position [P14] shows monthly deficit of £288, yet the report does not address how ongoing adviser charging (implied by 'Adviser Charging Facilitation' benefit) will be funded from this negative cashflow. Vulnerability: The client is an HGV driver earning £300 per week net, with variable income noted as 'typically' [P1, page 3] not deterministic. This income variability is not reflected in the suitability analysis, and ongoing adviser fees on a volatile income base are not addressed.

  [G7.6] Costs are excessive relative to the benefits provided

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The report makes two assertions about costs: (1) the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than current plans when adviser fees are included, stated on page 11 as 'our calculations showed that the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than your current plans'; (2) the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive than current plans when considering annual recurring charges only, also stated on page 11 as 'our calculations show that the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive than your current plans'. The report frames the 4.24% figure as the 'required outperformance' needed to justify the recommendation despite higher all-in costs.

    File:     The case file contains no independent evidence passages to corroborate or contradict these figures, as the comparison is internal to the report itself. However, the derived figures computed from the report's own extracted values reveal critical arithmetic problems: (1) The solution total ongoing charge of £484.81 at 0.44% implies a fund value of £110,184.09, but the actual total transfer value across all five arrangements is £116,998.47 — a mismatch of £6,814.38. (2) EA5 existing annual charge of £186.19 at 0.18% is computed on Zurich Managed Pn AP's value (£103,438.89) rather than Standard Life's value (£3,002.00), making this row unreliable. (3) EA5 impact charge of £268.94 at 0.26% is similarly computed on the wrong arrangement. (4) The required outperformance figure of 4.24% at £4,672.00 implies a fund value of £110,188.68, which does not match any stated arrangement value or total.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states ongoing annual recurring charges are 0.24% less expensive, but derived fund values used in that calculation (£110,184.09) do not align with actual total transfer value of £116,998.47

      - Report states Aviva plan requires 4.24% outperformance to justify recommendation, but this figure is calculated against an implied fund value of £110,188.68 which does not match any stated arrangement value

      - Report's charge comparison appears to use selective or incorrect fund values for one or more arrangements, particularly Standard Life (EA5) which should have £3,002 current value but rows are calculated against £103,438.46-£103,438.89

    The report makes internal comparative assertions about costs and required outperformance. However, the arithmetic underlying these assertions is unreliable. The derived figures reveal that the charge calculations in the recommendation table operate on implied fund values that do not correspond to the stated transfer value total or individual arrangement values. Most critically: (1) The ongoing charge calculation (0.44% = £484.81) implies a fund value of £110,185, which is £6,814 less than the stated total transfer value of £116,999. This means either the charge figure, the transfer value, or both are internally inconsistent. (2) The Standard Life arrangement (EA5) with a current value of £3,002 is treated in charge rows as if it were worth £103,438, suggesting computational error in at least two rows. (3) The 4.24% required outperformance figure cannot be verified because its calculation basis (fund value of £110,189) is not independently validated. The report's comparative cost assertions therefore rest on calculations that appear arithmetically or logically impossible when tested against the report's own stated values. The discrepancy between the implied fund value in charge calculations (£110,185) and the actual total transfer value (£116,999) is material and suggests either: charges are understated (if applied to only part of the fund), or the transfer value stated elsewhere in the report is incorrect. Either way, the cost comparison cannot be relied upon. Additionally, the report does not substantively address benefits justifying the 4.24% higher all-in cost — it merely asserts the required outperformance as a mathematical fact without explaining what benefits the client receives in exchange for bearing that cost premium.

 

  [G7.7] Alternatives provide better cost or benefit to the client

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report asserts lower costs as a primary reason for switching to Aviva Platform. Page 6 states 'Lower Costs - The receiving scheme has a lower overall cost structure compared to your existing pension' and 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' for Standard Life. For Zurich, the report states 'Lower Costs' but then on page 10 states 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' which appears to contradict the lower cost claim. The report does not provide a systematic cost comparison table showing all alternatives considered with their respective costs. The canonical model contains no value at any path for alternativesConsidered/comparativeCost.

    File:     The pension switch report (P1, P2, P3) provides detailed comparisons but focuses primarily on three specific existing schemes (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life) being switched to Aviva Platform. P2 shows a comparison table with maturity values: New solution Aviva Platform at 0.6% (£3,430 projected), Retained Aviva at 0.59% (£4,280), Retained Zurich at 0.29% (£120,000), totalling £127,000. P8 shows the new Aviva Platform charge structure: 0.21% platform (£231.39) + 0.22% investment management (£253.43) = 0.44% total (£484.81). P10 states for Standard Life 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution'. File notes (P18) indicate alternatives were considered: Scottish Widows (discounted for no servicing and reduced flexibility), alternative Aviva fund (discounted as only risk-rated 4 available, not meeting risk 5 requirement), Zurich alternative (discounted as no multi-asset funds in risk range and performance marginally worse), and Standard Life (discounted as funds within 20-60% equity range more expensive than Aviva). However, no cost figures or comparative cost table for rejected alternatives is provided.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report claims 'Lower Costs' for Standard Life switch but states 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' which contradicts the lower cost assertion

      - Report provides no aggregated comparative cost table showing all alternatives considered with their respective charge rates or annual costs

      - Report cites 'Lower Costs' as primary benefit but does not quantify cost savings in monetary terms or as a percentage comparison across alternatives

      - The charge figure for Aviva Platform (0.44% total) is stated but no comparative figures given for Scottish Widows, alternative Aviva, or Zurich alternatives that were rejected

      - File notes indicate alternatives were rejected for non-cost reasons (fund range, flexibility, performance) but no cost comparison is shown to demonstrate whether cost was or was not a factor in those rejections

    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Comparative cost figures for all alternatives considered (Scottish Widows, alternative Aviva fund, alternative Zurich fund options), Aggregated all-in-cost comparison table showing existing arrangements versus new solution and any rejected alternatives, Monetary cost impact calculations (annual savings or additional costs in £) for each alternative considered, Cost comparison for Standard Life alternative (only narrative statement '0.08% more expensive' provided, no supporting table or calculation), Evidence of whether cost comparisons were performed for alternatives rejected on non-cost grounds

    The report asserts 'Lower Costs' as a key benefit of switching but the evidence does not support a comprehensive cost comparison of alternatives. The canonical model extraction confirms that no value exists at the path for alternativesConsidered/comparativeCost, meaning the report did not extract or present comparative cost data for alternatives. The suitability report (P7, P10, P11) repeatedly cites 'Lower Costs' as a switching reason but quantifies this only for Standard Life (0.08% more expensive) without a supporting comparison table. The pension switch report (P1, P2, P3, P8) provides detailed charges for the new solution (0.44% at £484.81 annually) and some existing scheme charges but does not present a side-by-side cost comparison table for rejected alternatives. File notes (P18) explain why alternatives were rejected (Scottish Widows: no servicing; Zurich: fund range and performance; Standard Life: fund costs; alternative Aviva: risk rating mismatch) but these are non-cost justifications. The critical gap is the absence of a comparative cost analysis table or aggregated comparison showing the client why the chosen solution is cost-advantaged relative to realistic alternatives. The charge arithmetic from the derived figures shows the new solution charge is calculated at 0.44%, but no alternative cost figures are provided to validate the 'lower cost' claim. The report's assertion of lower costs lacks the supporting comparative evidence required by COBS 6.1ZA and the requirement specification. The comparative cost research that would typically support such a finding is absent from the evidence pack, and the extraction itself confirms no comparative cost data was captured from the report.

  [G7.8] Reduction in yield, critical yield and cumulative cost illustrations

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The report asserts six RIY and critical yield figures across three growth scenarios: (1) Low growth (0.00%): RIY 2.1%, Critical Yield 1.9%, Effect of switching -£10,400 (9.6% decrease); (2) Mid growth (2.95%): RIY 2.2%, Critical Yield 4.8%, Effect of switching -£12,000 (9.4% decrease); (3) High growth (5.88%): RIY 2.3%, Critical Yield 7.8%, Effect of switching -£15,000 (10.0% decrease). For existing arrangements, RIY is uniformly stated as 0.3% across all three growth scenarios. The report also states: 'Based on a mid-growth rate of 2.94%, including our advice fees, the recommended plan would need to grow by 4.8% per year up to age 75' and 'need to outperform your current plan by £4,672 in the first year (in monetary terms)'. Retirement age is 75. All figures are stated on page 18 of the suitability report except the critical yield mid-growth explanation which appears on page 11.

    File:     The evidence pack contains two distinct pension switch report tables ([P2] and [P1]). [P2] shows: Low growth RIY 0.3% (existing) vs 2.1% (new solution), Critical Yield 1.9%, Effect of switching -£10,400 (9.6% decrease); Mid growth RIY 0.3% vs 2.2%, Critical Yield 4.8%, Effect of switching -£12,000 (9.4% decrease); High growth RIY 0.3% vs 2.3%, Critical Yield 7.8%, Effect of switching -£15,000 (10.0% decrease). [P1] shows materially different figures: Low growth RIY 0.3% vs 0.6%, Critical Yield 0.3%, Effect of switching -£2,000 (1.9% decrease); Mid growth RIY 0.3% vs 0.6%, Critical Yield 3.2%, Effect of switching -£2,000 (1.6% decrease); High growth RIY 0.3% vs 0.6%, Critical Yield 3.8%, Effect of switching -£3,000 (2.0% decrease). Illustration [P13] from Aviva shows cumulative impact: at age 75, before charges £129,000, after all charges £115,000, representing a RIY of 2.1% (reduction from 2.9% growth to 0.8%). The suitability report text on page 16 quotes 2.94% mid-growth basis and 4.8% critical yield. Passages [P4], [P6], [P8] show alternative pension comparisons with different RIY and critical yield figures (0.6% RIY, critical yields ranging 0.3% to 6.0%). The fund values used to compute these yields vary: [P2] implies £110,185 fund value at switching; [P1] implies a different base. The report's stated fund value at point of switching is £110,185.60 ([P7]).

    Discrepancies:

      - Report asserts RIY for recommended plan at Low growth: 2.1%; [P1] table shows RIY 0.6% for new solution at Low growth rate (0.00%), a difference of 1.5 percentage points.

      - Report asserts RIY for recommended plan at Mid growth: 2.2%; [P1] table shows RIY 0.6% for new solution at Mid growth rate (2.95%), a difference of 1.6 percentage points.

      - Report asserts RIY for recommended plan at High growth: 2.3%; [P1] table shows RIY 0.6% for new solution at High growth rate (5.88%), a difference of 1.7 percentage points.

      - Report asserts Critical Yield Mid growth: 4.8%; [P1] table shows Critical Yield 3.2% at Mid growth, a difference of 1.6 percentage points.

      - Report asserts Critical Yield High growth: 7.8%; [P1] table shows Critical Yield 3.8% at High growth, a difference of 4.0 percentage points.

      - Report asserts Effect of switching Low growth: -£10,400; [P1] table shows -£2,000, a difference of £8,400.

      - Report asserts Effect of switching Mid growth: -£12,000; [P1] table shows -£2,000, a difference of £10,000.

      - Report asserts Effect of switching High growth: -£15,000; [P1] table shows -£3,000, a difference of £12,000.

      - Two different pension switch report tables exist in evidence ([P1] and [P2]) presenting conflicting RIY, critical yield and switching effect figures for identical growth scenarios applied to the same pension portfolio, suggesting either different calculation methodologies, different switch analyses, or an updated vs superseded version.

      - Report page 18 table figures align with [P2] table exactly; [P1] table presents substantially lower RIY and switching costs across all scenarios.

    The suitability report's RIY, critical yield and switching effect figures on page 18 are fully corroborated by the detailed pension switch report table in [P2]. Every figure stated — RIY 2.1%, 2.2%, 2.3%; Critical Yields 1.9%, 4.8%, 7.8%; Effects of switching -£10,400, -£12,000, -£15,000 — matches [P2] exactly. The illustration [P13] provides independent corroboration of the 2.1% RIY on the recommended solution using cumulative charge impact (from 2.9% growth to 0.8% growth = 2.1% reduction), though this illustration applies a different growth rate assumption (2.9%) than the pension switch report scenarios. The report's critical yield statement on page 11 (4.8% at mid-growth of 2.94%) is also corroborated by [P16], which quotes the same basis and figure. However, a second pension switch report table [P1] exists in the case file presenting materially different figures for identical scenarios: RIY of only 0.6% for the recommended plan across all growth rates, critical yields of 0.3%, 3.2%, and 3.8%, and substantially lower switching costs (-£2,000, -£2,000, -£3,000). This creates a critical inconsistency within the evidence pack itself. The pack contains no metadata or explanation indicating that [P1] is a superseded version, an alternative analysis, or applies to a different portfolio subset. The existence of two irreconcilable switch reports, both purporting to analyze the same pension consolidation, means the comparison cannot establish internal consistency of the suitability report. The report relies entirely on [P2] figures; [P1] contradicts them fundamentally. The report does not acknowledge [P1], does not explain why one analysis was selected over the other, and does not reconcile the £8,400–£12,000 difference in switching costs. This is not a minor rounding difference: at Low growth (0.00%), [P2] shows a switching cost of £10,400 while [P1] shows £2,000—an 80% difference in the stated cost of the advice. For a client with total existing pension assets of £110,185, this represents a material variance in the represented value and cost impact of the recommendation.

  [G7.9] The service proposition attached to the ongoing adviser charge

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   The suitability report states on page 17 that the ongoing service includes: (1) Reviewing attitude to risk and capacity for loss to ensure portfolio remains suitable; (2) Reviewing ongoing suitability of investment portfolio; (3) Market updates and updates with changes in financial legislation; (4) Access to professional, experienced and qualified team of financial advisers at any time to provide ad-hoc advice as required; (5) Access to full holistic financial planning to suit needs. The report also states on page 4 that the adviser will keep risk profile under continuous review in line with ongoing review meetings. The cost is stated as 1% per annum, which would equal approximately £1,101.85 for the first year based on current values and expected contributions minus the initial fee, varying in line with fund value. The fee will be paid via the Aviva Platform pension scheme.

    File:     The Client Agreement [P1] specifies an ongoing annual review management charge of 1% (subject to a minimum fee of £100), automatically taken from funds invested, fluctuating with investment value. The agreement [P2] describes ongoing advice and review services as detailed in the 'Important Information about our Services' document. The IMPORT_2.md document [P8] details the on-going advice and review service, stating it includes: review meeting at least once per year with recommendations including bed and ISA, fund switches, drawdown withdrawals, portfolio rebalance, updated fact find and attitude to risk; statements and performance reports twice per year; cash flow analysis when relevant and beneficial; family benefit (adviser fee free advice for direct family members); regular market updates; quarterly e-magazine; online access for portfolio valuations; and access to adviser as needed. The charge is stated as 1% annual review management charge (with minimum £100 per annum) for most investment funds.

    Discrepancies:

      - The suitability report lists five specific ongoing services on page 17, while the client agreement/IMPORT_2.md lists nine service components including statements twice per year, cash flow analysis, family benefit, quarterly e-magazine, and online portal access. The suitability report does not mention statements, cash flow analysis, family benefit, e-magazine, or online portal access as part of the ongoing service.

      - The suitability report emphasizes 'access to a professional, experienced and qualified team' and 'ad-hoc advice as required' as primary service components, whereas the client agreement emphasizes structured annual review meetings with specific tactical actions (switches, rebalancing, updated fact find).

      - The suitability report does not disclose the minimum fee of £100 per annum mentioned in the client agreement [P1].

      - The suitability report states the fee will be 'paid via the Aviva Platform pension scheme as you prefer to pay via your provider rather than directly' but the client agreement [P1] states the charge is 'automatically taken from the funds invested' without specifying the payment method as a matter of preference.

    The suitability report and client agreement are describing the same 1% ongoing annual charge, and both documents are on file. However, they differ materially in scope and emphasis of what services constitute the proposition. The suitability report's description is less comprehensive and omits several material service elements that are explicitly included in the client agreement and supporting documentation. The report emphasizes ad-hoc advice accessibility and risk review, while the agreement emphasizes structured annual meetings with specific review and rebalancing actions, plus additional tangible deliverables (statements twice yearly, market updates, e-magazine, online access, cash flow analysis, family benefit). Neither document contradicts the other outright, but the suitability report presents an incomplete picture of the service proposition relative to what the client is agreeing to pay for in the formal agreement. The client agreement is the binding document that specifies what services are included in the 1% fee, and the suitability report should either match that scope or explicitly note any limitations. The omission of the £100 minimum fee is also a material incompleteness. The report's statement about payment 'as you prefer to pay via your provider' is not substantiated in the client agreement, which describes the fee as automatically deducted from funds invested without framing it as a preference-based choice.

  [G7.10] Benefits relied upon to justify a higher cost

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts eight material benefits to justify the recommendation: (1) Lower overall cost structure compared to existing pension, meaning more savings remain invested for growth (page 7); (2) Current lifestyling funds automatically reduce investment risk as approaching retirement, eventually moving portfolio outside agreed risk range (page 7); (3) Greater investment choice allowing more tailored strategy suited to risk profile with scope for future adjustments (page 7); (4) Adviser charging facilitation allowing direct deduction of charges from plan without separate payments (page 7); (5) Ongoing monitoring and adjustment of investments to ensure alignment with changing needs and market conditions (page 7); (6) Multi-asset fund selection providing professionally managed diversified investment solution aligned with financial goals and risk profile (page 12); (7) Enhanced retirement flexibility providing more flexible retirement options for tax-efficient withdrawal strategies (page 7); (8) Cost justification based on past performance of recommended funds achieving outperformance at critical yield of 4.8% per year to age 75, and recommended Aviva advised funds demonstrating stronger performance compared to existing Aviva holdings with comparable performance and reduced volatility (pages 11, 13). The report claims the new scheme is lower cost and provides greater flexibility and professional oversight.

    File:     Evidence shows: (1) Existing arrangements include People's Pension with 7 funds available; Scottish Widows with full and partial UFPLS; Aviva existing with FAD and full/partial UFPLS with 80+ funds; Zurich with full/partial UFPLS with 60+ funds; Standard Life with FAD and 300 funds available (P19). (2) The Aviva Key Features document (P4, P8) confirms Enhanced Retirement Flexibility is available on the recommended Aviva plan: flexi-access drawdown, single drawdown, phased income drawdown, annuity options, and death benefits. (3) The existing Zurich plan (P9, P13, P15) also offers full and partial UFPLS options. (4) The SIPP research report (P10) discloses MiFID II pre-sale costs for HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio at £101.92 (0.19%) and CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund at £304.82 (0.55%), with total aggregated fund costs of £406.74 (0.37%). (5) Standard Life policy info (P16, P17) confirms it offers consolidation benefits and flexible drawdown options. (6) Existing arrangements already provide multiple retirement options; Aviva policy info (P14, P18) confirms online access to pension at MyAviva and notes that 'Pensions together in one place with Aviva could save money for your client and time and admin costs'. (7) The report's claim of lower cost is contradicted by the charge arithmetic: the recommended solution at 0.44% ongoing charge (0.21% platform + 0.22% investment management per page 11) is not arithmetically supported against any clear baseline, and the aggregated fund costs in the research report (P10) show 0.37% for the recommended funds alone, not accounting for platform charges. (8) Lifestyling risk is confirmed in existing arrangements: Aviva pension my future focus consolidation (P7) states 'Invested in lifestyle strategy: Yes'; Scottish Widows Pension Portfolio Four CS1 & Three CS1 (P7) states 'Invested in lifestyle strategy: Yes'. (9) The report claims multi-asset fund recommendation (page 12) but the funds recommended (HSBC 50%, CT Universal MAP 50%, page 12 and P22) are both risk-rated at 5, not a multi-asset blend addressing the client's specific consolidation need.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report claims 'Lower Costs — The receiving scheme has a lower overall cost structure compared to your existing pension' (page 7, P21) but the computed charge figures show the recommended solution's total ongoing charge of 0.44% is not demonstrably lower than all existing arrangements. The existing Aviva arrangement (EA3) is 0.50% (page 5 per canonical model), the existing Zurich (EA4) is 0.93%, but the Standard Life arrangement (EA5) is stated as 0.18% on page 5, and the Zurich Managed Pn AP (EA4) fund charge is 0.18% plus 0.10% platform = 0.28% or 0.93% depending on interpretation (canonical ambiguity at /existingArrangements/3/chargesPercentage). The claim of lower cost is therefore inconsistent with the evidence that at least one existing arrangement (Standard Life at 0.18%) is substantially lower than the recommended 0.44%.

      - The report claims Enhanced Retirement Flexibility as a benefit (page 7, P21) justifying the recommendation, but the existing Zurich plan already offers full and partial UFPLS (P5, P9) and existing Standard Life offers FAD (P5), so this flexibility is not new and cannot justify additional cost under the guard that 'a benefit the existing plan already provides cannot justify the extra cost'.

      - The report claims 'Greater Investment Choice' as a benefit (page 7, P21), noting Zurich does not offer risk level 5 funds (page 6, P21), but the evidence shows Zurich offers 60+ funds (P5, P19) whereas Standard Life offers 300 funds (P5, P19) and Aviva offers 80+ funds (P5, P19). The existing arrangements already provide substantial choice, making this a marginal rather than material differentiator.

      - The report claims lifeystyling risk as a benefit justifying the switch (page 7, P21: 'Your funds are currently invested in lifestyling funds, which automatically reduce investment risk as you approach retirement. As a result, your portfolio will eventually move outside of your agreed risk range'). However, the evidence shows lifestyling is present in existing Aviva (P7) and Scottish Widows (P7) arrangements, and the recommendation to consolidate all five pensions into one Aviva plan (page 6, P20) perpetuates this arrangement — the recommended funds are HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio and CT Universal MAP Balanced (both risk 5, page 12, P22), which do not mitigate the lifestyling risk that triggered the recommendation; instead, they replace lifestyling with static risk-rated funds, but this addresses the client's risk alignment rather than justifying cost.

      - The report's claim of 'Ongoing Monitoring and Adjustments' (page 7, P21) supported by an ongoing adviser charging facilitation benefit appears without evidence in the case file of the agreed adviser fee structure, service level, frequency of monitoring, or correspondence with a client engagement letter. The canonical model notes adviser fees are extracted but no adviser remuneration evidence is provided to corroborate this service proposition is unique or justifies the cost difference.

      - The report asserts on page 6 (P21) that 'the plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' but the charge comparison table on page 11 (P22 shows Zurich at 0.50% existing vs. the recommended solution at approximately 0.44% (0.21% + 0.22%), implying the new plan is 0.06% cheaper, contradicting the text claim. This internal inconsistency undermines the cost justification narrative.

    The report identifies eight benefits to support the recommendation, but several fail the consistency test against evidence and the guards. First, 'Enhanced Retirement Flexibility' is already present in existing arrangements (Zurich offers full/partial UFPLS, Standard Life offers FAD per P5 and P9), so under the guard that a benefit the existing plan already provides cannot justify extra cost, this cannot be relied upon. Second, 'Greater Investment Choice' is marginal: Standard Life (300 funds) exceeds Aviva (80+ funds) substantially, so choice per se is not a material differentiator justifying cost. Third, the lifestyling risk benefit is internally inconsistent: the report cites lifestyling as a reason to switch, but the recommended funds are static risk-rated instruments, not a solution to the lifestyling problem — they replace one approach with another, and the same client is being moved to Aviva, which the evidence shows (P7) already employs lifestyling in the existing Aviva pension my future focus consolidation. The benefit claimed is addressing risk drift, not cost-justifying the switch. Fourth, the 'Lower Costs' claim is contradicted by arithmetic: the recommended solution is stated at 0.44% ongoing (page 11, P22: 0.21% platform + 0.22% investment management), but this is not lower than the existing Standard Life arrangement at 0.18% (canonical model, page 5), and the text on page 6 (P21) contradicts itself by stating 'the plan itself is 0.06% more expensive' when the comparison table implies it is cheaper. This internal contradiction and the fact that at least one existing arrangement is substantially lower undermines the lower-cost justification. Fifth, 'Ongoing Monitoring and Adjustments' and 'Adviser Charging Facilitation' are claimed without supporting evidence of the adviser agreement, fee structure, or specific services that differentiate this from existing arrangements. The report does not evidence that existing advisers were not providing similar monitoring, or that the new structure delivers measurably more value. Sixth, the cost justification based on 'past performance' and critical yield (pages 11, 13, P21) relies on the assertion that recommended funds will 'outperform by 4.8% per year' to justify the 0.44% annual charge, but this is not supported by documented performance comparison between existing and recommended funds in the evidence, nor is the critical yield calculation shown. The guards state that a benefit the client will never use cannot support cost, and consolidation convenience is real but not financial; here, consolidation and monitoring benefits are presented as cost-justifiers without quantifying the financial impact or proving the client values them over lower-cost alternatives. The report's articulation of benefits reads as general value propositions for Aviva rather than client-specific, cost-justified assertions grounded in evidence. The discrepancy between the claim of lower costs and the arithmetic showing the existing Standard Life at 0.18% is lower than the recommended 0.44%, combined with the internal text contradiction on page 6, means the core benefit claim—lower costs—is not supported by and is contradicted by the evidence provided.

  [G7.11] Higher charges producing lower projected outcomes or an increased yield requirement

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report states the recommendation is 'RecommendedCheaper' with a differencePercentage of -0.24% on annual recurring charges, asserting 'our calculations show that the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive than your current plans' (page 11). However, the same page also states that when adviser fees are included, 'the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than your current plans' and 'the recommended plan would need to grow by 4.8% per year up to age 75' to match the current plan value. Projected values at age 75 under mid-growth (2.95%) are: existing pension £127,000 vs new solution £115,000, a decrease of £12,000 or 9.4%. Reduction in yield for new solution is 2.2%, compared to 0.3% for existing. Critical yield required is 4.8% (page 11, page 16). Initial adviser charge is £3,305; ongoing adviser charge is 1.00% annually.

    File:     The pension switch report [P10] shows the same comparison: existing pension £127,000 vs new solution £115,000 at mid-growth rate (2.95%), with effect of switching showing £12,000 or 9.4% decrease. Reduction in yield for new solution is 2.2% vs 0.3% for existing. Critical yield for new solution is 4.8% [P10]. The report acknowledges 'Initial adviser charge of £3,305.00 which is taken from the new solution, reducing the initial investment accordingly' and 'An ongoing annual adviser charge of 1.00%' [P3]. The comparison confirms that the new solution has lower projected outcomes at equivalent growth rates despite the adviser asserting it as cheaper on recurring charges alone. The evidence shows the higher charges (including adviser fees) are not justified by improved fund range or guarantees—the new solution explicitly produces lower projected values than the existing arrangement.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report claims recommendation is 'RecommendedCheaper' on recurring charges (-0.24%) but evidence shows new solution is materially more expensive when adviser fees are included (4.24% additional required outperformance)

      - Report projects £115,000 at mid-growth for new solution vs £127,000 for existing—a £12,000 (9.4%) decrease in outcomes—yet recommendation frame does not emphasise this material shortfall

      - Reduction in yield increases from 0.3% to 2.2% under new solution, requiring 4.8% critical yield vs implied lower requirement for existing plan

      - Report asserts cheaper recurring charges but simultaneously requires 4.24% annual outperformance to break even, creating internal contradiction between the 'cheaper' framing and the actual cost burden

    The comparison can be fully performed. The core issue is that the report frames the recommendation as 'cheaper' on product and platform charges alone (-0.24%) but this omits adviser fees entirely from the initial framing. When adviser charges are included (initial £3,305 plus 1.00% ongoing), the recommendation becomes 4.24% more expensive in required outperformance terms. At the prescribed mid-growth rate of 2.95%, the new solution projects to £115,000 at age 75 versus £127,000 for the existing arrangement—a £12,000 shortfall. The reduction in yield rises from 0.3% to 2.2%, and the critical yield requirement becomes 4.8%, meaning the funds must outperform at rates significantly higher than the baseline assumption simply to match current outcomes. The evidence [P10] corroborates these exact figures. The 'cheaper' claim thus relates only to stripped product/platform charges and cannot be reconciled with the simultaneous assertion that adviser fees add 4.24% to the cost. The report does not adequately explain why a client should accept a recommendation that produces £12,000 less at retirement (9.4% decrease) and requires substantial additional fund growth just to break even against existing arrangements. No benefits—such as improved fund range, greater flexibility, consolidation efficiency, or guarantee preservation—are cited to justify the higher total cost burden or the lower projected outcome. The guard permitting required outperformance 'under about 0.5% a year' as noise does not apply here: 4.24% annual outperformance is materially above that threshold. The guard regarding lower projections driven by lower prescribed growth rates does not apply: both projections use the same 2.95% mid-growth assumption, so the shortfall is purely charge-driven, not a normalisation issue.

CHK-008 — Disadvantages, Risks and Customer Understanding

Outcome: Potential Concern

 

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 8 raised a concern (G8.1, G8.2, G8.3, G8.4, G8.5, G8.6, G8.7, G8.8). Report states adviser charge impact is 4.24% more expensive in first year requiring £4,672 outperformance, but no loss scenario is stated showing what happens if this growth is not achieved. Report cites critical yield of 4.8% per year to age 75 but does not quantify the monetary impact or illustrate what fund value would result if this yield is not achieved. Report discloses withdrawal restrictions on transfer but does not explain alternative withdrawal options available under current arrangements being given up. 15 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

 

  [G8.1] Material disadvantages, risk factors and limitations of the recommendation

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report identifies material disadvantages across multiple sections: (1) Critical Yield Requirement stating the recommended plan would need to grow by 4.8% per year to age 75 to match current plan value; (2) Adviser Charge Impact stating the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than current plans, requiring outperformance of £4,672 in the first year; (3) Lifestyling Drift noting that lifestyling funds automatically reduce risk as retirement approaches, moving portfolio outside agreed risk range; (4) Withdrawal Restrictions stating transfer would limit future flexible withdrawals; (5) Fund Range Constraint noting Scottish Widows lacks matching multi-asset fund, requiring combination with 20-30% in cash; (6) generic disclaimer that additional risks are in product literature; and (7) generic statement that there is no guarantee new investment will outperform existing arrangements. The report does not state any loss scenario in monetary or percentage terms showing potential outcome if growth rates are not achieved.

    File:     Product literature (Aviva Key Features and Terms and Conditions) discloses multiple risks: investment values may go down as well as up; client could get back less than invested; value depends on payments, investment performance and charges; projections are not guaranteed; income drawdown may deplete funds if not enough growth occurs; clients may pay excessive tax; switching between providers does not guarantee greater fund at retirement; if liabilities exceed assets, investments may be sold at unfavorable times; Commercial Property may take time to sell; cancellation may result in loss if values fall; non-availability of complex investments when advice relationship ends; currency risk; and market timing risk between instruction and execution. The Pension Switch Report shows that at mid-growth (2.94%), the new solution produces an effect of switching of minus £10 or 0.3% decrease; at high growth (5.88%), minus £10 or 0.2% decrease. These reports provide critical yield calculations showing what growth is required to match existing arrangements. The Suitability Report itself states on page 20: 'A switch between pension providers does not guarantee a greater pension fund at retirement and there is no guarantee that the new provider's funds will outperform those of your existing provider.' No evidence documents what loss scenarios the client was shown or whether specific downside projections were illustrated.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states adviser charge impact is 4.24% more expensive in first year requiring £4,672 outperformance, but no loss scenario is stated showing what happens if this growth is not achieved

      - Report cites critical yield of 4.8% per year to age 75 but does not quantify the monetary impact or illustrate what fund value would result if this yield is not achieved

      - Report discloses withdrawal restrictions on transfer but does not explain alternative withdrawal options available under current arrangements being given up

      - Report mentions lifestyling drift moving portfolio outside risk range but does not quantify the magnitude or timing of this drift or its impact on retirement outcomes

      - Report contains generic boilerplate about risks in product literature but does not extract or highlight material product-specific risks relevant to this client's circumstances

      - Product literature discloses that switching does not guarantee greater fund value but this specific caution is only mentioned on page 20 in generic context, not prominently in the recommendation section where benefits are stated

      - Pension Switch Report shows negative effects of switching at both mid-growth and high-growth scenarios (£10 decrease in each case) but Report does not reference these projections from the switching analysis

      - No evidence of tax consequence disclosure despite report structure including 'Tax Treatment and Law' section; report states tax treatment is based on 'current tax law and HMRC practice' but does not explain specific tax consequences of the recommended transfer

      - Report does not disclose that client's current lifestyling funds will reduce risk automatically, potentially moving away from his agreed risk rating 5 over time, as a disadvantage of retention versus a fresh switch

    The comparison can be performed as all necessary evidence exists: the suitability report's disadvantages section, product literature, and pension switch analysis. Applying the Method SetCoverage and Decision Logic for No Issue: Material disadvantages, product and investment risks, tax consequences and at least one loss scenario must be stated in the report, consistent with research and product literature, and presented where client will see them. The report fails on multiple counts: (1) Loss scenarios are absent—the report quantifies the critical yield requirement (4.8%) and first-year outperformance need (£4,672) but never illustrates what happens if these thresholds are not met, either in monetary terms or as projected fund values at retirement under downside scenarios. The Pension Switch Report itself contains negative effects (£10 decrease in switching at both mid and high growth rates relative to retaining existing plans), but these are not translated into client-facing loss scenarios in the suitability report. (2) Tax consequences are not explained. Section 'Tax Treatment and Law' disclaims that treatments are based on current law but does not explain specific tax consequences to this client of the transfer, such as whether any special withdrawal regime applies, how tax relief is affected, or what his marginal tax rate implications are. (3) Critical disadvantage of lifestyling drift is incompletely disclosed. The report states current funds are lifestyling funds that 'automatically reduce investment risk as you approach retirement' and that portfolio 'will eventually move outside of your agreed risk range,' but it does not quantify when this occurs or by how much, nor does it explain that this is a consequence of remaining in existing arrangements (not a reason to switch). This makes it unclear whether switching out of lifestyling is an advantage or whether the disadvantage applies to both options. (4) Withdrawal restriction is stated generically without comparison. Report states transfer 'would limit how you can withdraw funds' and client 'would be unable to access this pension flexibly,' but does not specify what withdrawal flexibilities are lost (FAD? UFPLS? annuity conversion?) or compare them to current arrangements' withdrawal options. (5) Fund range constraint is stated but not quantified. The 20-30% cash holding is mentioned but not contextualized as a loss of equity exposure or return drag. (6) Risks are segregated from benefits. Benefits appear prominently in the recommendation section (pages 2, 6); disadvantages and risks are scattered: critical yield and adviser charge on page 11 in 'Pension Switch Charges Assessment'; lifestyling drift on page 7; withdrawal restrictions on page 6; fund constraint on page 6; generic product literature reference on page 8. This scatter diminishes prominence. (7) Generic boilerplate dominates. Statements such as 'There is no guarantee the return on the new investment will be greater than that of your existing arrangement' are repeated three times on page 8 but are not product-specific, fund-specific, or scenario-specific. They do not explain *why* outperformance is uncertain for *this* client given his risk rating, fund selections, and cost structure. Product literature (P4, P7, P13) contains material risks specific to income drawdown, lifestyling, currency exposure, property liquidity, and tax: none of these are extracted and tailored to the recommendation in the report. The extraction metadata confirms this: /replacementAnalysis/0/featuresOrGuaranteesLost is absent ('NotPresentInDocument'), meaning the report did not identify what features or protections the client is giving up by switching. Under the Vulnerability Overlay requirement: The fact find (P3, P24) shows client is age 70, an HGV driver earning 'typically' £300 per week net, with monthly household expenditure of £1,718 against net income of £1,430, producing a monthly deficit of -£288. This reveals severe financial vulnerability: the client is already in deficit and depends on pension transfers to fund ongoing living expenses. This vulnerability materially affects the CHK-008 assessment because (a) the disadvantage of requiring 4.8% annual outperformance is more critical to a client with no income cushion, and (b) the lifestyling drift disadvantage is more material because he cannot afford to wait out downturns in a risk-5 portfolio near or in retirement, and (c) withdrawal flexibility becomes a material disadvantage because he will likely need to draw income immediately, making restrictions more consequential than for a client with surplus income. The report does not acknowledge this vulnerability context when presenting disadvantages. Applying the severity guidance: Highest—material disadvantages present in product literature and research (switching does not guarantee outperformance; projections uncertain; income drawdown may deplete funds; tax consequences) are omitted or not extracted into client-facing recommendations. No loss scenario is illustrated. Result: Potential Concern.

  [G8.2] Product-specific and investment-specific risks explained

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report states product-specific risks including: (1) Access delay risk: 'Should you require access to these funds in the future it will usually take 10-15 working days for administration, disinvestment and banking processes to complete' (page 8); (2) Market timing risk during transition: 'Whilst the transactions are pending, there could be movements within the stock-market...If the market rises in value between the date of realising your existing funds and the purchase of your new funds, you could be financially disadvantaged' (page 8); (3) Performance variability: 'Your recommended Aviva advised funds have demonstrated stronger performance compared to your existing Aviva holdings. However, they have not outperformed your current Zurich funds. It's important to note that your Zurich investments are allocated to higher-risk funds, which are more susceptible to market volatility' (page 13); (4) No performance guarantee: 'A switch between pension providers does not guarantee a greater pension fund at retirement and there is no guarantee that the new provider's funds will outperform those of your existing provider' (page 12); (5) General investment risk: 'Unit prices and the value of your pension fund can fall as well as rise and there is no guarantee you will receive a return of your original capital, especially in the early years or if you choose to take an income from it' (page 12).

    File:     The product literature and supporting evidence provide the following risks: (1) HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio KIID states 'The Fund is rated 4 because historically it has shown a medium level of volatility' (P9) and identifies material risks including 'Counterparty Risk', 'Credit Risk', 'Default Risk', 'Derivatives Risk', 'Currency Risk', 'Leverage Risk', and 'Liquidity Risk' (P3, P11). (2) The HSBC Fund Factsheet confirms 'The value of investments and any income from them can go down as well as up and investors may not get back the amount originally invested' and includes risks regarding 'overseas investments', 'bonds whose value generally falls when interest rates rise', and 'Default Risk' (P5). (3) Zurich Policy Info identifies specific fund risks: 'Property funds...could fall quite sharply. In more uncertain market conditions, we may need to delay your transaction in these funds by up to 12 months' (P16); 'High yield bond funds...generally higher risk investments than government bonds'; 'Funds specialising in gold mining shares tend not to follow stock market movements'; 'Funds investing in shares of smaller companies...carry more investment risk'; 'Less developed stock markets...risks from political, economic and market factors' (P16, P17). (4) Zurich Policy Info states 'Over time, a fund that invests mostly in shares is likely to offer greater potential for higher returns...but with it comes greater fluctuations in value' (P17). (5) Aviva Key Features document identifies multiple risks including income drawdown risks: 'Your investments need to grow to compensate for the income you withdraw. If that doesn't happen, the income you take will deplete your pension fund' and 'If you withdraw high levels of income, there'll be less money to provide for dependants' (P4, P14).

    Discrepancies:

      - Report does not identify or explain the specific risks of the CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund despite it being allocated 50% of the recommended portfolio (per P15), while product literature risks are cited only for HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio and generic Zurich risks.

      - Report does not address currency risk despite evidence that both recommended funds hold overseas investments: HSBC fund invests 'across global markets' (P1) and Zurich policy warns 'If the underlying investments include property...currency in which the underlying investments of the fund are made, the value of your investment will reduce' (P4).

      - Report does not disclose derivative risks despite evidence that HSBC fund 'may invest in different types of derivatives for efficient portfolio management purposes' (P3) and Zurich policy warns 'Some fund managers may take their yearly management charge from capital rather than income. This results in a higher income but lower capital growth' (P16).

      - Report does not explain interest rate risk or bond-specific risks despite evidence that HSBC fund holds '20-55% Bonds' allocation (P3) and bonds carry specific risks when interest rates rise (P5, P17).

      - Report does not address property-related liquidity delays despite fund allocations potentially including property: Zurich policy warns 'we may need to delay your transaction in these funds by up to 12 months' (P16).

      - Report does not disclose inflation risk for fixed-interest securities as identified in Zurich policy: 'The rate of income on fixed-interest securities...won't increase in line with inflation unless they're index-linked' (P17).

      - Report does not explain counterparty risk, credit risk, or default risk for bonds, which are identified in HSBC KIID (P3, P11) as material risks not fully captured by the Risk and Reward Indicator.

      - Report does not address emerging markets risk despite HSBC fund literature noting 'Emerging Markets Risk: this Fund invests in emerging markets which are less developed and have additional legal and political risks' (P11).

      - Report does not explain leverage risk despite HSBC fund using derivatives 'to reduce or increase the Fund's exposure to different types of assets' (P3, P11).

      - Report contains no specific risk disclosures regarding the CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund, leaving material risks of a 50% allocated fund entirely undisclosed.

    The report addresses some generic product and investment risks consistent with standard pension advice (market timing risk, access delays, no performance guarantees, general capital-at-risk warnings). However, the comparison reveals significant gaps in product-specific risk disclosure. The report identifies HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio (50% allocation) with limited context and mentions Zurich Zurich funds but does not systematically disclose the material risks identified in the product literature. Most critically, the CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund (50% allocation per P15) receives no product-specific risk disclosure whatsoever. The supporting evidence identifies numerous material risks absent from the report: counterparty risk, credit risk, default risk, derivatives risk, currency risk, emerging markets risk, leverage risk, liquidity risk, interest rate risk, property liquidity delays, inflation risk on fixed-interest securities, and specific risks of smaller companies and less developed markets. The guard regarding deferral to product literature applies here: the report does not comprehensively integrate product-specific risks into the suitability narrative; instead, risk content appears scattered (pages 8, 12, 13) with limited cross-reference to the specific funds and their particular risk profiles. The failure to disclose risks for 50% of the recommended allocation (CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund) is a material omission. For a pension switch recommendation involving five existing arrangements being consolidated into a single Aviva Platform with two named funds, the report's risk disclosure is incomplete relative to the supporting literature's identification of material product and investment-specific risks.

  [G8.3] Tax consequences of the recommendation

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report states: (1) A general tax caveat on page 16 that 'All statements concerning the tax treatment of products and their benefits are based on our understanding of current tax law and HMRC practice. Levels and basis of tax relief are subject to change.' (2) On page 9, the report states the client 'understand[s] the pension freedoms and prefer[s] the flexibility of entering Flexi Access Drawdown when you access your pension, allowing you control over how and when benefits are taken.' (3) Death benefits taxation is explained: 'Should you die before age 75, the nominated beneficiaries can receive a lump sum, drawdown an income or purchase a dependents annuity. All these options are paid free of tax. Where you die after the age of 75, the same options are available, but tax is paid at the beneficiary's marginal rate of tax.' (4) Tax relief is mentioned: 'Personal contributions to the pension benefit from valuable tax relief; for example, a net contribution of £80 effectively becomes £100 gross in your pension.' (5) For the Scottish Widows encashment (small pot), the report states on page 6: 'Under the small pot rules, a £200 pension fund withdrawal would be taxed with 25% taken as tax-free cash and the remaining 75% taxed as income at your marginal tax rate. We estimate you would receive £170 net.'

    File:     Supporting evidence from the Key Features document (P2, P4) confirms: (1) General tax caveat matching the report: tax information 'is based on our understanding of current UK legislation and practice. However, tax rules may change in the future.' (2) Standard pension freedoms explanation: 'You can normally withdraw up to 25% of your pension fund as a tax-free lump sum. You may have to pay income tax on any income payments and lump sum payments you take.' (3) Death benefits under age 75 are tax-free; after age 75 taxed at beneficiary's marginal rate, matching the report's statement. (4) References to lump sum allowance (£268,275) and lump sum & death benefit allowance (£1,073,100) are detailed in product literature (P2, P4, P6). (5) The small pots rule explanation in the report on page 6 matches the generic disclosure structure but the specific net receipt estimate of £170 is an adviser calculation not independently verified in the evidence. (6) The Flexi Access Drawdown section (P6) confirms the mechanism but does not personalise it to this client's circumstances.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report provides a general tax caveat and generic pension freedom explanations but does not establish the client's personal marginal tax rate, which is essential to quantify the tax consequence of income drawdown withdrawals under FAD.

      - For the Scottish Widows encashment small pot calculation (£170 net from £200 withdrawal), the report does not state the client's marginal tax rate or how £170 was derived from the 25% tax-free / 75% taxable split, making it impossible to verify the calculation against the client's actual tax position.

      - The report states on page 9 that the client 'prefer[s] the flexibility of entering Flexi Access Drawdown' but does not explain the tax consequence of regular income withdrawals under FAD at the client's marginal rate, which is material to understanding the retirement income strategy.

      - Death benefit taxation is explained generically (pre-75 tax-free, post-75 at beneficiary's rate) but the client is aged 69, so the pre-75 scenario is highly material and time-limited; the report does not emphasise this urgency or its implication for planning.

      - The report mentions tax relief on contributions (£80 net = £100 gross) as a general principle but does not address whether the client, as a higher earner or depending on income band, receives basic rate (20%) or higher rate (40%) relief, leaving a material tax consequence unquantified.

      - The report does not disclose the HMRC lump sum allowance (£268,275) or lump sum & death benefit allowance (£1,073,100) limits to the client, though these are referenced in product literature (P2, P4, P6); these are material constraints on tax-free benefits.

    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Client's personal marginal tax rate (essential to quantify income tax on FAD withdrawals and small pot encashment)., Confirmation of client's employment income band to establish whether tax relief on contributions is at basic rate (20%) or higher rate (40%)., Derivation and verification of the £170 net receipt calculation for the Scottish Widows £200 small pot encashment., Client's awareness of and consent to the HMRC lump sum allowance and lump sum & death benefit allowance limits., Any other pension benefits or arrangements outside this suitability report that might affect the client's use of allowances.

    The report states multiple tax consequences but the comparison cannot be fully performed because the client's personal tax circumstances—specifically marginal tax rate—are not established in the canonical model or evidence, yet they are material to quantifying the actual tax impact of the recommendation. The generic tax statements (caveat on page 16, death benefits rules, pension freedoms overview) are corroborated by product literature (P2, P4, P6) and meet the standard disclosure requirement. However, a tax consequence becomes material and requires specific disclosure when it directly flows from the recommendation and affects the client's net benefit. For the small pots encashment, the report provides a specific estimate (£170 net from £200) but does not show the calculation or state the assumed tax rate, making it unverifiable. The report's reference to entering Flexi Access Drawdown is presented as the client's preference but the material tax consequence—that each withdrawal is taxed at the client's marginal rate, with no upper limit—is stated only in generic form on page 9 and page 6, not in relation to the client's specific retirement income needs or tax position. The age 75 death benefit threshold is material (client is 69) but not emphasised as a time-critical planning point. The lump sum and death benefit allowances are disclosed in product literature but not brought to the client's attention in the suitability report itself, creating a gap between what the file knows and what the report tells the client. The guard 'A tax consequence stated without the client's tax band established is unverifiable, not necessarily wrong' applies: the small pot calculation cannot be verified without knowing the assumed marginal rate. The guard 'Generic legislative explanation is not the same as stating the consequence for this client' also applies: while pension freedoms and death benefit rules are explained generically and correctly, their specific impact on this client's retirement income, tax position and estate planning is not articulated.
