  [G8.4] Loss scenarios illustrated

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report asserts one loss scenario: 'if your plan sustained an immediate loss of -30% and the increased inflation continued, there would be no shortfall' (page 15, Alternative Scenarios & Stress Tests section). This is presented as a stress test combining immediate market crash (-30%) with high inflation (6%).

    File:     The case file contains multiple risk disclosures across Key Features, KIIDs, and illustrations. P2 lists general investment risks including market falls, currency exposure, property liquidity, and fixed-term access restrictions. P3 contains a detailed three-scenario projection table (Low 0.00%, Mid 2.95%, High 5.88% growth rates) showing projected values and switching effects. P10 and P15 provide illustration tables with lower (1.9%), medium (5.0%), and higher (8.0%) example growth rates. P22 confirms the stress test: 'if your plan sustained an immediate loss of -30% and the increased inflation continued, there would be no shortfall that requires your pension or investment funds to be used.' P21 states assumptions including general expenditure inflation at 2%. However, the file contains no forward-looking loss scenario table showing downside outcomes (e.g., capital loss illustrations, shortfall timelines, or depletion scenarios under adverse conditions).

    Discrepancies:

      - The report states a single named loss scenario (-30% crash with 6% inflation), while the supporting evidence shows three-scenario projection tables (Low/Mid/High growth) across multiple documents but no corresponding downside loss scenarios explicitly illustrated for the recommended solution

      - The stress test in P22 confirms the -30% crash outcome but presents it only narratively ('there would be no shortfall') rather than as a quantified loss scenario table comparable to the growth projections in P3, P10, or P15

      - Product literature (P2, P7) discloses generic risks (market falls, currency, liquidity) but the suitability report does not connect these generic risks to client-specific loss illustrations

      - The illustration documents (P10, P15) show only positive growth scenarios (1.9% lower, 5.0% medium, 8.0% higher) and do not include downside or loss scenarios in visual or tabular form

    The report asserts that a loss scenario has been illustrated: the -30% immediate market crash combined with 6% inflation. This scenario is confirmed in the case file at P22, where the same stress test result is stated. However, the guard permits stress tests in cashflow models to count as loss scenarios even if not labelled as such in a risk section. The -30% stress test is mentioned only narratively in both P22 and the report; it is not presented in the report as a quantified loss scenario table showing projected fund values, depletion timelines, or income shortfalls under that adverse condition. The report does not illustrate what the client's pension fund would be worth after a -30% loss, or when funds would deplete, or what income could be sustained. The growth projection table in P3 (covering Low 0.00%, Mid 2.95%, High 5.88% scenarios) shows projected values at age 75 under different growth assumptions, and the illustration in P10 and P15 shows accumulation outcomes. Neither the report nor the file contains a corresponding downside scenario table. The stress test demonstrates that the plan would survive a -30% loss without requiring pension withdrawal, but the report does not illustrate this outcome in the format expected for loss scenarios—as quantified projections comparable to the growth illustrations provided. The discrepancy is that loss scenarios are typically illustrated as specific fund value projections or income/depletion timelines under adverse conditions, whereas here the result is stated only as a narrative conclusion ('there would be no shortfall'). The COBS 9.4.7R and associated guidance expect disadvantages and loss scenarios to be clearly and prominently explained with specific figures. A narrative statement of a stress test outcome, without corresponding quantified illustrations, falls short of this standard. The generic risks in product literature are not connected to client-specific loss scenarios in the report.

  [G8.5] Disadvantages and risks are fairly and prominently presented

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report asserts that a dedicated section titled 'Other Considerations of Switching' exists on page 8, where disadvantages and risks are presented. The report states this section appears on page 8 and is not confined to an appendix only (appearsOnlyInAppendix: false).

    File:     Page 8 of the suitability report contains a section headed 'Other Considerations of Switching' (P6). This section begins with a statement about stock-market movements during pending transactions. The report also presents loss scenarios in a dedicated table on the same primary pages (P1, P11) showing projected value reductions under three growth scenarios (low 0.00%, mid 2.95%, high 5.88%), with specific monetary and percentage losses quantified: at mid-growth, a £12,000 or 9.4% decrease. Charge comparisons are presented in a table on the main body (P4) showing 0.44% total charge. Critical yield requirement of 4.8% at mid-growth is stated in the main text (P2). Risk warnings about lack of guarantee appear in the body (P5): 'A switch between pension providers does not guarantee a greater pension fund at retirement and there is no guarantee that the new provider's funds will outperform those of your existing provider.' Disadvantages are also listed on page 6 (P6), including mention of lifestyling fund risks and cost comparisons, though one cost statement contains an error.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states plan is '0.08% more expensive' (P6) but derived figures show the new solution charge is 0.44% versus Aviva existing at 0.50%, making it 0.06% cheaper, not more expensive — a contradiction in the report's own arithmetic

      - The prominence field asserts the section 'appearsOnlyInAppendix: false' but P8 shows ongoing monitoring services and adviser charges are discussed, with critical yield detail explicitly relegated to the Appendix (P8 states 'This is called the Critical Yield and can be found in the Appendix of this report')

      - Loss scenarios appear with full quantification in the main body tables (P1, P11) but the critical yield calculation methodology and detailed computation are reserved for the Appendix (P8)

    The suitability report does present a dedicated section on page 8 titled 'Other Considerations of Switching', confirming the dedication assertion. Material disadvantages are present: stock-market movement risk, lifestyling fund drift, and lower cost benefit are all stated. Loss scenarios are quantified prominently in main-body tables across three growth scenarios, showing reductions of £10,400–£15,000 or 9.6%–10.0% depending on growth rate (P1, P11). Critical yield thresholds (1.9%–7.8%) are stated in the body text (P2) and appendix (P8). Risk warnings appear in the body (P5) disclaiming guarantees. Charge impact is disclosed (0.44% new vs 0.50% existing for Aviva alone, P4). However, a material internal contradiction exists: page 6 (P6) asserts 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution,' which contradicts both the arithmetic shown in P4 (0.50% − 0.44% = 0.06% cheaper) and the derived figures. This is not a comparison against external evidence but an internal inconsistency within the report itself regarding a core disadvantage claim. The guards note that a dedicated risk section is not mandatory if disadvantages are clearly stated, and distributed content can still be prominent. The content here is both dedicated and distributed with quantification in the body, meeting the structural requirement. The critical yield detail placement in the appendix, while the loss scenario is in the body, is permissible under the guard ('A material disadvantage quantified only in an appendix, while the body leads with a benefit' — here both are in the body). The report fairly presents loss scenarios in all three growth states, tax consequences are not applicable to pension consolidation (not a withdrawal), and material investment risks are identified. The internal arithmetic error regarding the cost comparison (0.08% vs 0.06% or the direction of the claim) undermines the accuracy of a claimed disadvantage, though the actual lower cost is correctly shown in the charge table. No vulnerability overlay indicators are recorded in the evidence that would materially affect prominence or clarity for this client.

  [G8.6] Benefits, features or protections the client is giving up

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report identifies one feature being lost: 'Employer contribution will be lost' with the quote 'If you were to transfer away, you would miss out on the valuable benefit of your employer's contributions.' The report also states across all 5 existing arrangements that 'This pension arrangement does not contain any safeguarded benefits'. The report lists existing retirement options across the plans as: FlexiAccessDrawdown, UFPLS, PartialUFPLS, with the Standard Life arrangement offering only FlexiAccessDrawdown.

    File:     The policy literature shows: (1) People's Pension [P7]: charges 0.5% ongoing management charge, no life cover, no guaranteed annuity rates, offers flexible retirement options. (2) Scottish Widows [P5]: no Guaranteed Minimum Pension, no Guaranteed Minimum Fund, no waiver of premium, no life cover. (3) Aviva [P14]: annual fund charge 0.50%, no adviser charging, free unlimited switching, no mention of employer contributions or employer matching. (4) Zurich [P3]: notes 'life cover and waiver benefits are no longer available on new pension plans' and states 'Some of our plans have guaranteed annuity rates when you retire'. (5) Standard Life [P10]: Differential Fund Discount from TER with 0.4800% leaver discount noted as benefit not available elsewhere; no Guaranteed Annuity Rates (GARs), no Guaranteed Minimum Pension (GMP), no Guaranteed Fund at retirement [P17]; facilitates drawdown [P17]. The policy research [P1,P2,P8] shows retirement options available: Flexi Access Drawdown (FAD), Full and Partial UFPLS, Annuity options. Zurich shows FAD available only if fund value increased to £10k; Standard Life shows FAD available; Aviva shows Annuity available. No document evidences employer contributions being made to any of the five existing arrangements.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report states employer contributions will be lost if the client transfers, but no evidence in the case file shows employer contributions are actually being made to any of the five existing arrangements. The Fact Find [P19] shows 'Death in service spousal benefits: £0.00' and contains blank fields for 'Applicable Penalties' and 'Other Benefits/or Material Features' but does not explicitly record employer contributions on any plan.

      - The report does not disclose the loss of the Standard Life Differential Fund Discount (Leaver Discount 0.4800%) identified in [P10], which represents a material feature specific to that plan not available from other providers.

      - The report does not identify as a lost feature the Zurich life cover and waiver benefits explicitly noted in [P3] as 'no longer available on new pension plans' and requiring separate medical questionnaire if obtained elsewhere.

      - The report lists retirement options available but does not explain which specific options are being lost or retained for each plan under the consolidation recommendation, given that Standard Life offers only FAD while other plans offer FAD, UFPLS and PartialUFPLS.

    The requirement asks whether the report fairly, clearly and accurately explains all material disadvantages, risks and benefits the client is giving up. Three material gaps emerge: (1) The employer contribution claim is unsupported. The report asserts employer contributions will be lost, but the evidence pack contains no documentation showing such contributions are currently being made to any of the five arrangements. The Aviva policy [P14] shows no pension input in recent years (£0 for 2025/2026, 2024/2025, 2023/2024, 2022/2023, 2021/2022; only £756.14 in 2020/2021), suggesting no active employer contributions. Without supporting evidence that employer contributions exist, this assertion appears to be made on a false premise. (2) The Standard Life Differential Fund Discount is a material feature documented in [P10] ('This member benefits from a Differential Fund Discount from the Total Expense Ratio (TER)' with 0.4800% leaver discount, explicitly noted 'These low charges may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer'). This specific benefit is not mentioned in the report's list of features lost, representing a material omission. (3) Zurich life cover and waiver benefits are identified in [P3] as features 'no longer available on new pension plans' and noted as 'particularly important if your health has deteriorated', yet the report does not disclose this as a material disadvantage being given up, contrary to the regulatory requirement to state all material disadvantages. (4) The retirement options analysis reveals complexity: the report lists options available but does not explain which are being relinquished. Standard Life [P17] explicitly states 'Standard Life do not permit partial transfers for investment purposes from this Pension contract', yet the new solution involves transfer. The report's generic treatment of safeguarded benefits ('This pension arrangement does not contain any safeguarded benefits' repeated identically for all five arrangements) masks plan-specific features like the Standard Life discount. These omissions constitute material clarification required.

  [G8.7] Justification for increased costs or reduced projected outcomes

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts that the recommended solution is justified by: (1) 'Based on the past performance of your recommended funds, I do believe that this is achievable' regarding a critical yield of 4.8% per year to age 75; (2) 'The recommended Aviva advised funds have demonstrated stronger performance compared to your existing Aviva holdings'; (3) 'the Aviva platform funds selected for you align with your agreed risk tolerance and have delivered comparable performance with reduced volatility'; (4) ongoing review services including attitude to risk assessment, investment suitability review, market updates, and access to professional advisers. The report also states 'There is no guarantee the return on the new investment will be greater than that of your existing arrangement for these funds' (repeated three times). The report identifies the recommended solution as 'RecommendedCheaper' in its cost comparison direction. Page 18 projects values at age 75: Low growth £97,600, Mid growth £115,000, High growth £135,000.

    File:     The pension switch report (P1) shows that at mid growth rate (2.95%), the existing pension projects to £127,000 versus the new solution at £125,000 - a reduction of £2,000 or 1.9% decrease. At high growth rate (5.88%), existing pension projects to £150,000 versus new solution at £147,000 - a reduction of £3,000 or 2.0% decrease. The critical yield required for the new solution to match existing outcomes is shown as 3.2% (mid growth) and 6.2% (high growth). The pension switch report (P1) also shows that the existing solution ranks 2nd (£127,000) in a comparison of pension products, while the new solution ranks 5th (£125,000). The SIPP research report (P13) shows MiFID II costs: combined fund costs total £406.74 (0.37%), with CT Universal MAP at 0.55% (£304.82) and HSBC at 0.19% (£101.92). The report states there is an initial adviser charge of £3,305.00 and ongoing annual adviser charge of 1.00%. The suitability report (P19) states the Aviva Platform Personal Pension total charge is 0.44% (£484.81 annually), comprising platform charge 0.21% (£231.39) and investment management 0.22% (£253.43).

    Discrepancies:

      - Report projects Mid growth value of £115,000 at age 75; file evidence shows existing pension projects £127,000 and new solution £125,000 at same growth rate - a variance of £10,000-£12,000 from file projections.

      - Report projects High growth value of £135,000 at age 75; file evidence shows existing pension projects £150,000 and new solution £147,000 - a variance of £12,000-£15,000 from file projections.

      - Report asserts recommended funds have 'demonstrated stronger performance compared to existing Aviva holdings' and that new solution is 'RecommendedCheaper'; file evidence shows new solution ranks 5th among alternatives with £125,000 maturity value versus existing Zurich at 1st (£128,000) and existing solution at 2nd (£127,000).

      - Report states critical yield needed is 4.8% per year; file evidence (P1) shows critical yield of 3.2% for mid growth and 6.2% for high growth scenarios, with no mention of 4.8% figure.

      - Report projects £115,000 mid-growth outcome; this figure does not match the £110,185 transfer value stated elsewhere or either the £127,000 (existing) or £125,000 (new solution) shown in pension switch report.

      - Report claims new solution delivers 'reduced volatility'; file evidence shows 'Reduction in yield' of 0.6% for new solution versus 0.3% for existing solution at mid growth - indicating higher yield drag, not lower volatility.

    The report asserts cost justification and performance expectations that are contradicted by the supporting evidence on multiple material points. First, the projected maturity values in the report (£115,000 mid, £135,000 high) do not reconcile with the pension switch report projections (£125,000 new solution mid, £147,000 high). Second, the report claims the recommended funds have demonstrated stronger performance and that the solution is cheaper, yet the pension switch report ranks the new solution 5th with a lower maturity value (£125,000) than both the existing solution (£127,000, ranked 2nd) and Zurich (£128,000, ranked 1st). Third, the report states a critical yield of 4.8% is 'achievable' but provides no evidence or calculation showing this figure. The pension switch report shows the critical yield required is 3.2% to 6.2% depending on growth scenario, not a flat 4.8%. Fourth, the report claims the new solution delivers 'comparable performance with reduced volatility', yet the evidence shows the reduction in yield (charge impact) is 0.6% for the new solution versus 0.3% for the existing - indicating higher charge drag not lower volatility. The report does disclose that 'There is no guarantee the return on the new investment will be greater than that of your existing arrangement' (repeated), and it does reference ongoing services and review, but these generic justifications do not connect to the specific cost increases or outcome reductions evident in the comparator data. The pension switch report shows the new solution is more expensive in terms of outcome (lower maturity value) and requires higher critical yields to match existing outcomes, yet the report presents it as the justified recommendation based on performance and cost grounds that the evidence does not support.

  [G8.8] Material risks or limitations identified in the evidence but not disclosed in the report

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report discloses three material disadvantages: (1) Critical Yield Requirement—the recommended plan must grow by 4.8% per year to age 75 to match the current plan value, based on mid-growth of 2.94%; (2) Adviser Charge Impact—the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than current plans, requiring outperformance of £4,672 in year one; (3) Lifestyling Drift—funds currently in lifestyling will move outside the agreed risk range (5) as they reduce risk toward retirement. Additionally, limitations are stated: withdrawal restrictions (unable to access flexibly), fund range constraint (Scottish Widows lacks a single multi-asset fund at risk 5, leaving 20-30% in cash), and access delay (10-15 working days). Product-specific risks include market timing risk during switching and delay in accessing funds.

    File:     The evidence pack contains multiple pension switch comparison tables and product literature. Key figures from P1, P2, P3, P10 show critical yield requirements for the full consolidation scenario: at mid-growth (2.94-2.95%), critical yield stated as 6.3% in P1 table, with effect of switching listed as £3,000 or 2.1% decrease. P10 shows a different scenario (possibly retention-based) with critical yield 4.8% at mid-growth, matching the report's 4.8% statement. However, P1's table shows critical yield of 6.3% for the main recommendation, not 4.8%. P8 discloses loss of life cover and waiver benefits on new Zurich pension plans, requiring medical questionnaire if separate cover is desired. P15 (Standard Life) notes low charges may not be available elsewhere upon transfer, constituting a material benefit being given up. P11 and P20 contain generic income drawdown risks (means-tested benefits impact, depletion risk, tax consequences). Reduction in yield figures in P2 and P10 show 0.3% existing vs 0.6% new solution (P3, P10), indicating charge increase not explicitly quantified in the report's main narrative.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states critical yield of 4.8% at mid-growth; P1 table for main consolidation scenario shows critical yield 6.3% at mid-growth (2.94%). P10 shows 4.8% critical yield but this appears to be a retention scenario, not the primary recommendation.

      - Report asserts Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive; derived figures show this percentage implies fund value £110,188.68, which does not match any single arrangement value or total, making the calculation unreliable.

      - Report discloses withdrawal restrictions generically; evidence in P10 states 'Any fees applied at the point of switching may reduce the capital value of the pension which will reduce any death benefit payable,' a specific harm not mentioned in report.

      - Report mentions 10-15 working day access delay; P11 states 'If the underlying investments include property, it may take some time before we can sell your investment' and 'It may not be easy to sell investments such as commercial property,' implying potentially longer delays for certain assets not quantified in report.

      - Report does not mention loss of life cover and waiver benefits as a disadvantage. P8 explicitly states 'Both life cover and waiver benefits are no longer available on new pension plans' and notes medical questionnaire required for separate cover, with warning 'This will be particularly important if your health has deteriorated since taking out these benefits.' This is material.

      - Report does not mention loss of low fund charges available on Standard Life plan. P15 states 'These low charges may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer' and notes Differential Fund Discount, a material benefit being surrendered.

      - Report reduction in yield shown as 0.6% for new solution in P3 and P10, compared to 0.3% for existing, indicating charge increase of 0.3 percentage points (doubling), but report quantifies impact only in terms of 4.24% overall cost increase, not transparently showing charge deterioration.

      - Report does not reference tax consequences of income drawdown explicitly. P11 warns 'If your tax position isn't exactly as described, the income you get through our Self-Select phased income drawdown option may not match exactly what is shown on your illustration' and mentions means-tested benefits impact.

    The report states material disadvantages and risks, but discrepancies exist between what the report asserts and what the evidence shows. Most significantly: (1) The critical yield figure of 4.8% stated in the report appears to come from a retention scenario (P10) rather than the primary consolidation recommendation, which P1 shows as 6.3% at mid-growth. This is a material difference—6.3% vs 4.8%—affecting client understanding of the required return. (2) The adviser charge impact calculation (4.24% more expensive) produces an implausible fund value in derived figures, suggesting the figure may be incorrectly calculated or presented. (3) The report omits material losses not captured in its three stated disadvantages: loss of life cover and waiver benefits (P8), loss of preferential fund charges on Standard Life (P15), and explicit tax consequences of income drawdown (P11). The guard 'A research finding that undercuts the recommendation is material information the report withheld' applies to the Standard Life charge discount. The report discloses risks in generic and product-specific terms but fails to address specific benefits foregone and tax consequences adequately. The withdrawal restriction disclosure is generic; the specific harm of death benefit reduction (P10) adds clarity the report lacks. Access delay is stated as 10-15 days but literature indicates property holdings may delay further. The disclosure is present but incomplete against evidence.

CHK-009 — Replacement or Switch Justification

Outcome: Potential Concern

 

  Potential Concern across 10 requirement(s). 9 raised a concern (G9.1, G9.2, G9.3, G9.5, G9.6, G9.7, G9.8, G9.9, G9.10). Report claims Aviva charges 'Better' at 0.44% vs 0.50% existing, but Aviva plan alone shows -£10 decrease, not improvement. Report claims Zurich charges 'Better' at 0.44% vs 0.93%, but Zurich pension only shows -£2,000 or 1.6% decrease, contradicting benefit claim. Overall Defaqto rankings place New Solution 5th at £125,000 (0.6%), behind Zurich 1st £128,000 (0.2%), Existing Solution 2nd £127,000 (0.3%), contradicting 'Better' charge assessment. 28 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

 

  [G9.1] Comparison of existing versus recommended product and investments

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report presents multiple comparisons across five existing arrangements (People's Pension, Scottish Widows, Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life) being switched to Aviva Platform. For Aviva existing (0.50% fund, £4.50 admin): charges assessed as 'Better' at 0.44% (0.21% platform + 0.22% investment management), with -0.06% -£2.25 quoted. For Zurich existing (0.18% fund + £2.12/month admin = 0.93% total): charges assessed as 'Better' at 0.44%, with -0.49% -£506.85 quoted. Fund range: 80+ vs 7, 80+ vs 29, 80+ vs 60+ all marked 'Better'. Risk alignment: existing Aviva lifestyling fund (Risk 2, moving outside agreed range) vs Risk 5 Balanced marked 'Better'; Zurich Risk 7 vs Risk 5 marked 'Better'. Adviser servicing not available on existing, available on recommended (marked 'Better'). Retirement options mostly equivalent, with Aviva recommended adding Flexi Access Drawdown. Overall assessed recommendation supports consolidation and switch.

    File:     Evidence from Pension switch report (P3-P5, P9, P17, P20) shows: (1) Aviva plan alone comparison shows -£10 or 0.3% decrease at mid growth (projected £4,280 existing vs £4,260 new), not an improvement. (2) Zurich pension only comparison (P4, P17) shows -£2,000 or 1.6-2.0% decrease at mid growth (£127,000 existing vs £125,000 new), contradicting claimed benefit. (3) Standard Life comparison (P10) shows -£10 or 0.2-0.6% decrease at mid growth. (4) Overall combined comparison (P17) shows existing solution £127,000 vs new solution £125,000 at mid growth = -£2,000 or 1.6% decrease. (5) Defaqto switch report ranks solutions by maturity value at medium growth: Zurich pension ranked 1st (£128,000), Existing Solution 2nd (£127,000), New Solution 5th (£125,000), all outperforming the recommended switch. (6) Charge data in P3-P5 confirms Zurich 0.29%, Aviva retained 0.59%, new solution 0.6% charges by provider. (7) Retirement options in P1 confirm FAD and UFPLS available on all existing arrangements; Annuity only via OMO on new solution. (8) Fund range in P1 confirms Zurich '60+', not matching report's stated comparison points. (9) Suitability report (P19) shows existing pensions at mid growth: £127,000 vs new solution £115,000, a -£12,000 or 9.4% decrease — more adverse than pension switch report's -£2,000 figure.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report claims Aviva charges 'Better' at 0.44% vs 0.50% existing, but Aviva plan alone shows -£10 decrease, not improvement

      - Report claims Zurich charges 'Better' at 0.44% vs 0.93%, but Zurich pension only shows -£2,000 or 1.6% decrease, contradicting benefit claim

      - Overall Defaqto rankings place New Solution 5th at £125,000 (0.6%), behind Zurich 1st £128,000 (0.2%), Existing Solution 2nd £127,000 (0.3%), contradicting 'Better' charge assessment

      - Suitability report projects new solution at £115,000 (mid growth) vs pension switch report's £125,000, a £10,000 discrepancy in projected value

      - Report states retirement options 'Equivalent' but Aviva recommended adds Flexi Access Drawdown not available on all existing (Standard Life offers FAD only, not full/partial UFPLS per P1)

      - Fund range comparison states Zurich offers 60+ funds, but report compares it in one analysis as 80+ in another dimension

      - Adviser servicing marked 'Better' for Aviva Platform, but this is a feature-availability claim not corroborated by fund performance or cost-benefit

      - Report's charge arithmetic implies fund values of £110,185 but existing arrangements total £116,998.47; charge comparisons appear calculated on wrong asset base

    The report asserts multiple comparisons claiming the recommended switch is 'Better' on charges, fund range, risk alignment and adviser servicing. However, critical evidence contradicts the core claim: (1) Charge benefit claim: Report states Aviva and Zurich switches reduce charges and are 'Better', but the Pension Switch Report (P3, P4, P17) projects the new solution will result in fund decreases of £2,000-£10 at mid growth rates. The Defaqto rankings (P9) place the recommended 'New Solution' 5th by maturity value (0.6% charges), behind the existing solutions. The suitability report's own cashflow analysis (P19) shows even more adverse impact: £12,000 or 9.4% decrease. This directly contradicts the 'Better' assessment on charges. (2) Fund range: Report compares existing fund ranges (7, 29, 60+ funds) against 80+, marked 'Better', but this is a feature comparison not corroborated by evidence that wider choice delivers better outcomes for this client's risk profile. (3) Risk alignment: The report correctly identifies that Aviva's lifestyling fund (Risk 2) moves outside agreed risk range (5), supporting the 'Better' assessment for Risk 5 Balanced alignment. However, for Zurich, the report claims Risk 7 misalignment is 'Better' switched to Risk 5, but Zurich pension comparison shows -£2,000 decrease, suggesting the switch is not actually beneficial despite risk alignment. (4) Adviser servicing: Marked 'Better' as a feature, but no analysis addresses whether direct charge deduction justifies the cost increase shown in projections. (5) Retirement options: Report marks 'Equivalent', but evidence (P1) shows Standard Life offers only FAD (not full/partial UFPLS), creating an inconsistency in comparability. (6) Valuation discrepancy: The report's own figures suggest fund values totalling £116,998.47, but charge calculations appear to use £110,185, suggesting the comparison may be computed on incomplete or incorrect asset base. (7) Performance data (P6, P14): Zurich funds have outperformed new Aviva funds over 5 years (46.14% vs 42.40%), yet report still recommends switch. This is acknowledged but not sufficiently analysed against the financial detriment shown in projections. The fundamental issue is that the report asserts 'Better' outcomes on charges and other dimensions, but the evidence (pension switch report projections and Defaqto rankings) consistently shows the new solution produces lower maturity values and therefore worse financial outcomes. A guard (provider fund-range and option data changes over time) does not explain this contradiction, as the underlying financial projections, not feature availability, are the issue. The report's claim to support the switch is contradicted by its own comparative projections.

  [G9.2] Benefits and costs of switching, including where benefits outweigh higher costs

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts multiple benefits of switching: (1) Small pots can be withdrawn tax-efficiently with 25% tax-free and 75% taxed at marginal rate, netting £170 from a £200 fund. (2) Lower costs - the receiving scheme has a lower overall cost structure, with annual charge difference of -0.06% (saving £2.25 annually). (3) Lifestyling funds currently move portfolio outside agreed risk range; new scheme offers greater investment choice suited to risk profile. (4) Adviser charging facilitation, ongoing monitoring, and multi-asset fund selection (HSBC 50%, CT Universal MAP 50%) providing risk level 5 profile unavailable in existing provider. (5) Enhanced retirement flexibility. (6) No exit penalties stated (£0). (7) Critical yield achievable: mid-growth 2.94% requires 4.8% annual growth to match existing plan value; based on past fund performance, this is stated as achievable. (8) Required outperformance: £4,672 in first year.

    File:     The pension switch report (P2-P7, P12-P15) provides detailed comparisons. For the three-plan switch (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life): existing pension reduction in yield 0.3%, new solution 0.6%; at mid-growth 2.95%, effect of switching is £2,000 or 1.6% decrease in maturity value (P6). For Zurich pension alone (P3): existing reduction in yield 0.3%, new solution 0.6%; critical yield 0.3%; effect of switching £2,000 or 2.0% decrease. For Standard Life alone (P2): existing 0.6%, new solution 0% at mid-growth; critical yield 6.0%; effect of switching £30 or 0.6% decrease. For People's Pension alone (small pot, £211.32 value per evidence): no specific comparison provided. Zurich policy info (P9) confirms no fund switch charge (£0.00) and no transfer penalties. No evidence corroborates the claimed -0.06% annual charge advantage or £2.25 annual saving. Charge arithmetic from report: Aviva existing 0.50%, new Aviva Platform 0.44% (text claims new plan 0.06% more expensive, contradicting calculation). The switch report shows new solution consistently has higher reduction in yield (0.6% vs 0.3%) across all comparisons, meaning new solution charges more, not less. No evidence provides tax calculation for small pot (25% tax-free, 75% income-taxed yielding £170 net from £200).

    Discrepancies:

      - Report claims annual charge difference of -0.06% (lower cost) but charge arithmetic shows Aviva existing 0.50% vs new 0.44%, which is 0.06% lower for new (not higher). However, text on page 6 states 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution,' contradicting the arithmetic.

      - Report states £2.25 annual saving from charge difference but this figure does not match any value in the evidence pack.

      - Report claims lower overall cost structure and greater investment choice as switching benefits, but pension switch report (P6) shows 'Effect of switching' as £2,000 or 1.6% decrease in maturity value at mid-growth (2.95%), meaning the new solution underperforms the existing.

      - Report asserts critical yield of 4.8% is achievable based on past fund performance, but evidence (P12) for People's Pension shows critical yield 4.8% at mid-growth 2.95% with effect of switching -£12,000 or 9.4% decrease in projected value at age 75.

      - Report states lifestyling moves portfolio outside agreed risk range; new scheme offers greater investment choice. However, no evidence confirms that lifestyling in existing arrangements actually moves the portfolio outside the client's agreed risk rating of 5 (Balanced).

      - Report claims small pot withdrawal yields £170 net (25% tax-free, 75% taxed), but no evidence in the pack corroborates this tax treatment or calculation.

      - Report asserts multi-asset fund selection (HSBC 50%, CT Universal MAP 50%) provides risk level 5 profile unavailable in Zurich. Evidence (P7) confirms these two funds are recommended at 50/50 allocation but provides no comparison showing Zurich cannot deliver risk level 5.

      - Report states no exit penalties (£0). Evidence confirms Zurich (P9) has no fund switch charge and no transfer penalties stated, but does not explicitly confirm zero exit penalties for all five existing arrangements.

    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Evidence of actual charge comparison between all five existing arrangements and recommended Aviva Platform solution - report asserts -0.06% annual charge advantage but figures in evidence show opposite or are absent, Corroboration of small pot tax treatment (25% tax-free, 75% income-taxed) and net receipt of £170 from £200 withdrawal, Evidence that existing arrangements' lifestyling mechanism moves portfolio outside client's agreed risk rating 5, Confirmation that existing providers (People's Pension, Scottish Widows, Standard Life) cannot or do not offer risk level 5 multi-asset fund strategies, Exit penalties or transfer charges for Scottish Widows (£211.32) and People's Pension (£6,601.55) arrangements, Explicit comparison showing Zurich cannot deliver risk level 5 profile without blended funds, Evidence supporting the critical yield of 4.8% is achievable based on past fund performance of recommended funds

    The requirement asks whether replacement/switch advice is consistent with and supported by evidence on benefits and costs of switching. Critical findings: (1) Charge advantage contradiction: The report claims -0.06% annual charge difference (lower costs for new solution) on page 6, but the derived figures from the report's own values show Aviva existing at 0.50% and recommended Aviva Platform at 0.44%, representing a 0.06% advantage to the new solution. However, the pension switch report evidence (P6, P12) consistently shows the new solution has reduction in yield of 0.6% versus existing 0.3%, meaning higher total charges in the new solution. The report's statement that 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive' directly contradicts the arithmetic that should show it 0.06% cheaper. (2) Maturity value effect: The evidence (P6) for the three-plan switch shows 'Effect of switching' as £2,000 or 1.6% decrease, and for People's Pension alone (P12) shows £12,000 or 9.4% decrease. This means switching results in materially worse outcomes at mid-growth rates, contradicting the benefit claim of lower costs. (3) Small pot treatment: The report asserts tax-efficient withdrawal of £200 netting £170, with 25% tax-free and 75% taxed at marginal rate. This specific calculation is not evidenced in any document provided. (4) Critical yield: The report states required outperformance of £4,672 in year one and mid-growth requiring 4.8% annual return to match existing. Evidence in P12 shows this critical yield is indeed 4.8% for People's Pension, consistent with the report. However, whether this is 'achievable' based on past performance is not evidenced; no fund performance data is provided comparing recommended funds against this hurdle. (5) Lifestyling and risk drift: The report claims existing funds in lifestyling move portfolio outside agreed risk range. No evidence confirms whether existing lifestyling arrangements actually breach the client's agreed risk rating 5. (6) Missing charge comparisons: The £2.25 annual saving figure is not corroborated by any evidence. The charge analysis provided in the evidence (P15) does not isolate individual plan comparisons with stated annual savings. (7) Alternative arrangements: Evidence shows comparisons for Aviva-only switch (P3), Zurich-only switch (P3), Standard Life-only switch (P2), and three-plan switch (P6). These alternatives are considered in the evidence, addressing that guard. However, the report's claim that benefits outweigh higher costs is contradicted by the 'Effect of switching' showing consistent material decreases in projected maturity values. The comparison cannot be fully performed because the charge difference figures asserted by the report (-0.06%, £2.25) do not match or are contradicted by the evidence pack.

  [G9.3] Details of lost features and guarantees

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report identifies one lost feature: 'Employer contribution will be lost' on page 6, with the quote 'If you were to transfer away, you would miss out on the valuable benefit of your employer's contributions.' The report confirms no safeguarded benefits exist on any of the five existing arrangements (all stated as 'This pension arrangement does not contain any safeguarded benefits' on page 5). The report recommends switching four plans (Scottish Widows, Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life) but retaining People's Pension. Only the first arrangement (People's Pension) is flagged with employerContributionsLost = true; the other four show false.

    File:     Policy provider documentation confirms across all five arrangements: (1) People's Pension (P8): No mention of employer contributions or their status. (2) Scottish Widows (P2, P10, P21, P24): 'No' life cover, 'No' waiver of premium, 'No' guaranteed benefits of any kind; contribution history shows 'N/A' with note member has no current regular contributions. (3) Aviva (P17): 'None' for life cover, 'None - it's no longer possible to add this' for waiver of payment; no guarantees stated. (4) Zurich (P1, P15): Guarantees and bonuses noted as 'do not apply to this plan'; life cover and waiver benefits mentioned as 'no longer available on new pension plans'; confirmed 'Guarantees, bonuses, guaranteed minimum pension, safeguarding, life styling, block/bulk transfers, market value reduction, protected or enhanced tax free cash do not apply to this plan.' (5) Standard Life (P4, P23): Confirmed 'No Guaranteed Annuity Rates (GAR's)', 'No Guaranteed Minimum Pension (GMP)', 'No Guaranteed Fund at retirement'; stated 'As confirmed above there are no Safeguarded benefits attached to this member's plan.' Test Case 1 Policy Research (P3, P11, P19) tabulates all five plans and confirms across every row: GAR = No, GMP = No, Life Cover = No, Guaranteed growth = No.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report asserts employer contribution will be lost for People's Pension (employerContributionsLost = true) but provides no evidence from the policy documentation that People's Pension includes an employer contribution or describes what is being lost. P8 (People's Pension Policy Info) contains no mention of employer contributions.

      - The report states employer contributions will be lost for the four plans being switched (Scottish Widows, Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life) with employerContributionsLost = false, but P21 (Scottish Widows Plan Schedule) explicitly shows 'Employer - N/A' and 'This member has no current regular contributions being made to their pension plan,' suggesting no active employer contributions to lose on the Scottish Widows plan at the time of the recommendation.

      - The report correctly identifies no safeguarded benefits on any plan, but provides only two generic statements on page 5 repeated five times rather than plan-specific evidence from provider documentation.

    The report's central claim—that employer contribution will be lost—is addressed to People's Pension (the only plan being retained, not switched), not to the plans actually being switched away. This creates a logical inconsistency: if employer contributions are valuable and will be lost, the recommendation should address this for the arrangements being transferred. The policy documentation for People's Pension (P8) makes no mention of employer contributions, so the basis for claiming one exists is not evident in the file. The Scottish Widows documentation (P21) explicitly shows 'Employer - N/A' and confirms no current contributions, suggesting no active employer contribution is being lost on that plan. The four plans recommended for switch show employerContributionsLost = false, yet the report does not explain why no employer contributions exist on them or were not being paid into them. For safeguarded benefits, the report's assertion is corroborated: all five policy documents and the test case tabulation (P3, P11, P19) confirm the absence of guaranteed annuity rates, guaranteed minimum pensions, life cover, waiver of premium, and other protected features. The comparison can be performed: the assertion of no safeguards aligns with the evidence. However, the employer contribution claim lacks supporting evidence from the plan documents. The guard requiring explicit corroboration of 'no safeguarded benefits' statements is satisfied by provider confirmation in each policy document; the guard does not excuse the unsupported employer contribution assertion.

  [G9.5] Switching costs exceed the benefits

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts zero exit penalties on all ceding plans ('No charges for transfer and/or switching'), and claims net annual charge reductions ranging from -0.06% to -0.49% on switched arrangements. For the largest plan (Zurich, £103,439.24), the report states 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution', implying a saving. The report projects maturity value increases from switching (e.g., Zurich mid-growth: £127,000 vs £115,000 retained, stated as £12,000 or 9.4% decrease effect), and cites benefits including lower costs, lifestyling correction, greater investment choice, adviser charging facilitation, and ongoing monitoring. For small pots (Scottish Widows £211.32), the report recommends encashment under small pots rules with estimated net receipt of £170 and states 'This would not detract from your financial retirement plan.' The report provides a critical yield for Zurich of 4.8% at mid-growth rate.

    File:     The pension switch report evidence [P7] shows for Zurich (the largest ceding plan at £110,185.60 existing value) at mid-growth rate (2.95%): existing pension projected value £127,000 vs new solution £115,000, creating a £12,000 or 9.4% decrease in projected maturity value. Reduction in yield is 0.3% (existing) vs 2.2% (new solution). Critical yield required from new solution is 4.8%. At low growth (0.00%): existing £108,000 vs new £97,600 (£10,400 or 9.6% decrease). At high growth (5.88%): existing £150,000 vs new £135,000 (£15,000 or 10.0% decrease). [P1] shows overall position: new solution 0.6% charge with £117,000 maturity, retained arrangements at 0.29-0.59%. [P2] shows immediate switch impact: existing pension £110,185.60 vs new solution day one £106,880.60, a £3,305 shortfall in death benefit. The evidence does not show a cost saving of 0.06% or 0.49% for any plan; instead it shows higher reduction in yield (RIY) in the new solution across all growth scenarios for the major plans switched.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report claims annual charge difference of -0.06% (saving) for largest plan (Zurich); evidence shows new solution RIY of 2.2% vs existing 0.3%, a net increase of 1.9% in the cost impact, not a reduction.

      - Report asserts charge saving of -0.49% on another plan; evidence does not corroborate this figure in any comparative table.

      - Report projects £12,000 improvement ('effect of switching') for Zurich at mid-growth; evidence shows £12,000 decrease in projected maturity value, not an improvement.

      - Report states small pots withdrawal would net £170 after tax; evidence [P5] does not confirm the £170 figure or the 25%/75% tax split claimed.

      - Report claims 'Lower costs - receiving scheme has lower overall cost structure'; evidence shows new solution carries higher RIY across all growth scenarios for the major transferred plans.

      - Report asserts benefits justify the switch (lifestyling correction, investment choice, adviser monitoring); evidence does not independently corroborate these claimed non-financial benefits as material advantages justifying a net cost increase and maturity value reduction.

      - Immediate capital impact: report does not disclose the £3,305 shortfall in death benefit immediately following the Zurich switch, as evidenced in [P2].

    The core issue is that the report's assertion that switching costs are justified by benefits cannot be reconciled with the evidence. The report claims cost savings (-0.06%, -0.49%) but the switch analysis tables in [P7] and [P1] show the new solution has materially higher reduction in yield (RIY) than the existing arrangements across all growth scenarios. For Zurich (the largest plan), RIY increases from 0.3% to 2.2%, a significant increase in the cost drag, not a saving. The report's statement that 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' contradicts the evidence that the new solution's RIY is 1.9 percentage points higher. The projected maturity values in [P7] show the new solution underperforms at all growth rates: low growth -9.6%, mid-growth -9.4%, high growth -10.0%. These are material cost-to-benefit mismatches. The report cites non-financial benefits (lifestyling, investment choice, adviser monitoring) but provides no independent evidence from categories E (costs/charges) or G (switching/transfer analysis) to support that these benefits outweigh the projected £10,400–£15,000 maturity value reductions. [P2] shows an immediate £3,305 death benefit loss following the switch, which the report does not disclose. The small pots encashment claim of £170 net receipt is not corroborated by the evidence, which discusses Standard Life small pots but does not confirm the tax treatment or receipt amount stated. The extraction note flagged ambiguity on whether charge comparisons were correctly computed. The derived figures confirm that the report's charge differences (-0.06%, -0.49%) are not supported by the fund values they reference: the -0.06% and £268.94 figures compute to fund values in the range of £103,438, which is the Zurich arrangement, not the arrangement label assigned to those rows. This indicates the charge comparison rows are computed on the wrong plan, rendering any switching cost analysis based on them unreliable. The evidence demonstrates that costs do exceed benefits: the projected maturity value reductions of 9–10% are not justified by the non-evidenced claims of lower costs or service improvements.

  [G9.6] Lost guarantees or benefits disadvantage the client

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts that all five existing arrangements have 'No charges for transfer and/or switching' (stated on page 5 for all arrangements). The extraction notes that /existingArrangements[]/featuresOrGuaranteesLost is 'NotPresentInDocument', meaning the report contains no analysis of safeguarded benefits, guarantees or other valuable features that may be lost on switching. The extraction flags this as absent from the canonical model.

    File:     Evidence from the case file confirms: (P13) Scottish Widows Master Trust plan has 'no guarantees, discounts or bonuses attached to this member's plan' and 'Money4Life Plans (Plans starting with ZU) do not support ongoing advisor charging, the transferring of Servicing Rights or Commission' but do not otherwise provide information on safeguarded benefits that would be lost.   [G9.7] Other alternatives provide better outcomes for the client

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report asserts that alternatives were considered for each plan. For People's Pension, switching funds within the plan was rejected because there are no multi-asset funds available and the funds do not meet the client's risk profile (rating 5). For Zurich, an internal fund switch was rejected because Zurich does not offer risk level 5 funds, blended funds have not performed as well as Aviva options (albeit marginally), and the main priority is consolidation, better control, and drawdown options. For Scottish Widows, encashment was recommended under small pots rules. For Standard Life, switching to Aviva was recommended due to lifestyling funds eventually moving outside agreed risk range and lower costs. The report recommends switching four plans (Scottish Widows, Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life) into a new Aviva Platform arrangement while retaining People's Pension. The overall conclusion is that this recommendation provides better outcomes through lower costs, greater investment flexibility, and consolidation benefits.

    File:     The pension switch report (P1, P3-P8, P11-P16) provides detailed comparison analysis. For the overall position with switching, projected maturity value at age 75 is £138,000 (new solution) versus £141,000 (existing pensions), representing a 0.6% reduction in yield and £3,000 or 2.1% decrease in effect of switching (P1). For Zurich alone, the new solution shows £100,000 versus existing £102,000 at low growth, with critical yield of 0.3% (P3). For Aviva alone, projections show £3,620 (new) versus £3,630 (existing) at low growth, a £10 or 0.3% decrease (P5). For Standard Life alone at mid growth, projections show £2,900 (new) versus £2,910 (existing), a £10 or 1.9% decrease (P8). Policy information documents (P9, P20-P24) confirm Zurich has no guarantees, bonuses, guaranteed minimum pension or safeguarding; Scottish Widows and Standard Life support transfers via Origo; and Aviva supports transfers but in-specie transfers not possible (P23). The suitability report (P10, P12, P17, P19) states recommendations are based on consolidation objectives, investment choice, flexibility, and lower costs, with advice charges of £3,305 initial and 1.00% ongoing (P14).

    Discrepancies:

      - The report claims the new solution provides 'lower costs' (P10, P17) but the Reduction in Yield comparison table (P1) shows identical RIY of 0.6% for both existing and new solution, contradicting the claim of cost advantage

      - The report states Zurich blended funds 'have not performed as well as the options available through Aviva, albeit marginally' but projected maturity values at mid growth show Zurich at £127,000 versus Aviva Platform at £117,000, meaning the existing Zurich arrangement outperforms by £10,000 or 8.5%, not underperforms

      - The overall effect of switching is stated as positive in recommendation language but P1 shows projected value decreases from £141,000 to £138,000 (£3,000 decrease or 2.1%)

      - The report recommends switching Aviva and Standard Life to Aviva Platform but individual comparisons show negligible or negative impacts: Aviva alone shows £10 decrease (P5), Standard Life alone shows £10 decrease (P8)

      - The report claims 'lower overall cost structure' justifies switching Standard Life but states 'plan itself is 0.08% more expensive' (P17), which contradicts the cost benefit claim

      - For Zurich specifically, the report rejects internal fund switch citing consolidation as main priority, but the comparison shows Zurich outperforms the recommended solution significantly, making the rejection rationale about performance unsubstantiated

    The trigger is satisfied: the report contains multiple replacement/switch recommendations affecting five pension schemes. The requirement [G9.7] tests whether alternatives provide better outcomes. Analysis of each element: (1) Overall comparison: P1 provides explicit projected maturity values showing existing pensions at £141,000 versus new solution at £138,000, a £3,000 decrease. The report's language emphasizes benefits ('lower costs', 'greater investment flexibility') but the financial comparison does not support these claims. The Reduction in Yield is identical at 0.6%, meaning the cost advantage claimed is not evidenced. (2) Plan-specific alternatives: For Zurich, the report rejects internal fund switch citing performance underperformance 'albeit marginally', but P3 shows Zurich existing at £127,000 (mid growth) versus new solution at £115,000, meaning Zurich actually outperforms by £12,000 or 9.4%. This is a material contradiction: the report's performance rationale contradicts the evidence. For Aviva, switching produces £10 decrease (P5). For Standard Life, switching produces £10 decrease (P8). These individual comparisons show the consolidation strategy is not delivering financial benefit. (3) Consolidation objective: The report states consolidation, control, and drawdown options are priorities. P18-P19 show multiple alternatives have FAD (Flexi Access Drawdown) available, and P19 confirms Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life all support transferable options, so the drawdown benefit is not exclusive to the recommended solution. (4) Cost analysis: The report claims lower costs but P14 shows initial adviser charge of £3,305 and ongoing 1.00%, and the RIY comparison shows no cost advantage. The claim that Standard Life is more expensive is contradicted by the report's own statement that it is '0.08% more expensive' than the solution. (5) Alternatives considered: The report considers retention, in-situ switches, and consolidation into employer scheme, satisfying the procedural requirement for alternatives consideration. However, the rationale for rejecting lower-cost or better-performing alternatives is not supported by the quantitative evidence. The report recommends a switch that produces lower maturity values (£3,000 decrease overall, £12,000 decrease for Zurich alone) and relies on non-financial benefits (flexibility, consolidation) that are not differentiated from alternatives. The guard confirms consolidation is a valid objective if client-requested, but does not excuse overlooking that the chosen route underperforms financially.

  [G9.8] Evidence supporting claims that higher costs are justified by additional benefits

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report claims multiple benefits of switching that justify switching costs: lower costs (0.06-0.49% saving depending on arrangement), greater investment choice and multi-asset fund selection, lifestyling fund drift correction, enhanced retirement flexibility, adviser charging facilitation, ongoing monitoring, and consolidation benefits. For the Zurich arrangement specifically, the report states 'Despite higher annual charges (£268.94 more per year), the benefits of proper risk alignment, consolidation, and active management justify the switch' and 'the plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' for one arrangement while claiming cost savings elsewhere.

    File:     The pension switch report (P1-P13, P14) shows: (1) For Aviva plan: existing charge 0.50% vs new solution 0.44% = -0.06% saving (P14); (2) For Zurich plan: existing 0.93% vs new 0.44% = -0.49% saving (P14); (3) For Standard Life: existing 0.26% vs new 0.44% = +0.18% cost increase (P14, P15); (4) Critical yield analysis shows switching requires funds to outperform by 4.8% per year for mid-growth scenario (P18); (5) Immediate post-switch fund value drops from £110,185.60 to £106,880.60 creating £3,305 shortfall in death benefit (P2, P4); (6) Effect of switching shows value decreases under all scenarios: Zurich only comparison shows -£2,000 (2.0% decrease) at low growth, -£12,000 (9.4% decrease) at mid-growth (P4); Alternative comparisons show new solution ranks 5th in maturity value (position) vs existing solution rank 2 (P8).

    Discrepancies:

      - Report claims 'lower costs' as benefit but Standard Life arrangement shows +0.18% cost increase (higher cost), not lower (P15, P14)

      - Report states for Aviva 'plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than recommended solution' but charge table shows 0.50% existing vs 0.44% new = 0.06% saving, not cost (P15, P14)

      - Report asserts Zurich switch justified despite 'higher annual charges (£268.94 more per year)' but charge analysis shows -0.49% saving = approximately -£510 annually on £103,438 fund value, not +£268.94 (P8, P14, P19)

      - Report claims benefits justify switching but critical yield shows new solution must outperform existing by 4.8% annually to match maturity value, not exceed it (P18)

      - Report claims consolidation and flexibility benefits but switching projections show value reductions: Aviva Aviva-only £30 decrease (0.6%), Zurich-only £2,000-£15,000 decrease (2-10%) depending on growth rate (P3, P4, P7, P11)

      - Report asserts 'greater investment choice' as benefit but neither the report nor evidence specifies what fund options client lacked on existing plans or how many funds are available on each (P19, P15)

      - Report claims 'lifestyling fund drift' correction but evidence shows client's existing Zurich fund allocation and states funds are blended (Managed AP fund plus Interest Deposits), with no confirmation lifestyling automatic de-risking is actually occurring (P19, P12)

      - Report claims 'adviser charging facilitation' as new benefit but pre-switch arrangement already contemplated adviser charges (1% annual fee paid via provider noted in recommendations section) (P17)

    The guard 'where the recommended plan is cheaper, this requirement does not bite' applies to some arrangements (Aviva -0.06%, Zurich -0.49%) but NOT to Standard Life (+0.18% higher cost). For Standard Life, the report must justify why higher costs are acceptable through benefits. The report's claim of 'lower costs' as a universal benefit contradicts the charge data for Standard Life. The Zurich arrangement presents a specific contradiction: the report states higher charges (£268.94 annually) justify switching, but the charge comparison table (P14) shows -0.49% or approximately -£507 annual saving, not an increase. The critical yield requirement (4.8% outperformance needed) is not a benefit; it is a cost that must be overcome. The 'Greater Investment Choice' benefit lacks specificity—no fund count or named missing option on existing plans is provided. The 'Lifestyling' benefit claim (portfolio drifting outside risk range) is asserted but the evidence shows existing Zurich arrangement uses blended funds and does not confirm automatic lifestyling is in place. The 'Adviser Charging Facilitation' is claimed as new but the report itself shows adviser charging (1% per annum) was already contemplated in the existing setup. The consolidation benefit is legitimate but the switching analysis shows value reductions across all growth scenarios, contradicting any assertion that consolidation alone justifies the switch. The report's claim that benefits justify switching contradicts its own charge analysis showing either savings (Aviva, Zurich) where the guard applies, or cost increases (Standard Life) without adequate benefit substantiation.

  [G9.9] Evidence elsewhere in the file that supports or contradicts the replacement rationale

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The report asserts five replacement actions: (1) Retain People's Pension workplace scheme citing ongoing employer contributions that would be forfeited on transfer, with recommendation to switch investments to Global Investments fund to align with risk rating 5. (2) Encash Scottish Widows plan because it lacks a single multi-asset fund aligned with risk rating 5; combining two funds would leave 20-30% out of market. Plan does not allow adviser management and limits withdrawal flexibility. (3) Switch Aviva plan to better align with risk rating 5, move away from lifestyling fund, lower costs, and enable ongoing adviser servicing. (4) Switch Zurich plan for greater investment choice, multi-asset fund selection properly aligned with risk rating 5, enhanced retirement flexibility, lower annual charges (0.49% saving), and adviser servicing. (5) Switch Standard Life plan away from lifestyling fund that will drift outside agreed risk range, provide fixed risk level 5 portfolio, greater investment choice, enhanced retirement flexibility, and ongoing adviser oversight. Report acknowledges for Standard Life that despite higher annual charges (£268.94 more per year), benefits justify the switch.

    File:     Provider documentation confirms: (P3, P4) Zurich plan has no guarantees, bonuses, or safeguarded benefits. (P8) Aviva plan is invested in 'Aviva Pensions My Future Focus Consolidation (Pre-2024) S2' with auto-switching 'Lifestage Investment Approach' and no guaranteed rates or Guaranteed Minimum Pension. (P10, P14) Scottish Widows Money4Life Plans (starting ZU) do not support ongoing adviser charging and do not include life cover. (P10, P14) Scottish Widows member can invest into as many available funds as they wish, with no guarantees, discounts or bonuses. (P16) File notes state: Scottish Widows alternative was 'Discounted as no servicing and reduced flexibility with withdrawing'; Zurich alternative notes 'Zurich do not have risk level 5 funds, a blend of a 7 and a 2 has been used' with no multi-asset funds in risk range, 'blend of funds have not performed as well as what we are offering on Aviva'; Standard Life alternative 'Discounted as all funds within the same range of 20-60% equities are more expensive than what we can offer at Aviva'. (P5, P20) Pension switching report shows three plans being switched (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life) to Aviva Platform. (P6) Switching report ranked pension options by maturity value: Zurich 0.2% (£128,000), Existing Solution 0.3% (£127,000), Aviva 0.5% (£126,000), Standard Life 0.5% (£126,000), New Solution 0.6% (£125,000). The New Solution ranked 5th with projected value lower than existing solutions including the plans being switched from. (P22) Charges comparison table shows: Aviva existing 0.50%, Zurich existing 0.93%, Standard Life existing 0.26%, with New Solution showing 0.44% total charge. For Zurich: table states -0.06% impact but computation produces fund value of £103,438.46, which is Zurich's actual value, making the impact calculation unreliable per derived figures analysis showing charge arithmetic computed on wrong plan.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states Aviva switch justified by 'lower costs (0.49% saving)' but charges comparison shows Aviva existing 0.50% vs new solution 0.44%, a difference of 0.06%, not 0.49%

      - Report states Standard Life switch justified by 'lower annual charges (0.49% saving)' but derived figures show charge arithmetic on Standard Life row (EA5) computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,438.46) rather than Standard Life's actual value (£3,002), making this comparison unreliable

      - Report states Standard Life switch to lower costs but also acknowledges 'higher annual charges (£268.94 more per year)', creating internal contradiction about cost justification

      - Pension switching report (P5, P20) ranks New Solution 5th by projected maturity value (£125,000) below Existing Solution (£127,000), Zurich (£128,000), Aviva (£126,000), and Standard Life (£126,000), contradicting premise that switch produces better outcome

      - Report states Zurich switch justified by better risk alignment but file notes (P16) indicate Zurich requires blend of risk level 7 and risk level 2 funds (not risk level 5) and the blend 'have not performed as well as what we are offering on Aviva'

      - Report recommends Aviva alternative was 'Discounted' because 'Only mixed asset aviva fund available is risk rated 4 which does not meet the clients ATR' (P16), yet report recommends switching to Aviva Platform - this appears contradictory unless alternative internal Aviva fund differs from recommended Aviva Platform

      - Report states Scottish Widows lacks single multi-asset fund and requires combining two funds leaving 20-30% out of market, but provider literature (P10, P14) states 'The member can invest into as many of the available funds as they wish' without stating fund unavailability or market mismatch

      - Report justifies retention of People's Pension on employer contributions but does not provide evidence of forfeiture or detail the quantum of ongoing contributions to support materiality claim

    Comparison performed: The report's replacement rationale can be compared against provider literature, file notes, and charges schedules. Material contradictions exist. (1) Cost justification fails: Report claims 0.49% charge savings for both Aviva and Standard Life switches. Aviva shows only 0.06% saving (0.50% to 0.44%). Standard Life charge row (EA5) is computed on Zurich's fund value per derived figures, rendering comparison unreliable. The report also contradicts itself by stating Standard Life switch justified by lower costs while simultaneously disclosing £268.94 higher annual charges. (2) Investment performance contradicts replacement premise: The pension switching report (P5, P20) ranks investment outcomes and shows the New Solution projects to £125,000 (5th rank), below the Existing Solution (£127,000, 2nd rank) and three of four plans being switched from (Zurich £128,000, Aviva £126,000, Standard Life £126,000). This directly undermines the stated objective to consolidate and improve retirement readiness. (3) Risk alignment claims unsupported: Report claims Zurich switch addresses lifestyling drift and achieves risk level 5 alignment, but file notes (P16) confirm Zurich has no risk level 5 funds and requires a blend of risk level 7 and risk level 2 funds. The blend performs worse than Aviva alternatives per adviser notes. (4) Aviva alternative discounting contradicts recommendation: File notes discard Aviva as alternative because 'Only mixed asset aviva fund available is risk rated 4' which does not meet ATR, yet the recommendation is to switch to Aviva Platform SIPP. Unless the Platform offers different funds than the rejected alternative fund, the rationale contradicts the recommendation. (5) Scottish Widows encashment rationale not directly evidenced: Report claims plan lacks single multi-asset fund and requires combining funds, but provider confirms member can invest into multiple funds without restriction. Provider literature does not confirm fund unavailability or the 20-30% out-of-market claim. (6) Employer contributions retention: Report justifies People's Pension retention due to ongoing employer contributions but does not quantify contributions or analyse forfeiture materiality. The workplace scheme recommendation is embedded in consolidation objective (OBJ3) to consolidate pensions, creating inconsistency: if consolidation is an objective, retaining one scheme contradicts it. (7) Charges arithmetic failure (derived figures): Standard Life and potentially Zurich charge comparisons are computed on incorrect fund values per the derived figures analysis, rendering the cost-benefit analysis on these plans unreliable. The report's own internal inconsistencies flag that charge analysis lacks a sound foundation. (8) Guard application: File notes document that alternatives were considered and reasons given for discounting them; however, the rationales applied to discard alternatives (e.g., 'not risk rated 4', 'no servicing', 'more expensive') differ from or contradict the rationales used to recommend the solution (e.g., Aviva Platform alternative discounted for risk rating 4, but Aviva Platform recommended despite risk alignment claimed via different mechanism). No evidence contradicts that Scottish Widows encashment was offered advice; the gap is that the rationale (fund unavailability) is not directly corroborated by provider literature, which permits multi-fund investment.

  [G9.10] Material replacement-related information omitted from the recommendation

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts that employer contribution will be lost if the client transfers away from People's Pension, quoting: 'If you were to transfer away, you would miss out on the valuable benefit of your employer's contributions.' The canonical model records employerContributionsLost as true for arrangement 0 (People's Pension) and false for the other four arrangements. The report recommends retaining People's Pension but switching four other plans (Scottish Widows, Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life) to Aviva Platform. Exit penalties are stated as absent across all five arrangements ('No charges for transfer and/or switching'). The report does not explicitly state whether employer contributions are active on the other arrangements or whether they would be lost on those transfers.

    File:     Evidence P1-P8 from People's Pension Policy Info shows a consistent pattern of both Employee Contributions and Employer Contributions being paid into the People's Pension from 2021 through 2023, confirming active employer participation. P11 (Zurich Policy Info) states 'Currently, no payments are being made to this plan' under Current regular payments, with total employer payments shown as £0.00. P14 (SW MT Plan Schedule) and P16 (Standard_Life_Policy_Info) contain no evidence of active employer contributions. P9 shows Standard Life current value of £3,002.02 with 'We've assumed no further payments will be paid.' The evidence confirms People's Pension has active employer contributions but does not clearly establish whether the other four arrangements had active employer contributions at the time of the recommendation.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report addresses loss of employer contributions only for People's Pension (retained) but does not state whether the three switching arrangements (Scottish Widows, Aviva existing, Standard Life) have active employer contributions that would be forfeited by the transfer. The file shows Zurich has no active employer contributions ('Currently, no payments are being made to this plan'), but the report does not disclose this for any arrangement.

      - The extraction notes an internal inconsistency: the recommendation states 'Retain your people's pension plan' but also recommends consolidating pensions (objective OBJ3) and switching four other plans, creating ambiguity about the complete strategy.

      - The report does not provide a detailed plan-by-plan comparison table showing which arrangements had active employer contributions, the amount/frequency of those contributions, and the financial impact of loss on each switching arrangement.

    The rule guard applies: 'An employer contribution that stops on leaving service is not lost by the transfer; check whether the client is still an active member.' The evidence shows the client is actively employed (P3-P8 show contributions through 2023, P14 notes payroll integration possible). The People's Pension evidence (P1-P8) conclusively demonstrates ongoing active employer contributions. The report correctly identifies this risk for People's Pension (retained). However, the report's failure to address whether the other four arrangements have active employer contributions is a material omission. P11 explicitly states Zurich has no current regular employer payments, so no loss there. For Scottish Widows and Standard Life, the evidence is ambiguous but suggestive: P14 contains no evidence of active payments, and P9 (Standard Life) states no further payments will be paid, suggesting the arrangement is not receiving active contributions. The report does not explicitly state this; it merely lists exit penalties as absent. The canonical extraction expected but found absent 'featuresOrGuaranteesLost' for all non-People's arrangements, confirming the report did not analyse safeguarded features or lost contributions on a plan-by-plan basis. The charge analysis on page 6 claims cost savings but does not reconcile this against lost contributions, which would be a material dimension of the switching justification. The derived figures show arithmetic errors in the charge calculations (EA5 and EA5 impact computed on wrong plan), undermining reliability of the cost-benefit comparison that should justify the switches. The report's assertion that switching is appropriate lacks documented analysis of whether the projected fund growth will offset the loss of any employer contributions on the ceding plans.
